Equating this expression to an Exp(1) variate gives the next opening by a quadratic solve; closing
waits are Exp(γ). In the surging model, the opening wait is simply Exp(ν). The fixed-gate flows
also solve exactly, so both models admit event-driven simulation without time-step error in the
switching. Their analysis nevertheless begins with the joint generator (2.44): the countable-state
methods of section 2.3.2 do not by themselves close the continuous-state transport or the mixed
moment E(Gt At ).


2.3           Methods for Markov chains
Having set out the processes, we turn to the techniques for extracting information from them.
The organising idea is the same throughout: condition on one step, and solve the self-consistent
equation that results. In discrete time the step is a generation and the equation is a functional
iteration; in continuous time the step is the first jump and the equation is a differential one; for
generating functions the step is an infinitesimal interval and the equation is a partial differential
equation.

2.3.1          Discrete-time methods
The techniques below reduce every question about the Galton–Watson process of section 2.2.1.2
to a question about the orbit of the quadratic map (2.12). They are set out here because the
same three ideas — conditioning on one step, generating functions, and functional iteration —
run through the continuous-time work that follows; the orbit itself is analysed in the following
chapter.

2.3.1.1            First-step analysis

When the question is a hitting probability or a mean absorption time rather than a full time
course, conditioning on the first step replaces the evolution problem by an algebraic one, because
after that step the chain starts afresh from wherever it landed.



                                                         37
Proposition 2.6 (First-step principle). Let {Xn } be a discrete-time Markov chain with transition
matrix P and absorbing set A, and let hi be the probability of ever reaching A from state i. Then
hi = 1 for i ∈ A, and for every transient i,
                                                   X
                                           hi =            Pij hj .                                   (2.51)
                                                       j

The same conditioning applied to a continuous-time chain with generator Q gives
                                                   X qij
                                           hi =                     hj ,                              (2.52)
                                                   j̸=i
                                                               qi
                                                                            P
and the expected time to absorption satisfies ki = 1/qi +                       j̸=i (qij /qi )kj .

Proof. Absorption occurs eventually if and only if it occurs from the state reached at the first
step; conditioning on that state and using the Markov property gives (2.51). In continuous time
the chain waits an Exp(qi ) time and then, by proposition 2.1, jumps to j with probability qij /qi
independently of the waiting time, which gives (2.52); adding the expected holding time 1/qi to
the same decomposition gives the equation for ki .

   The holding times have vanished from (2.52); mean hitting times do depend on the rates,
which is why 1/qi appears in the companion system. The gambler’s-ruin formula (2.7) is the
two-term instance of (2.51).
   For a branching process the first-step decomposition produces something better than a linear
system. Because the offspring of the founder generate independent subpopulations, conditioning
on the first generation gives a fixed-point equation: the ultimate extinction probability s from a
single individual satisfies
                                              s = ϕ(s),                                      (2.53)
ϕ being the offspring generating function.

2.3.1.2   Generating functions

For a random variable X taking values in {0, 1, 2, . . . } with pk = P(X = k), the probability
generating function is
                                                                  ∞
                                                                    X
                                      ϕ(z) = E z X =                       pk z k ,                   (2.54)
                                                                    k=0

convergent at least for |z| ≤ 1. The series determines the distribution, since pk = ϕ(k) (0)/k!, and
moments are read off at z = 1:

                    ϕ(1) = 1,      ϕ′ (1) = E(X) ,                   ϕ′′ (1) = E(X(X − 1)) .          (2.55)

If X and Y are independent then ϕX+Y = ϕX ϕY , so sums become products; and if N is an
independent count with probability generating function ψ, the random sum X1 + · · · + XN of
independent copies of X has probability generating function ψ ◦ ϕ, so composition of generating
functions corresponds to compounding.
   For a pair of counts the definition extends to
                                                 XX
                      G(x, y) = E xX y Y       =                    P(X = i, Y = j) xi y j ,          (2.56)
                                                   i           j

with marginals recovered by setting the other variable to one. The same construction with n
arguments handles vector-valued states and is the prepared ground for the chapter on multi-type
processes; it is not developed here.

                                                       38
2.3.1.3    Functional iteration

Applying (2.9) repeatedly, the generating function of Zn is the n-fold composite ϕn = ϕ◦n . For
the binary law (2.11) this is the quadratic recursion (2.12) for the survival probability, obtained
by evaluating at z = 0 and taking complements.
   Everything the binary process does is contained in the orbit of that recursion — whether Sn
reaches zero, how quickly it does so, and what constant stands in front of the decay when it
does — and following it is the business of the following chapter. Here the recursion is needed
only as the denominator of the conditional mean of section 2.3.2.6.

2.3.2     Continuous-time methods
The continuous-time methods are presented on the linear birth–death process of section 2.2.2.4
throughout.

2.3.2.1    Forward and backward equations
                                                                         P
Differentiating the Chapman–Kolmogorov identity pij (t + ∆t) =               k pik (t)pkj (∆t) at ∆t = 0
gives the forward equations,
                       dpij (t) X                                  dP

which condition on the last jump before time t. Differentiating in the other slot gives the
backward equations,
                                            dP
                                                = QP,                                        (2.58)
                                            dt
which condition on the first jump after time 0 — first-step analysis in differential form. The two
carry the same information but suit different questions: forward equations track the distribution
as it spreads, backward equations track a functional of the whole future and are the natural
setting for absorption probabilities and for the generating function of a branching process.
   Written out for a fixed initial state, with pj (t) the probability of occupying state j, (2.57)
becomes the master equation
                                dpj (t) X
                                       =      qkj pk (t) − qj pj (t) :                            (2.59)
                                  dt     k̸=j

probability flows into j from every state that can reach it and out of j at total rate qj .
  For the birth–death rates (2.23) the master equation is
                 dpn (t)
                         = λ(n − 1)pn−1 (t) + µ(n + 1)pn+1 (t) − (λ + µ)n pn (t),                 (2.60)
                   dt
an infinite coupled system, and on its own it is not much use. The step that makes it tractable
is to collapse it into a single equation for a generating function. Multiply (2.60) by z n , sum
over n ≥ 0, and shift indices so that every sum is again of the form (2.54); the factors of n
produced by the linear rates become derivatives ∂z , and the three terms become λz 2 ∂z G, µ∂z G
and −(λ + µ)z∂z G. Hence
            ∂G                        ∂G                   ∂G
               = λz 2 − (λ + µ)z + µ      = (λz − µ)(z − 1)    ,             G(z, 0) = z N ,      (2.61)
            ∂t                         ∂z                   ∂z
for a process started from N individuals. This is the template for every generating-function
calculation in this thesis. The quadratic coefficient factorises with roots z = 1 and z = µ/λ, and
the same factorisation reappears in section 2.B.2.

                                                   39
   Solving (2.61) along its characteristics gives the classical result of Kendall [41],
                                                                   !N
                                  µ(1 − z) − (µ − λz)e−(λ−µ)t
                     G(z, t) =                                          ,    λ ̸= µ.            (2.62)

2.3.2.2    Mean population size

Differentiating (2.62) at z = 1 is possible and unpleasant. It is quicker to take the mean of the
master equation directly: multiplying (2.60) by n and summing, the terms telescope to
                             d
                                E(Xt ) = (λ − µ)E(Xt ) ,         E(X0 ) = N,                    (2.63)
                             dt
whence
                                          E(Xt ) = N e(λ−µ)t .                                  (2.64)
The mean obeys a closed equation — exactly the deterministic rate equation one would have
written down without any probability at all — and this is a special feature of linear rates. It
is also the reason the mean is uninformative here: (2.64) is the same whether the process is a
near-certain extinction with a rare enormous excursion or a steady moderate growth.

2.3.2.3    Variance and higher moments

The second moment is obtained the same way. Multiplying (2.60) by n2 and summing gives
                            d  2              
                               E Xt = 2(λ − µ)E Xt2 + (λ + µ)E(Xt ) ,                           (2.65)
                            dt
a linear equation driven by the known mean (2.64). Solving with the integrating factor e−2(λ−µ)t
and subtracting E(Xt )2 gives, for λ ̸= µ,
                                            λ + µ (λ−µ)t  (λ−µ)t   
                             Var(Xt ) = N        e        e       −1 ,                          (2.66)
                                            λ−µ
and in the critical case λ = µ the limit is Var(Xt ) = 2N λt. In the subcritical case λ < µ both
mean and variance decay, but the variance decays more slowly — the coefficient of variation
grows, and the process becomes relatively more random as it dies. At criticality the mean is
constant and the variance grows without bound, which is the analytic signature of the heavy
tail that the following chapter examines in discrete time.
   The pattern generalises. Multiplying the master equation by nϱ produces an equation for the
ϱth moment driven by the lower ones, so the moments can be found recursively without ever
solving (2.61). For a process with nonlinear rates the moment equations do not close, and the
generating-function route of section 2.3.3 becomes not merely convenient but necessary.

2.3.2.4    Hitting probabilities

By proposition 2.6 the probability hi of ever reaching a target set from state i satisfies (2.52),
which involves only the ratios qij /qi . For the birth–death rates (2.23) those ratios are independent
of the state,
                        qn,n+1          λn          λ         qn,n−1       µ
                                =              =        ,            =        ,                  (2.67)
                          qn        (λ + µ)n      λ+µ           qn      λ+µ
so the embedded chain is the simple random walk of section 2.2.1.1 with q = λ/(λ + µ), and
(2.7) answers the question immediately. With absorbing barriers at 0 and M , the probability of
reaching M before extinction, starting from i individuals, is
                             1 − (µ/λ)i                            i
                     hi =                    (λ ̸= µ),     hi =             (λ = µ),            (2.68)
                            1 − (µ/λ)M                             M

                                                  40
since θ = (1 − q)/q = µ/λ. Letting M → ∞ gives the probability of unbounded growth,
1 − (µ/λ)i when λ > µ and 0 otherwise, which is the complement of the extinction probability
found independently in section 2.3.2.5. The two routes agreeing is a useful check on both.
   Mean hitting times behave differently. They depend on the rates and not merely on their
ratios, and at criticality λ = µ the mean time to absorption is infinite even though absorption is
certain — the continuous-time counterpart of the phenomenon derived in full for the critical
Galton–Watson process in the following chapter.

2.3.2.5           Extinction probabilities

Extinction by time t is the event Xt = 0, so its probability is the generating function evaluated
at the origin: p0 (t) = G(0, t). From (2.62),

                                                              41
2.3.2.6   Conditional means

Absorption is often inevitable — for λ < µ, and for the whole subcritical branching range —
and yet a process on its way to absorption need not be featureless. In many cases it settles,
long before it is absorbed, into a distribution that no longer changes with time: a distribution
describing the population conditional on its not yet having been absorbed. The phenomenon
is quasi-stationarity and the limit is the Yaglom limit [45]; modern accounts are Méléard and
Villemonais [46] and Collet, Martínez and San Martín [47].
Definition 2.7 (Quasi-stationary and Yaglom laws). Let {Xt } be a Markov process with
absorbing state 0. A probability distribution ν on the non-absorbing states is quasi-stationary
if P(Xt = j | Xt ̸= 0) = νj for all t ≥ 0 and j ̸= 0 whenever X0 ∼ ν. If, from a specified initial
state, P(Xt = j | Xt ̸= 0) → νj , then ν is the corresponding Yaglom limit.
   Existence of the Yaglom limit for the processes used here is classical, from Yaglom [45] with
modern treatments in [35, 36, 46], and is taken from that literature rather than re-proved.
What is computed below is the limiting conditional mean, explicitly, in both time scales. The
distinction matters: convergence of moments is consistent with the Yaglom theorem but does
not on its own establish convergence of the full conditional law.

The continuous-time constant.          Since Xt = 0 contributes nothing to its own expectation,
                                                        E(Xt )
                                    E(Xt | Xt > 0) =               ,                        (2.71)
                                                        S (N ) (t)
and for N = 1 substituting (2.64) and (2.70) gives, exactly,

                    E(Xt | Xt > 0) =                               =                .       (2.72)
                                               λ−µ                        λ−µ

                                                   µ       1       1
                           lim E(Xt | Xt > 0) =       =         =:    ,                     (2.73)
                        t→∞                       µ−λ   1 − λ/µ    Ac
the reciprocal of the amplitude identified in (2.70). To compare this with the discrete-generation
model the two parametrisations must be matched, and proposition 2.1 is what matches them:
the probability that the birth clock wins is p = λ/(λ + µ), so λ/µ = p/(1 − p), and
                           1 − 2p    2ε             1      1−p
                Ac (p) =          =     ,                =        ,         ε := 1 − 2p.    (2.74)
                           1−p      1+ε           Ac (p)   1 − 2p
The symbol ε is used for the distance from criticality throughout this thesis. (2.74) is elementary
and complete: Ac is a rational function of p, decreasing from Ac (0) = 1 to Ac ( 12 ) = 0, with
                                                                                      −

Ac (p) → 2ε near criticality.
   The reason a closed form was available deserves emphasis, because it is exactly what fails
in discrete time. The survival probability (2.70) is elementary at every time, not merely
asymptotically, because (2.61) is a linear first-order equation with elementary characteristics.
The amplitude is a value of that solution; nothing had to accumulate.

The discrete-time constant. For the binary Galton–Watson process the same decomposition
gives E(Zn | Zn > 0) = (2p)n /Sn . The mean in the numerator is exactly geometric; the survival
probability in the denominator is only asymptotically so, since the quadratic term in (2.12)
becomes negligible once Sn is small, and the decay therefore carries a constant in front of it,
                                                                            Sn
                      Sn ∼ A(p) (2p)n ,     that is       A(p) = lim             ,          (2.75)
                                                                       n→∞ (2p)n


                                                  42
the amplitude of the decay. Granting the limit — which is established in the following chapter,
along with everything else that is known about A(p) — the geometric factors cancel exactly:

                                                       (2p)n      1
                           E(Zn | Zn > 0) −−−→               n

                                            n→∞      A(p)(2p)    A(p)

The limiting conditional mean of the discrete-generation process is thus the reciprocal of its
amplitude, precisely as (2.73) is the reciprocal of the continuous-time amplitude Ac . The same
argument carried to the second moment returns a limiting conditional variance which is again a
function of p and A(p) alone; it is stated and derived in the following chapter, and is not needed
here.

and figure 2.12 compares them.




Figure 2.12: The continuous-time constant Ac (p) = (1−2p)/(1−p) (green) against high-precision
numerical values of the discrete-time constant A(p) (red points). Both vanish at p = 12 , but at
p = 0 the continuous curve starts at 1 and the discrete one at 12 , and the two are not related by
a constant factor in between. The black line is a formula obtained by symbolic regression; it
tracks the points closely but is not exact, and the following chapter explains why no elementary
formula could be.

   The following chapter takes up the constant itself. It shows that the limit (2.75) exists, with
0 < A(p) ≤ 12 , so that the quasi-stationary mean is never below 2; it represents A(p) as a
convergent infinite product; it derives the limiting conditional variance noted above; it identifies
A(p) with a value of the Koenigs linearising function of the logistic map, and proves that function
hypertranscendental, which is why the search for an elementary formula was never going to
succeed; and it gives the near-critical behaviour together with a practical scheme for computing
A(p) across the whole subcritical range. Two of those facts are all the present chapter needs:
that the limit exists, and that it is computable to arbitrary accuracy by iterating (2.12), which
is how the values plotted in figure 2.12 were obtained.



                                                43
2.3.3     The method of characteristics
Every generating-function equation met above is first order: (2.61) for the birth–death process,
and the bivariate equations of section 2.B for the absorption models. That is not a coincidence
but a consequence of linear rates, and it is worth exploiting, because first-order equations have a
general solution method. This subsection presents it.

2.3.3.1    The idea

A first-order partial differential equation says that a certain directional derivative of the unknown
function is prescribed. If one can find the curves along which that direction points — the
characteristics — then along each such curve the equation collapses to an ordinary differential
equation, which can be solved. The solution is then transported along the curve back to a
surface where the initial data are known.
   Concretely, suppose G satisfies
                    ∂G           ∂G           ∂G
                       = a(x, y)    + b(x, y)    ,        G(x, y, 0) = G0 (x, y).                 (2.78)
                    ∂t           ∂x           ∂y

Reparametrise the independent variables as (x, y, t) → (σ, η, τ ), where σ and η label a character-
istic curve and τ runs along it. Along such a curve G is a function of τ alone, so the chain rule
gives
                                 dG   ∂G dt     ∂G dx ∂G dy
                                    =        +         +           .                         (2.79)
                                 dτ    ∂t dτ    ∂x dτ     ∂y dτ
Comparing this with (2.78) rewritten as 0 = −∂t G + a ∂x G + b ∂y G and matching the coefficient
of each partial derivative gives the characteristic system
                   dG            dt              dx                   dy
                      = 0,          = −1,           = a(x, y),           = b(x, y).               (2.80)
                   dτ            dτ              dτ                   dτ
Four ordinary differential equations have replaced one partial differential equation. The first
says that G is constant along characteristics, so G depends only on the labels (σ, η); the second
says the parameter runs backwards in time, which is what allows the initial surface to be reached
from any (x, y, t); the last two are the curves themselves.
   The parametrisation is fixed by anchoring the labels to the initial surface,

                       x(σ, η, 0) = σ,      y(σ, η, 0) = η,      t(σ, η, 0) = 0,                  (2.81)

so that G(σ, η, τ ) = G0 (σ, η). The whole problem then reduces to a change of variables: express
σ and η in terms of (x, y, t) by integrating the characteristic equations and substitute. That
final substitution is where the work lies.
   Figure 2.13 shows the geometry for the x-equation of the example that follows.

2.3.3.2    A worked example: absorption–death

The example is chosen so that the answer can be checked. Consider a single compartment
that takes in particles from a surrounding medium, in which a resident particle may then die;
figure 2.14 sets out the geometry, which is common to every absorption model in this thesis. Let
Xt count particles inside the compartment and Yt those outside, and let the events be

          P(∆X = 0, ∆Y = 0 | Xt = i, Yt = j) = 1 − (jα + iµ)∆t,                    (no event)

Figure 2.13: Characteristics of the x-equation (2.88), dx/dτ = µ(1 − x) with τ = −t, drawn

σ at which it meets the initial surface t = 0, drawn as the heavy vertical line; together they

Recovering the solution.        Rearranging (2.93) for η, substituting σ from (2.92) and τ = −t
from (2.91), gives
                          µ −αt    α −µt    α             
                η =1−        e  +     e  +     e−αt − e−µt x + e−αt y,
                         µ−α      µ−α      µ−α

and (2.90) then reproduces (2.85) exactly. The two routes agree.

2.3.3.3    What the example shows

The structure is always the same. The equations for G and for t are universal: G is constant
on characteristics and τ = −t. Only the equations for the state variables change from model
to model, and they are ordinary differential equations in one variable each, solved in cascade —
x first, since (2.88) does not involve y, then y using the result. The triangular structure is a
consequence of the model, not of the method: absorption moves particles from outside to inside,
so the exterior count depends on the interior one and not conversely.
   The difficulty is concentrated in one integral. In the worked example (2.88) was linear and its
solution elementary, so the substitution into (2.89) produced another linear equation. When
the interior dynamics include births, the coefficient of ∂x G acquires a quadratic term  R
                                                                                           and (2.88)
becomes a Riccati equation; it still solves in closed form, but the subsequent integral eατ x(τ )dτ
no longer does, and the solution acquires a special function. That is the absorption–birth–death
model, carried out in full in section 2.B.2, where the closed form obtained is a result of this
thesis.
   The method needs no independence. In the worked example the particles were independent,
and characteristics merely confirmed an answer that independence had already supplied; once
replication is allowed inside the compartment that shortcut fails, and only the first-order structure
of the equation remains — which is all the method uses.


2.A       Survival of small populations
Section 2.2.1.2 defined the binary Galton–Watson process from a single progenitor, and the
following chapter follows that process through: whether it dies out, how long it takes, and what
remains of it when the survivors are gathered together. One question belongs here rather than
there, because it concerns founding cohorts rather than late-time behaviour and is used by the
modelling chapters that follow. How long does a small population last, and how much difference
does the size of the founding group make?
   Near criticality, with p and 1 − p each close to a half, a lineage dies at its first round almost
half the time. Should it survive one generation, both its offspring fail at their own first attempt


                                                 47
with probability around 0.25, so the lineage reaches the second generation only with probability
about 0.375:
                      0.375 ≈          (1 − 0.5)        × (1 − 0.5 × 0.5) .
                                          |    {z    }             |          {z         }
                                 no extinction at the first step       nor at the second

More generally, survival to a given generation is obtained by iterating (2.12). At exactly critical
branching the extinction probabilities for n = 1, 2, 3, 4 are un = 0.500, 0.625, 0.695, 0.741 to
three significant figures, so the probability of survival decays quickly over the first few rounds,
and does so almost as fast just above criticality as just below (figure 2.16).

               Sn
           1


          0.5                                                                                    p = 0.55
                                                                                                 p = 0.50
                                                                                                 p = 0.45
                0           1                  2                   3                     4   n

Figure 2.16: Survival probability over the first four generations from a single progenitor. Even
in the supercritical case (p = 0.55) the early decay is steep, and the three curves have barely
separated by n = 4.


An initial cohort of size k

Suppose now Z0 = k: the process begins with a group rather than an individual. Under
supercritical branching, growth becomes more predictably exponential as the count rises, because
a large number of individuals lets the expected dynamics show through the noise. The founding
cohort is precisely the regime in which that noise dominates instead.
   The lineages are independent and each is extinct by generation n with probability un , so all
k have gone with probability unk and

                                              Sn(k) = 1 − unk .                                             (2.94)

Letting n → ∞, the survival probability of a single lineage tends to the attracting fixed point
of (2.12), which is 0 for p ≤ 12 and (2p − 1)/p for p > 12 ; both are established in the following
chapter. A single lineage is therefore ultimately extinct with certainty below criticality and with
probability (1 − p)/p above it, so that the establishment probability of the whole cohort is 0
                              k
for p ≤ 12 and 1 − (1 − p)/p for p > 12 : a larger cohort helps decisively above criticality and,
                                                                                   (k)
in the long run, not at all below it. Figures 2.17 and 2.18 show Sn through 500 generations
for k = 1, 10, 100, 1000. The improvement in longevity that a larger first cohort buys is acutely
sensitive to criticality, and the two panels of figure 2.18 differ only in the third decimal place of
p.


2.B     The absorption models in full
Section 2.3.3 developed the method of characteristics on one absorption model. This appendix
collects the family that model belongs to: the simplest member, in which particles are only
taken up; and the hardest, in which they may replicate once inside, and for which the closed
form obtained is a result of this thesis.


                                                     48
                                      (k)
Figure 2.17: Survival probability Sn from (2.94) out to 500 generations at exact criticality,
p = 0.5, for k = 1, 10, 100, 1000 (blue through to red). A larger founding cohort buys longevity,
but at criticality every curve is still headed for zero.




Figure 2.18: As figure 2.17, but with p displaced slightly from criticality: p = 0.505 on the left
(supercritical), p = 0.495 on the right (subcritical). A displacement of half a per cent separates
curves that were nearly coincident in figure 2.17.


   The setting is a single compartment drawing particles from a surrounding medium. Once
several compartments share a medium, a question arises that the models of section 2.2 do not
address: what becomes of the contents of the first compartment to rupture? The shared medium
acquires, at a stroke, a potentially large cohort of particles available for subsequent absorption by
the compartments that remain. The analysis elsewhere in this thesis assumes immediate transfer,
in which the entire contents of a ruptured compartment relocate at once. The alternative is an
absorption period, during which absorption events redistribute the released cohort among the
remaining compartments and only then do birth, death and rupture resume.
   Throughout, Xt and Yt count particles inside and outside the compartment, the initial
condition is X0 = 0, Y0 = N , and events have exponentially distributed inter-event times.

2.B.1    Absorption only
The simplest case has no dynamics inside the compartment at all:

             P(∆X = 1, ∆Y = −1 | Yt = j) = jα ∆t,                         (absorption)
               P(∆X = 0, ∆Y = 0 | Yt = j) = 1 − jα ∆t,                    (no event)

2.B.2    Absorption–birth–death
Introducing replication for particles inside the compartment, the factorisation (2.97) survives but
no longer helps: from a single initial particle, birth events make infinitely many states reachable,
so G1 is an infinite sum. The partial differential equation must be solved directly.
   Birth at per-capita rate λ is added to the absorption–death model of section 2.3.3.2:

              P(∆X = 1, ∆Y = −1 | Yt = j) = jα ∆t,                                                                    (absorption)

Proposition 2.8 (Generating function for absorption–birth–death). With x+ = 1, x− = µ/λ,
b1 = λ − µ, ω = α/b1 , W = (x − x+ )/(x − x− ) and Ψ as in (2.112),
                                        n                                  oN
                            G(x, y, t) = e−αt y − Ψ(W ) + Ψ W eb1 t                .         (2.114)


   Three checks confirm the result. At t = 0 the two Ψ terms cancel and G = y N , the
required initial condition. At x = y = 1 we have W = 0 and Ψ(0) = x+ = 1, so G = 1 and
probability is conserved. And setting x = 1 alone gives G(1, y, t) = (e−αt y + 1 − e−αt )N , so
Yt ∼ Binomial(N, e−αt ) — exactly right, since the exterior particles are absorbed independently
at rate α and what happens inside the compartment cannot affect them. Beyond these, (2.114)
has been verified numerically against direct integration of (2.99), agreeing to around 10−13 across
sub- and supercritical interior regimes and for N = 1, 2, 3.

2.B.3    A parameter-regular representation
The form (2.114) is compact, but its derivation separated an antiderivative into pieces that
are individually singular at certain parameter values. The underlying probability calculation
supplies a second representation that stays finite everywhere.

                                                    52
   Let F (x, t) be the generating function, at time t, of an interior linear birth–death process
started from a single particle. Conditioning on the first event gives its backward equation,
                                  ∂F
                                     = λF 2 − (λ + µ)F + µ,              F (x, 0) = x,                 (2.115)

                                  F (x, t) − 1     x − 1 b1 t

                                                                 1−x

Now follow a single exterior particle. Either it has not been absorbed by time t, which happens
with probability e−αt and contributes y; or it is absorbed at some time v ∈ (0, t), with density
αe−αv , and thereafter founds an interior birth–death process of duration t − v. Conditioning on
the absorption time,
                                           Z t
          g(x, y, t) = e−αt y + αe−αt              eαv F (x, v) dv,     G(x, y, t) = g(x, y, t)N ,     (2.118)

the second identity because the N exterior particles are independent throughout. (2.118) is real
and finite for x, y ∈ [0, 1] and all positive rates, including λ = µ where (2.114) degenerates. For
λ ̸= µ, substituting (2.116) into the integral and integrating term by term returns (2.114); so
(2.118) also specifies unambiguously which continuation of the special-function expression is
intended.

Remark 2.9 (Domain of validity). The series (2.112) converges for |ζ| < 1, and the arguments
appearing in (2.114) are W and W eb1 t . In the subcritical interior regime µ > λ we have b1 < 0
and x− = µ/λ > 1, so for x ∈ [0, 1]
                                                   λ
                                    0≤W ≤            < 1,        0 ≤ W eb1 t ≤ W :
                                                   µ

                                                            53
the series representation is valid on the whole of the unit square, which is the case of most interest
here. When λ > µ the arguments may leave the unit disc or cross the standard hypergeometric
cut; one may then continue analytically, tracking the branch along the characteristic, but the
real integral (2.118) is usually safer. A convenient continuation is the Euler integral
                                                 Z 1
                                                        uω−1
                  2 F1 (1, ω; ω + 1; ζ) = ω                   du         (ω > 0, ζ ∈
                                                                                   / [1, ∞)),
                                                  0    1 − ζu
which was used for the numerical verification quoted above. The apparent singularity of W at
x = x− is a coordinate singularity only: F (x− , t) = x− for every t, and the limit of (2.118) there
is perfectly regular.
Remark 2.10 (Resonance). The denominators in (2.112) vanish when n + ω = 0 for some integer
n ≥ 1, that is when
                              α = n(µ − λ),     n = 1, 2, . . .                     (2.122)
This is the classical resonance condition for linearising the y-equation: the decay rate α of
the absorption channel coincides with a harmonic of the interior relaxation rate µ − λ. The
generating function itself is perfectly regular there. The two Ψ terms of (2.114) each acquire a
pole, but their combination does not: in the resonant term the difference
                                                           n
                                               W eb1 t      − e−αt W n
                                                           n+ω
has a finite limit as ω → −n, producing the familiar secular factor t e−αt in place of a simple
exponential. Equivalently (2.118) has no singular denominator at all and supplies the limit
directly. The resonant parameter values form a set of measure zero, but the degeneracy should
be kept in mind when α happens to sit near an integer multiple of µ − λ.

Remark 2.12. Applied to the two pieces of (2.109) — the first with h = α, the second with
h = α + b1 , and both with k = b1 , P = σ − x− and Q = −(σ − x+ ) — proposition 2.11 reproduces

factor (σ − x+ )/(σ − x− ) attached to the second piece is easily lost, and its loss is invisible at
t = 0; checking G(1, 1, t) = 1 catches it immediately.

                                                      57
Chapter 3

Galton–Watson branching and the
constant A(p)

      Abstract.

      This chapter develops the binary Galton–Watson process and the constant A(p) that governs
      its behaviour conditional on survival. After deriving the survival recursion and its fixed
      points, we turn to the subcritical range, where the population conditioned on being alive
      settles to a quasi-stationary law with mean 1/A(p) and a limiting variance determined by p
      and A(p) alone. The constant is defined for 0 < p < 12 as the limit of Sn /(2p)n ; existence
      is established by rewriting that limit as a convergent infinite product, which also fixes the
      degenerate endpoint A(0) = 12 . Rescaling the survival recursion to the logistic map identifies
      A(p) with a value of the associated Koenigs linearising function. A theorem of Becker and
      Bergweiler shows that this function is hypertranscendental, so that for each fixed multiplier r
      it has no elementary formula as a function of its spatial argument z. That theorem does not
      by itself settle the separate closed-form question for p 7→ A(p), but it helps explain the failure
      of a long search for one. We then treat the critical endpoint, where extinction is certain and
      the expected lifetime is nevertheless infinite, and use its power-law decay to motivate the
      near-critical approximation A(p) ∼ 2(1 − 2p). The chapter closes with a practical scheme
      for computing A(p) across the subcritical range and a complex-plane view that places this
      scalar within the Koenigs coordinate; appendices preserve the full hypertranscendence proof,
      the complete account of the formula search, and the second-moment calculation behind the
      limiting conditional variance.




3.1     Introduction
For 0 < p < 21 , both the expected population and the probability of survival decay geometrically.
Their ratio is governed by a constant A(p), and the population conditioned on survival conse-
quently approaches a finite limiting mean 1/A(p). This chapter asks three related questions:
why that constant exists, what can be said about an elementary expression for it, and how it
can be computed. The answers begin with the elementary branching model and lead naturally
to an infinite product, a Koenigs linearising function, and the singular limit at criticality.
   The Galton–Watson process is a discrete-time, discrete-space Markov chain. It is a stochastic
model in which every member of a population independently either replicates or dies at each
generational increment; by these rules, the population fluctuates over the course of its lifetime.
Originally formulated by Francis Galton and Henry Watson in the late nineteenth century, it
stands as one of our most fundamental models of stochastic branching.
   Let Zn denote the population census at generation n. In the standard case, there is a single



                                                     58
initial individual,
                                             Z0 = 1.
At the passage to each successive generation n, all Zn−1 existing individuals independently

   • replicate with probability p, or

   • die with the remaining likelihood 1 − p.

This is the simple Galton–Watson process. It is also possible to define the process as having
potentially multiple offspring per individual, but here we focus on the classic binary case.




 Realisation of the simple Galton–Watson process with p = 0.58. Galton–Watson visualiser ª

Generally, the expected value of a discrete random variable X is defined by
                                            X
                                   E(X) =        P(X = xk ) xk ,
                                             k

the sum over all possible states xk in product with their probability of realisation.
   Hence, after one generation (with Z0 = 1), we have

                             E(Z1 ) = P(Z1 = 2) · 2 + P(Z1 = 0) · 0.

Replication occurs with probability p, resulting in two offspring. So this simplifies to

                                          E(Z1 ) = 2p.

By the following argument, we can compute the expected population size at generation n: E(Zn ).
  If we know the value of Zn , then the conditional expectation of the next generation is
                                                 Zn
                                                 X
                               E(Zn+1 | Zn ) =         E(Z1 ) = 2p Zn .
                                                 i=1

More generally, if we do not know Zn , the tower property of conditional expectation gives
                                                   
                      E(Zn+1 ) = E E(Zn+1 | Zn ) = E(2p Zn ) = 2p E(Zn ).

Hence,
                                   E(Z2 ) = 2p E(Z1 ) = (2p)2 ,

                                                 59
                                   E(Z3 ) = 2p E(Z2 ) = (2p)3 ,
so, induction gives
                                         E(Zn ) = (2p)n .
From this result, we observe a standard behaviour of branching processes:

   • If 2p > 1, then E(Zn ) grows geometrically with n. In this case, the branching process is
     said to be supercritical.

   • If 2p < 1, then E(Zn ) decays geometrically with n, and the branching process is subcritical.




                      Subcritical Galton–Watson realisation with p = 0.47.

When 2p = 1 (that is, p = 0.5), the Galton–Watson process is said to be critical. And in this
case, the expectation remains constant:

                                  E(Zn ) = 1n = 1      for all n.


3.2     Survival probability and extinction
We will see that un , the probability of extinction by generation n, can be computed using the
probability generating function. It will follow that the probability of ultimate extinction is 1 in
both critical and subcritical branching. We write Sn = 1 − un for the survival probability, the
likelihood of non-extinction at generation n. This recurrence will also provide the starting point
for the constant A(p) below.

The probability generating function is defined by

                                          ϕ(s) = E(sX ),

for a discrete random variable X.
   In our case, the offspring distribution is given by X = Z1 , and hence

                                 ϕ(s) = E(sZ1 ) = ps2 + (1 − p).

For the Galton–Watson processes, it is well known that

                                          un+1 = ϕ(un ),

                                                60
where un denotes the probability of extinction by generation n.
   This relationship can be understood intuitively by considering the fate of the population after
the first generation.

   • If the process dies in its first step, then extinction has already occurred. In this case, the
     probability of extinction by any generation is equal to 1. This event occurs with probability
     1 − p.
   • If the process replicates in its first step (with probability p), then the population reaches
     generation 1 with two individuals. Extinction by generation n + 1 then requires both of
     these fresh lineages to become extinct by their nth generation; and this happens with
     probability u2n .

Combining these cases yields the recursion

                                 un+1 = (1 − p) + p u2n = ϕ(un ),                                              (3.1)

which describes the probability of extinction from one generation to the next. And given the
population cannot come back to life once it has died out, un will be a non-decreasing sequence
bounded above by 1. Conversely, the probability of survival defined by the complement,

                                      Sn = P(Zn > 0) = 1 − un ,

will be monotonically decreasing and bounded below by 0, but may converge to a positive value
S∞ ∈ [0, 1]. And to determine how these probabilities behave in the late time limit, we can use
the fact that they converge and seek steady state solutions.
   Substituting un = 1 − Sn into (3.1), we obtain

                                1 − Sn+1 = p(1 − Sn )2 + (1 − p).

Expanding and simplifying gives

                                Sn+1 = 2pSn − pSn2 ,         S0 = 1.                                           (3.2)

To find steady-state solutions, let
                                          Sn+1 = Sn = S∞ .
Then S∞ satisfies
                                                                                         S∞
                                2                                 1
                  S∞ = 2pS∞ − pS∞ ,

or equivalently
                               
              p S∞ S∞ + p1 − 2 = 0.
                                                            S∞   0.5
Thus there are two roots for S∞ , corresponding to
two potential steady-state solutions:
                                        2p − 1
      S∞ = 0 and        S∞ = 2 − p1 =          .
                                          p                                Subcritical         Supercritical
                                                                  0
But since Sn is a probability, it must lie within the                  0                 0.5        p          1
interval [0, 1], so when p < 0.5, the second root is
negative and thus not physically meaningful. Then,
when p > 0.5, the probability of ultimate survival is
positive, given by the larger root,
                                                 2p − 1
                                           S∞ =         .
                                                    p

  0                                     0                                         0

                    S                                        S                                   S

Figure 3.1: Fixed points of the survival recursion S 7→ 2pS − pS 2 . Each panel shows the map
(blue) against the diagonal (dashed), with fixed points marked in red. Below and at criticality
the only fixed point in [0, 1] is the origin; for p > 12 a stable positive root S∞ = (2p − 1)/p
separates from it (at p = 0.74, this is approximately 0.649).


   So we have determined that in both the subcritical (p < 0.5) and critical (p = 0.5) cases,
extinction is certain. That certainty is not the end of the matter. Below criticality a few
realisations outlive the great majority, and the population conditioned on still being alive settles
to a definite mean; at the critical point itself, extinction is certain and the expected lifetime is
nevertheless infinite.


3.3        Limiting Conditional Mean E(Zn |Zn > 0) = 1/A(p)
With 0 ≤ p ≤ 12 , the Galton–Watson process becomes extinct almost surely. The constant
A(p) belongs to the subcritical range, and everything below is developed for 0 < p < 21 , where
A(p) ∈ (0, 12 ). The endpoint p = 0 is degenerate — the process dies at the first step — and is
treated separately in Section 3.4, where the product representation assigns it the value A(0) = 12 .
   But picture this: You initiate a large number of such branching chains with the same
0 < p ≤ 0.5, and watch as they simultaneously progress. Many will die out early, but some may
persist for many generations. Here, we will consider the behaviour of those realisations which
outlive the majority.
   Specifically, we will aim to compute E(Zn |Zn > 0) for n → ∞, the conditional limiting mean
of non-extinct populations, and we will see that it becomes stationary.


To begin, we can decompose the expectation of population size into the two conditional expecta-
tions of persistence and cessation:

                   E(Zn ) = E(Zn |Zn > 0) · P(Zn > 0) + E(Zn |Zn = 0) · P(Zn = 0).
Since E(Zn |Zn = 0) = 0, and P(Zn > 0) = Sn , this simplifies to

                                       E(Zn ) = E(Zn |Zn > 0) · Sn .
Hence,
                                                                  E(Zn )
                                             E(Zn |Zn > 0) =             .
                                                                   Sn



                                                          62
Then, we know that E(Zn ) = (2p)n , so

                                                       (2p)n
                                     E(Zn |Zn > 0) =         .
                                                        Sn
And we understand what happens to (2p)n for large n. But what about Sn ? It may go to 0 in
the limit, but how does it get there, and what will this say about the conditional mean?
   Recall the recursion
                                      Sn+1 = 2p Sn − p Sn2 .
Because Sn → 0, for growing generation count the quadratic term pSn2 diminishes faster than
the linear term. Consequently, at large n the recursion is increasingly well approximated by

                                          Sn+1 ∼ 2p Sn .

That suggests, but does not by itself establish, that Sn decays like (2p)n with some constant
standing in front of it. The object we want is that constant,
                                            Sn
                               A(p) = lim        ,      0 < p < 12 ,                          (3.3)
                                       n→∞ (2p)n

and the whole content of the claim lies in the existence of the limit. The restriction to p > 0 is
not a formality: at p = 0 the process dies at the first step, so Sn = 0 for every n ≥ 1 and the
ratio Sn /(2p)n reads 0/0. That endpoint is defined instead by the product representation of
Section 3.4, which gives A(0) = 12 and agrees with the limit of A(p) as p ↓ 0. For 0 < p < 12 ,
numerator and denominator both vanish, and nothing said so far prevents their ratio from drifting
to 0 or growing without bound; the approximation Sn+1 ∼ 2pSn controls the increments but
says nothing about what they accumulate to. So existence is not assumed here. It is established
in Section 3.4, where the same ratio is rewritten as an infinite product whose convergence to a
strictly positive value is elementary, and where the product also shows at once that A(p) < 12 .
Granting it, we may write
                                 Sn ∼ A(p) (2p)n     as n → ∞,                               (3.4)
where the tilde means that the ratio of the two sides tends to 1, and not that the two sides are
equal in the limit: Sn itself tends to 0 for p < 12 , and one must not write lim Sn = A(p)(2p)n as
an ordinary limit identity. The constant A(p) absorbs the cumulative effect of the quadratic
terms in the survival recurrence before the linear regime dominates — which is precisely why no
argument confined to late generations will produce it. And it follows that the conditional mean
in the late generation limit is given by

                                                     (2p)n    1
                            lim E(Zn |Zn > 0) = lim        =      .
                           n→∞                    n→∞ Sn     A(p)

So because A(p) is constant, the limiting conditional mean is also constant; it may also be
referred to as the quasi-stationary mean population. The continuous-time counterpart of this
constant, for the linear birth–death process, is elementary in closed form; the mathematical
introduction computes it and sets the two side by side. Figure 3.2 shows the approach to it for
five values of p, and shows at the same time the difficulty that will occupy us later: the closer p
lies to 12 , the higher the plateau and the more generations the process takes to reach it. The
same approach is visible in the raw paths: Figure 3.3 records a large ensemble at a single p close
to criticality.

                         Mean population conditioned on non-extinction.
                  Values of P vary from bottom to top: 0.3, 0.4, 0.45, 0.48, 0.49.

                  20

              n

                   0
                        0        20        40             60     80           100

Figure 3.2: The conditional mean (2p)n /Sn against generation number n, with Sn obtained
by iterating (3.2). From bottom to top, p = 0.3, 0.4, 0.45, 0.48, 0.49. Each curve approaches
the constant 1/A(p); the limiting values are 2.825, 4.208, 6.893, 14.703 and 27.476. The lowest
three have essentially converged by n = 100, but the p = 0.49 curve has reached only 24.1 of its
limiting 27.5.


3.3.1   The limiting conditional variance
The mean is not the only thing that settles. Carrying the same argument through the second
moment gives a limiting conditional variance
                                                                
                                                 1         1               1
                       Var(Zn | Zn > 0) −→           1+              −         ,            (3.5)
                                                A(p)    1 − 2p           A(p)2

which, like the mean, is a function of p and A(p) alone: conditioning on survival introduces no
second unknown constant. The derivation is short and is given in Appendix 3.C.
    This is worth more than a second moment usually is. A mean that stabilises is on its own
weak evidence that the conditioned population has settled down, since a mean can hold steady
while the distribution behind it spreads without limit; a second moment that stabilises alongside
it is the least one should ask of a population described as quasi-stationary. The law to which
the conditioned population actually converges is the Yaglom limit [45], whose existence for this
process is classical and is quoted from the mathematical introduction rather than re-proved
here; the constant 1/A(p) and the expression (3.5) are its first two moments. Both diverge as
p → 12 , the variance the faster of the two, which is the quantitative form of the remark that
       −

the near-critical process is slow to settle and large when it does.
    But the constant A(p) is still something of a mystery. As we will see, computing an analytic
formula is not straightforward.




                                                 64
Figure 3.3: Ensemble of binary Galton–Watson realisations at p = 0.49732333, followed for 670
generations. Grey paths are individual population sizes Zn ; the blue curve is the mean among lin-
eages still alive; the red dashed line is the limiting conditional mean 1/A(p). Yaglom visualiser ª

                                                   66
3.5     The logistic map and Koenigs representation
Recall the rescaling of Section 3.4: with Sn = 2wn and r = 2p, the survival recursion (3.2) is the
logistic map
                        wn+1 = rwn (1 − wn ),     w0 = 12 ,   0 < r < 1.                     (3.7)
That the survival probabilities are the orbit of a standard dynamical system is more than a
change of letters, and this section draws out what it gives: the constant A(p) is the value at w0
of the function that linearises (3.7).

Aside: the map outside the branching window. The logistic map is best known for
its behaviour at larger multipliers, where increasing r towards 4 produces the characteristic
period-doubling route to chaos (Figure 3.4). None of that regime is used below. The branching
problem fixes r = 2p ∈ (0, 1), where the origin is attracting and the orbit from w0 = 12 decreases
monotonically to it; the figure is included only to place the map in its usual setting.




Figure 3.4: Aside, not used in the argument. Phase diagram of the logistic map that shows the
period doubling of stable states, ultimately resulting in chaotic behaviour. Blue sample line on
the left at r = 3.5255, right plot shows a trajectory of the map with the same value of r. The
subcritical branching window is r = 2p ∈ (0, 1), at the far left of this diagram, where the only
attractor is the fixed point at the origin. Bifurcation visualiser ª


3.5.1   The identity A(p) = 2ψ2p (1/2)
We now relate A(p) to the Koenigs function ψr of the logistic map. The parameter r is fixed
throughout this section, and the subscript is dropped where no confusion arises, so that ψ always
means ψr .

Definition 3.1. Let f (z) = rz(1 − z) with 0 < |r| < 1, so that z = 0 is a fixed point of f
with multiplier f ′ (0) = r. Koenigs proved that there is a unique function ψr , analytic in a
neighbourhood of the origin, such that
                               
                       ψr f (z) = r ψr (z),     ψr (0) = 0,     ψr′ (0) = 1.

The last two conditions are normalisations, fixing the origin and the scale of the new coordinate;
the first says that ψr turns the nonlinear iteration z 7→ f (z) into multiplication by r.

  Here z = wn and f (z) = wn+1 , so the functional equation reads

                                      ψ(wn+1 ) = r ψ(wn ),


                                               67
which is what is meant by saying that ψ linearises the recursion. We show that A(p) = 2ψr (w0 ) =
2ψ2p (1/2), and the work lies in establishing that
                                                                 
                                                              wn
                                        ψ(w0 ) = lim             .
                                                   n→∞        rn

Applying the functional equation at the first two steps of the orbit gives

                                           ψ(w1 ) = rψ(w0 ),
                                                    ψ(w1 )
                                        =⇒ ψ(w0 ) =        ,                                     (3.8)
                                                      r
                                           ψ(w2 ) = rψ(w1 ),
                                                    ψ(w2 )

                                                      r

                                                ψ(w2 )
                                           ψ(w0 ) =    ,
                                                 r2

                                                       ψ(wn )

to the limit:                                              

The normalisation now does the work. Definition 3.1 sets ψ(0) = 0 and ψ ′ (0) = 1, so ψ(z)/z → 1
as z → 0; and wn = Sn /2 → 0 as n → ∞. The factor ψ(wn )/wn therefore tends to 1, while

                                                                 

                             A(p) = 2ψr (w0 ) = 2ψr (1/2) = 2ψ2p (1/2),

where the subscript is a reminder that ψr is a different function for each value of r, and hence
for each p.

3.5.2    The closed-form question
The identity above shows directly that A(p) can be written in terms of the Koenigs function,
ψr (z), which is associated with the discrete logistic map. That does not reduce the search for a
formula to a question about ψr — the two questions come apart, as the caveats below make
clear — but it does make the analytic nature of ψr the natural thing to ask about next. There
the answer is negative in a strong and provable sense: for every multiplier in the subcritical
window the Koenigs function is not an elementary function at all.
   The relevant notion is stronger than ordinary transcendence. A function g, analytic near a
point, is called differentially algebraic if it satisfies some algebraic differential equation, meaning
that there is an integer n ≥ 0 and a non-zero polynomial P with
                                                                      
                                 P z, g(z), g ′ (z), . . . , g (n) (z) = 0.

                                                    68
If no such equation exists, g is called hypertranscendental. The point of this test is its reach:
every elementary function—anything assembled from algebraic, exponential, logarithmic and
trigonometric pieces by finitely many arithmetic operations and compositions—is differentially
algebraic. That is a classical fact rather than an observation of ours, and it rests on two
ingredients. The generators of the class each satisfy an evident algebraic differential equation:
an algebraic function satisfies one obtained by differentiating its defining polynomial, g = ez
satisfies g ′ − g = 0, g = ln z satisfies zg ′ − 1 = 0, and g = sin z satisfies g ′′ + g = 0. And the
differentially algebraic functions are closed under the arithmetic operations, differentiation and
composition, so nothing built from those generators can escape the class. Rubel’s survey [49]
gives the closure arguments in full, together with the standard examples of functions that do
escape it. So establishing hypertranscendence rules out the entire elementary class in one stroke,
rather than defeating candidate formulas one at a time.
Theorem (Appendix 3.A, Theorem 3.5). For every r with 0 < r < 1, the Koenigs function ψr
of the logistic map f (z) = rz(1 − z) at the fixed point z = 0 is hypertranscendental. Consequently
it has no elementary analytic expression on any neighbourhood of the origin.
The proof is given in Appendix 3.A. It runs through the inverse change of coordinate: a theorem
of Becker and Bergweiler [20] shows that θr = ψr−1 , which carries the linear coordinate back to
the original one, is hypertranscendental, and the fact that differential algebraicity is inherited by
local inverses carries the conclusion back to ψr . Since the subcritical branching window p ∈ (0, 12 )
corresponds exactly to r = 2p ∈ (0, 1), the theorem covers every case relevant here.
   Two caveats are worth stating plainly. First, the theorem concerns ψr as a function of z
at each fixed r; it does not by itself settle whether the individual number A(p) = 2ψ2p (1/2),
or the map p 7→ A(p), admits a closed form. Those are separate questions, and the argument
here leaves them open. Second, non-elementary does not mean inaccessible: ψr is described
exactly by its functional equation, by the limit limn→∞ r−n f ◦n (z), and by a Taylor series whose
coefficients follow by recursion. What is excluded is a finite elementary expression, which is
precisely the thing we spent so long searching for (Appendix 3.B).
   It is not that closed forms never occur for this family. Having been discovered in the late 19th
century (1884) by French mathematician Gabriel Koenigs, the function has had some time to be
understood, and for the logistic map f (z) = rz(1 − z) it does submit to closed form description
in two special cases. When r = 2 & r = 4, ψr (z) can be written in terms of a finite number of
combined elementary functions:

   • r=2       =⇒      ψ2 (z) = − 12 ln(1 − 2z),
                                         √ 2
   • r=4       =⇒      ψ4 (z) = (arcsin ( z)) .

At r = 2 and r = 4 the multiplier at the origin is repelling (|r| > 1), so those closed forms are
Poincaré-type linearisations rather than attracting-Koenigs maps for r ∈ (0, 1). Neither case
lies in the subcritical branching window r = 2p ∈ (0, 1), and neither is a counterexample to the
theorem above.
    The complex-plane geometry of this coordinate is taken up in Section 3.9.


3.6     The critical endpoint: extinction and expected lifetime
We now focus on the critical case, p = 0.5. Although the expectation remains positive for all
finite generations, ultimate extinction is certain. So the process will eventually fixate at 0, but
the expected lifetime of the process is infinite.
   And this is a curious mathematical result...


                                                   69
  Intuitively, it can be understood by the fact that the total lifetime, T , of this critical branching
process is distributed according to a power law.
               (a) Survival probability                          (b) Extinction time PMF                          (c) Total progeny PMF

Figure 3.5: Power-law behaviour in a critical Galton–Watson branching process (p = 0.5).
(a) Survival probability Sn = P(Zn > 0), showing the late generation asymptotic scaling
Sn ∼ 2/n.
(b) Probability mass function of the extinction time Textinct = inf{n : Zn = 0}, exhibiting a
heavy tail P(Textinct = n) ∼ 2/n2 .
                                                      P
(c) Probability mass function of the total progeny T = k≥0 Zk , displaying the classical critical
branching asymptotic P(T = n) ∼ n−3/2 . (106 realisations).

For such power law distributed variables there is a modestly high chance of extraordinarily
large realisations. When the expectation is infinite, the sample mean of a growing collection of
realisations does not settle to any finite value. It is not that the sample average climbs steadily
with sample size; it need not, and between rare events it drifts down. What happens instead
is that the average is repeatedly dominated by single, exceptionally large observations: each
time one arrives it lifts the average by an amount comparable to the average itself, and however
many samples have already been taken, another such observation is still to come.
   But aside from this intuitive understanding, we will now see how this curious result of infinite
expected lifetime can be demonstrated.

3.6.1         Expected lifetime
We are interested in E(T ) when p = 0.5, where T denotes the extinction time or the number of
generations that the Galton Watson process survives. This is a measure of how many generations
the process survives and is defined as

                                                             T = inf{n : Zn = 0}.

that is, the smallest generation index n at which the population is no longer positive. The
process continues over generations
                                      0, 1, 2, . . . , T − 1,
then fixates to 0, surviving for T generations in total.

3.8     Practical determination of 1/A(p) near criticality
3.8.1    Direct numerical approximation
Approximate values of A(p) are obtained by iterating Sn+1 = f (Sn ) and reading off the normalised
ratio
                                                  SN

                                                (2p)N
which is the quantity that converges. It is worth being clear about what does not: SN is not
settling to a stationary value but decaying to zero, ultimately at the geometric rate 2p, so that
neither a threshold on SN nor one on the difference SN +1 − SN is by itself a statement about
the accuracy of RN . The telescoping identity of Section 3.4 shows moreover that the ratio and
the truncated product are the same sequence,
                                                                  
                                           1 NY
                                              −1
                                                    Sk

so monitoring either one is the same computation.
                                                                 Q
   The truncation error is then easy to control. Since A(p) = RN k≥N (1 − Sk /2) and Sk+1 ≤
2p Sk ,
                                    A(p)     1 X             SN
                          0 ≤ 1−          ≤         Sk ≤            .                 (3.19)

                                                    73
Every factor omitted is less than 1, so RN always overestimates A(p), and (3.19) bounds the
overestimate. This gives a stopping rule rather than a guess: for a target relative accuracy δ,
iterate until
                                      SN ≤ 2δ (1 − 2p),                                  (3.20)
and the returned RN is then within relative error δ of A(p). The same tolerance transfers
directly to the quasi-stationary mean, since 1/RN and 1/A(p) differ by the same relative amount
— though in absolute terms the error in 1/A is δ/A(p), which is large precisely where A(p) is
small.
   What fails near criticality is not the rule but its cost. Writing ε = 1 − 2p and using the
near-critical profile (3.17), condition (3.20) is first met at
                                                        
                                             1        1
                                      N ≈      ln 1 +   ,                                   (3.21)
                                             ε        δ

so the iteration count required for a fixed accuracy grows like 1/(1 − 2p). At δ = 10−6 the
rule stops after 1.4 × 104 iterations at p = 0.4995, 1.4 × 105 at p = 0.49995 and 6.9 × 105 at
p = 0.49999, in each case within a fraction of a per cent of (3.21). The scheme therefore does not
break down at some particular p; it simply becomes more expensive than one is willing to pay
for, and where that point falls depends on the accuracy demanded and the iterations available.

3.8.2    Product/asymptotic hybrid near criticality
In the absence of an exact formula for A(p) we are left with the product and the near-critical
asymptotic, and the practical question is where to change from one to the other. For most of
the range between p = 0 and p = 0.5 the product settles quickly: at δ = 10−6 the stopping rule
(3.20) is met after 60 iterations at p = 0.4 and 335 at p = 0.48. The two branches then pull in
opposite directions as p → 21 . The product remains valid, and its error remains bounded by
                               −

(3.19), but its cost grows like 1/(1 − 2p); the surrogate A ≈ 2 − 4p costs nothing and improves
as p increases, but at any fixed p < 12 it carries an error that the product does not. Underlying
both is the fact that A(p) → 0 while 1/A(p) → ∞, so that a small absolute error in A becomes
an O(1) error in the quasi-stationary mean; it is relative accuracy that must be controlled here,
which is what (3.20) does.
   The crossover should therefore be stated as a budget, not as a universal number. Fix a target
relative accuracy δ for the product branch and a ceiling Nmax on the iterations spent per value
of p. By (3.21) the product branch stays inside that ceiling exactly while

                                                ln(1 + 1/δ)
                                     1 − 2p ≥               ,                               (3.22)
                                                   Nmax

and equality defines the crossover c. The quoted value c = 0.49999 is the instance δ = 10−6 ,
Nmax ≈ 7 × 105 ; the distance 1 − 2c of the crossover from criticality scales as 1/Nmax , so a
tenfold budget buys one further decimal place in c.
   What that choice costs is set by the surrogate on the other branch. Measured against the
product, the relative error of 2 − 4p behaves like ε ln(1/ε) with ε = 1 − 2p: it is 7.7 × 10−3
at p = 0.4995, 1.0 × 10−3 at p = 0.49995 and 2.3 × 10−4 at p = 0.49999. Since it decreases
as p → 12 , its worst value on [c, 12 ) is the one at the crossover itself, and the accuracy of the
          −

scheme as a whole is the larger of δ and that value. With c = 0.49999 the hybrid returns A(p) to
a relative accuracy of 10−6 below the crossover and about 2 × 10−4 above it. Moving c towards
1
2 improves the second figure and increases the cost, and that trade, rather than any particular
decimal, is what fixes the cutoff.

3.9      The linearising coordinate in the complex plane
The analysis above has treated the Koenigs function ψr chiefly through the single value A(p) =
2ψ2p (1/2) and its consequences for the branching process. In the complex plane the same
function can be read as a coordinate, and its geometry locates that scalar within the larger
analytic object from which it comes. The figures are direct exports from the accompanying
interactive program ª.

3.9.1     Basin geometry and orbit capture
For fixed r, the iteration-limit representation

                                       ψr (z) = lim r−n fr◦n (z)
                                                 n→∞

is obtained by following an orbit as it is drawn towards the origin. Its natural domain here
is the attracting basin {z : fr◦n (z) → 0}, and this is the limit evaluated by the program. A
seed may be captured within a few steps, reach the same fixed point only after a long irregular
transient, or escape to infinity (Figure 3.7). The first two behaviours therefore belong to the
same basin, however different their transients appear; the third does not. Passing from these
individual orbits to the set of all captured seeds produces the coloured region in Figures 3.7 and
3.8, and in panels (a)–(e) of Figure 3.9. Its boundary is the Julia set of fr [50], which separates
the attracting basin from the escaping dynamics; the plotted boundary is therefore dynamical.




        (a) Rapid capture.               (b) Slower capture.                 (c) Escape to infinity.

Figure 3.7: Orbit fates for fr (z) = rz(1 − z) at r = 0.64 (p = 0.32). The seeds z = −0.229139 +
0.514714i, z = −0.812055 + 0.625998i and z = 1.043377 + 1.900730i in (a)–(c) give, respectively,
rapid capture, capture after a long transient and escape. The first two therefore lie in the same
attracting basin.

                           (a) The height at z = 12 against the A(p) curve.




                           (b) The same surface viewed from lower down.

        Figure 3.8: The Koenigs coordinate and A(p) (continued on the following page).


3.9.2    The Koenigs coordinate and the constant A(p)
What is drawn inside the basin is the Koenigs coordinate itself. The black curves are the
Cartesian grid of the linearised plane,

                             {Re ψr = const},         {Im ψr = const},

pulled back through ψr into the nonlinear z-plane. In the coordinate y = ψr (z) this grid is
regular and uniform; the figures record its distortion when expressed in z. The functional
equation                                     
                                    ψr fr (z) = rψr (z)
then accounts for the nested geometry. Multiplication by r preserves the form of the linearised
grid while rescaling it and, when r is complex, rotating it. Pulled back to the basin, this scaling
law produces progressively smaller copies towards the attracting fixed point, with each cell
recurring inside the next. The nested detail is therefore the functional equation made visible.
The attracting Koenigs coordinate defined here has the basin as its natural domain; the dark
exterior is instead shaded by escape rate.
   The identity already established in Section 3.5,
                                                        
                                                        1
                                         A(p) = 2ψ2p    2    ,

makes Figure 3.8 the centre of this construction. For the real branching slice r = 2p ∈ (0, 1), the
plotted height of |2ψr (z)| above z = 12 is A(p); its reciprocal is the quasi-stationary mean from
Section 3.3, and its value is computed from the product of Section 3.4. The cyan curve on the
back panel traces the same height as p ranges over (0, 12 ), while the dashed line joins its value on

   The branching initial condition fixes the seed: S0 = 1 gives w0 = 12 . This is also the critical
point of the logistic map because
                                       fr′ (z) = r(1 − 2z)
vanishes there. The two funnels descend to the zeros ψr (0) = ψr (1) = 0, at the attracting
fixed point and its other preimage fr (1) = 0; the same two zeros flank the marker on the later
coordinate plates. Thus the scalar A(p) is one distinguished value of the analytic coordinate
drawn across the whole basin.

3.9.3    Leaving the real branching slice
The branching problem occupies a one-real-dimensional slice of the two-real-dimensional r-plane.
Figure 3.9 begins with r = 2p = 0.64, the branching case p = 0.32, and then gives r a growing
imaginary part. More generally, Koenigs’ construction supplies ψr for every multiplier with
0 < |r| < 1, whereas the probabilistic reading requires r to lie on the open real segment (0, 1).
In panels (b)–(e), |r| < 1: the origin remains attracting, the Koenigs coordinate and its basin
remain well defined, and the figures retain their complex-dynamical meaning. What is lost is
the probability model. Once r/2 is not real in (0, 12 ), it is not a branching probability, so these
panels are analytic continuation in the parameter rather than new probabilistic cases. The
change is in interpretation, not in the analytic construction.
   Panel (f) crosses the boundary of this construction. There r = 0.64 − 0.78i has |r| ≈ 1.009 > 1,
the origin is no longer attracting, and the attracting Koenigs chart used above no longer applies.

          (a) r = 0.64                 (b) r = 0.64 − 0.09i              (c) r = 0.64 − 0.33i




      (d) r = 0.64 − 0.65i              (e) r = 0.64 − 0.75i             (f) r = 0.64 − 0.78i

Figure 3.9: Continuation from the real branching slice at Re r = 0.64. Panel (a) is the branching
case p = 0.32, with A(p) = 0.335521 and 1/A(p) = 2.980439. In (b)–(e), |r| is 0.646, 0.720, 0.912
and 0.986: the Koenigs coordinate persists, but r/2 is no longer a probability. In (f), |r| ≈ 1.009
and the attracting chart no longer applies. The marked point is z = 21 throughout.
