Figure 3.10: Five deep zooms outside the attracting window, with |r| equal to 1.147, 1.582,
1.582, 1.568 and 1.015 in panel order. Since all exceed one, none shows the attracting Koenigs
chart; the images instead place the branching segment r = 2p ∈ (0, 1) within the wider complex
quadratic family. Koenigs visualiser ª




                                                 79
3.9.4    Beyond the attracting window
Figure 3.10 places the short real interval r = 2p ∈ (0, 1) within the wider complex quadratic
family. Every displayed multiplier has |r| > 1, so these deep zooms were generated outside
the regime of the attracting Koenigs coordinate developed above. Their role is comparative:
the probabilistic window is only one real segment in a parameter space that continues in both
complex directions. Each image shows only a deep zoom of its coordinate plate.
   The visual complexity makes the negative closed-form result of Section 3.5.2 feel less surprising,
but it is not evidence for the theorem. The result obtained from Becker and Bergweiler concerns
the function z 7→ ψr (z) at each fixed r and is established by the argument in Appendix 3.A [20];
it does not decide whether the individual number A(p) at any chosen parameter, or the separate
map                                                        
                                      p 7−→ A(p) = 2ψ2p 12
has a closed form. Nor does a fractal basin boundary by itself preclude a tractable analytic
coordinate: the Böttcher coordinate on the basin of infinity has the same Julia set as boundary
and remains explicitly manageable [50]. The pictures provide context for the theorem, not a
proof of it.
   The complex-plane view therefore places the scalar A(p) inside the geometry of the Koenigs
coordinate while showing how little of the analytic family the branching model occupies. That
model retains only the real segment r = 2p ∈ (0, 1), ending at the critical boundary r = 1; on
this slice, the distinguished coordinate value is precisely the survival constant whose probabilistic
consequences were derived above.


3.10      Conclusion
For the subcritical binary Galton–Watson process, the survival probability has the asymptotic
form
                                      Sn ∼ A(p)(2p)n ,
where A(p) exists, is strictly positive, and is given by the convergent product (3.6). This single
constant determines the first two moments of the population conditioned on survival: the mean
converges to 1/A(p) and the variance to (3.5). It is therefore not merely a normalising constant
for survival probability, but a quantitative descriptor of the Yaglom limit.
   The logistic rescaling identifies A(p) with the Koenigs-function value 2ψ2p (1/2). Hypertran-
scendence rules out an elementary expression for z 7→ ψr (z) at fixed r ∈ (0, 1), while leaving
the separate closed-form question for p 7→ A(p) open. At the critical boundary the geometric
decay is replaced by Sn ∼ 2/n, producing infinite expected lifetime; matching the two regimes
gives A(p) ∼ 2(1 − 2p) as p ↑ 12 . Together, the infinite product and this near-critical asymptotic
provide a practical numerical description across the subcritical interval.


3.A      No elementary formula for the Koenigs function of the
         logistic map when 0 < r < 1
For the logistic map fr (z) = rz(1 − z) with 0 < r < 1, the origin is an attracting fixed point. Its
Koenigs function ψ is a local analytic change of coordinate that turns the nonlinear iteration into
the simple contraction y 7→ ry. We show here that ψ satisfies no algebraic differential equation;
functions with this property are called hypertranscendental. The route is indirect: a theorem
of Becker and Bergweiler gives hypertranscendence for the inverse change of coordinate, and a
standard fact about inverse functions carries the conclusion back to ψ. Since every elementary



                                                 80
analytic function does satisfy an algebraic differential equation, ψ has no elementary formula
near the origin. This is the result quoted in Section 3.5.2.

Notation. Throughout this appendix ψ = ψr denotes the Koenigs function of Definition 3.1,
and θ = ψr−1 its local inverse. The subscript is dropped in the content below, so ψ and θ always
mean ψr and ψr−1 .

3.A.1    The question
Fix a real parameter r with 0 < r < 1 and consider

                                         fr (z) = rz(1 − z).                                 (3.24)

In the branching setting of Section 3.5.2 this is the survival recurrence written in the coordinate
wn = Sn /2, with r = 2p; the subcritical window p ∈ (0, 12 ) is exactly r ∈ (0, 1).
   The origin is a fixed point, since fr (0) = 0. The derivative at a fixed point is called its
multiplier, and here it is
                                            fr′ (0) = r.
Because 0 < r < 1, each application of fr shrinks small displacements from the origin. Points
that start close enough are therefore drawn towards the origin under repeated iteration, making
it an attracting fixed point.
   Koenigs’ linearisation theorem [51, 50] supplies a unique function ψ, complex analytic near
the origin, satisfying

                         ψ(fr (z)) = rψ(z),        ψ(0) = 0,          ψ ′ (0) = 1.           (3.25)

The last two conditions are normalisations: they place the new coordinate at zero when z = 0
and fix its scale. The first says that two routes lead to the same place, in that one may apply
the nonlinear map fr and then change coordinates with ψ, or change coordinates first and then
simply multiply by r. In the coordinate y = ψ(z), therefore, the nonlinear iteration z 7→ fr (z)
becomes the linear contraction y 7→ ry.
   The phrase closed form has no universal mathematical definition, so we pin the question down
with a precise test: does ψ satisfy an algebraic differential equation? Every elementary formula
satisfies some equation of this kind, so a negative answer rules out the entire class at once.

3.A.2    A precise test for elementary formulas
Definition 3.2. An analytic function g is differentially algebraic if some polynomial relation
holds between z, the value g(z) and finitely many derivatives of g. More precisely, there must
be an integer n ≥ 0 and a non-zero polynomial P with complex coefficients such that
                                                                  
                                P z, g(z), g ′ (z), . . . , g (n) (z) = 0.                   (3.26)

If no such equation exists, g is called hypertranscendental.

    Hypertranscendence is a stronger condition than ordinary transcendence. The exponential
is the standard illustration: ez is transcendental, yet g = ez satisfies g ′ − g = 0 and so is
differentially algebraic. Logarithms and the trigonometric functions are differentially algebraic
too, wherever they are analytic, and so is anything built from algebraic, exponential, logarithmic
and trigonometric functions by finitely many arithmetic operations and compositions. To prove
that ψ is hypertranscendental is therefore to prove that no expression of that kind can represent
it.

                                                   81
   The result we need is a special case of a theorem of Becker and Bergweiler [20]. Their
statement concerns rational functions, so it is worth recalling that a rational function is a
quotient of polynomials; every polynomial—including fr —is therefore rational.
Theorem 3.3 (Becker–Bergweiler). Let R be a rational function of degree at least two with

                                     R(0) = 0,     0 < |R′ (0)| < 1.

If a local analytic function θ satisfies
                                 
                        θ R′ (0)z = R(θ(z)),           θ(0) = 0,       θ′ (0) = 1,          (3.27)

then θ is hypertranscendental.

  The role of θ is easiest to see by running the Koenigs change of coordinate backwards. Set
R = fr and θ = ψ −1 , so that z = θ(w) whenever w = ψ(z). Equation (3.25) then reads

                                           θ(rw) = fr (θ(w)).

Because fr′ (0) = r, this is exactly Equation (3.27). Theorem 3.3 therefore applies to θ, the
inverse Koenigs function, which carries the linear coordinate back to the original one; it does not
apply to ψ directly. Terminology varies here: Becker and Bergweiler call θ a Schröder function,
whereas some authors reserve that name for ψ.
   One further fact lets us carry the conclusion from θ back to ψ.
Lemma 3.4. Let g be analytic near the origin with g ′ (0) ̸= 0. If g is differentially algebraic,
then its local inverse g −1 is also differentially algebraic.

Proof. Write h = g −1 , so that g(h(w)) = w. Differentiating this identity repeatedly, with the
chain rule at each step, expresses the derivatives of g at h(w) in terms of the derivatives of h.
The first two are
                                        1                       h′′ (w)
                         g ′ (h(w)) = ′    ,    g ′′ (h(w)) = − ′       .
                                     h (w)                     (h (w))3
Every higher derivative has the same shape: a rational expression whose denominator is a power
of h′ (w). Now take an equation of the form (3.26) for g, substitute z = h(w), use g(h(w)) = w,
and replace each derivative of g by the corresponding expression in h. Multiplying through by
a sufficiently high power of h′ (w) clears the denominators and leaves an algebraic differential
equation for h, so h is differentially algebraic.

3.A.3     Main result
Theorem 3.5. For every r with 0 < r < 1, the Koenigs function ψ of fr (z) = rz(1 − z) at the
origin is hypertranscendental. Consequently, it has no elementary analytic expression in any
neighbourhood of the origin.

Proof. Because ψ ′ (0) = 1, the inverse-function theorem gives a local analytic inverse θ = ψ −1
with
                                     θ(0) = 0,     θ′ (0) = 1.
As shown above, the Koenigs equation then reads

                                           θ(rw) = fr (θ(w)).

It remains to check the hypotheses on R = fr . The map fr is a polynomial of degree two, so it
is rational and of degree at least two, and at the origin

                                 fr (0) = 0,      0 < fr′ (0) = r < 1.

                                                  82
Every hypothesis of Theorem 3.3 is now met, so θ is hypertranscendental.
   Suppose now, for contradiction, that ψ were differentially algebraic. Lemma 3.4 would then
make its local inverse θ differentially algebraic as well. But θ is hypertranscendental, which says
precisely that it is not. This contradiction proves that ψ is hypertranscendental, and since every
elementary analytic function is differentially algebraic, no elementary formula for ψ can exist
locally.

3.A.4    What the result means
Theorem 3.5 gives a rigorous sense to the informal statement that the Koenigs function has
no closed form: what it rules out is an elementary formula analytic on some neighbourhood of
the origin. The proof is carried out in the complex plane, but the real case is covered as well,
because an elementary real-analytic formula on an interval around zero would extend locally to
the same complex-analytic function.
   None of this makes ψ inaccessible or uncomputable. For z sufficiently close to the origin it is
given exactly by the limit
                                    ψ(z) = lim r−n fr◦n (z),                               (3.28)
                                             n→∞

where fr◦n denotes fr applied n times, and its Taylor coefficients can be computed recursively
from Equation (3.25). The functional equation, the limit and the Taylor series are all exact
descriptions of ψ; what the theorem excludes is a finite elementary expression.


3.B     Search for an elementary formula
There is no known formula which exactly describes A(p), so during the project, Grant and I
dedicated efforts to finding one. We tried a number of approaches; for example,

  • Evaluating the Sn+1 = f (Sn ) map as an ODE in the large generation limit using (Sn+1 −
    Sn )/1 → dS/dn: dS/dn = (2p − 1)Sn − pSn2 .

  • A machine learning method of evolving the terms and coefficients of potential formulas
    using the genetic algorithm.

But unfortunately, nothing gave way to a satisfactory result. It wasn’t until months after all
but giving up that I decided to have another look and found the dynamical argument presented
above: the Koenigs function through which A(p) is expressed admits no elementary formula in
its spatial argument, which is not a proof that p 7→ A(p) has none, but does say that the obvious
route to one is closed.

3.B.1    Genetic algorithm search attempts
Before the Koenigs connection was appreciated, a number of methods were tried in search of an
elementary formula. And one of those was a Genetic algorithm search based regression. The
concept is simple: create 50 functions at random, measure how close each one of them is to
correctly describing the curve, then select the best 5 and produce a new population of 50 by
creating variations on the best 5. Measure the new 50, and repeat this cycle of variation and
selection many times over. Figure 3.11 shows one such search, restricted to polynomials.
   When implementing the first version, I used a search space of combined polynomials; partly
because I had a feeling that the solution might show up in that form, and partly because of the
relative simplicity of encoding such functions.
   In the minimal case, a basic polynomial of the standard form can be used.


                                                83
Figure 3.11: A genetic algorithm evolving polynomials to fit numerically generated A(p) data.
Each genome encodes the terms and coefficients of a polynomial. Grey curves are candidates
from a population of 50 (mutation rate 30%); blue points are the data; the red curve is the best fit
after 90 generations (MSE 1.35 × 10−4 ). The first visualiser is a vibe-coded interface to a Python
implementation I wrote by hand; v2 is a later vibe-coded attempt at a better solver, made before
the Koenigs connection. Polynomial-fit visualiser ª Polynomial-fit visualiser (v2) ª

And the "genome" encoding this class of functions would be [a0 , . . . , ak ], to some arbitrarily
chosen maximum order k. The initial 50 genomes are produced as lists of uniformly random
generated real coefficients. It turns out that many different particular and substantially varying
formulas will produce a curve that looks very close to correct, but we wanted the one exact
formula... It occurred to me that the true formula may contain only integer or rational coefficients.
So an updated version pursued polynomials of the form,

Two years after setting this work aside, I decided to have another go at using an evolutionary
regression method like this one to find A(p). It was just starting to become apparent how little
reason there was to expect a formula, but I had another go anyway. This time with the option of
using AI tools to write a more sophisticated symbolic tree-search program with leveraged time
and effort. This new version is capable of searching a space of progressively nested elementary
functions of all kinds. Although, of course, a similar conclusion was reached: many functions of
varying form will closely resemble the target curve, but no satisfactory simple formula emerged,
even on favouring solutions of less component parts.

3.C       The limiting conditional variance
Section 3.3.1 quoted the limiting conditional variance (3.5) without deriving it. The derivation is
short, and worth having: the mean of a distribution says nothing about whether the distribution
itself is settling down, and a second moment that also stabilises is the least one should ask of a
population claimed to be quasi-stationary.
    Write ϕn for the probability generating function of Zn , so that ϕn = ϕ ◦ ϕn−1 , and recall that
ϕ′n (1) = E(Zn ) and ϕ′′n (1) = E Zn (Zn − 1) . For the binary offspring law the generating function

collapse. Differentiating the composition once,

which at z = 1 recovers E(Zn ) = 2p E(Zn−1 ) = (2p)n , since ϕn−1 (1) = 1. Differentiating (3.29)
again,                                 n                                o

large on the scale ε−1 , and of relative width comparable to its mean.




                                                 87
Chapter 4

The birth–death–catastrophe process

4.1     Introduction
A cell can empty by ordinary deaths, or it can rupture and release its load. At the first handful
of replicative cycles those fates are decided by chance: a deterministic trajectory that moves
smoothly between one bacterium and none answers the wrong question. What one wants is the
chance that the infection establishes itself at all, and how large the intracellular population is if
it does.
   The process that answers those questions is elementary. Each replicative unit inside the cell
independently
 • divides at rate λ,
 • dies at rate µ, or
 • triggers rupture of the whole cell at rate δ.
That is the birth–death–catastrophe (BDC) process, restated as definition 4.2 below. A positive
integer is the intracellular load; 0 is internal extinction; H records that the cell has ruptured.
   One reading is the early intracellular phase of Y. pestis in the macrophage: the bacterium
replicates within the cell and then lyses it, releasing the accumulated load. The word “catastrophe”
names that bursting event, not an adverse judgement on the bacterium, and its hazard grows with
the intracellular population. Closely related stochastic models have been used for F. tularensis
[16, 52], for Salmonella enterica [53], and for anthrax [54]. More broadly, the balance between
establishment and collapse in small infected populations is itself a stochastic object [14, 55].
None of the formulae requires that biological reading.
   The process used here is a specialisation of the birth–immigration–catastrophe model of
Brockwell, Gani and Resnick [21], who wrote in 1982 that

          “There has recently been a great deal of interest in the development and analysis
      of stochastic models for the growth of populations which are subject to catastrophes
      due either to death or large-scale emigrations.”

The process they introduced was more general than the one used here. It also allowed immigration,
through single upward steps arriving at a constant Poisson rate, and catastrophes whose sizes were
independent random variables. In the present model immigration is absent, and a catastrophe
ends the intracellular process altogether rather than removing a random number of individuals.
The population moves directly to 0, or to a distinct post-catastrophe state H, and thereafter
remains fixed. The mathematics rests on the classical theory of birth–death processes and their
killed variants [56, 57, 58].
   The derivation proceeds as follows.


                                                 88
 • A first-step argument produces an autonomous Riccati equation for the non-catastrophe
   probability I(t).
 • Internal extinction D(t) obeys the same flow from a different initial condition; their difference
   is the productive probability Ifix = I − D.
 • The mean load is J = −I ′ /δ; higher load moments are polynomials in I.
 • Release moments follow by balance, using the same higher-order terms.
 • Burst size and burst time are read off from those functions.
Once I is known, no other state probability is needed to advance it in time. The roots a > 1 > b
of the characteristic quadratic make the closed forms readable. The lower root b is the probability
of internal extinction before rupture; 1/a is the ratio of the geometric burst-size law; their
difference supplies the time scale θ = λ(a − b). Write A = a − 1 and B = 1 − b.
   The principal results, in advance, are these. From one founder, I(t), D(t) and Ifix (t) are
explicit in a, b and w = eθt . The mean load and mean release are
                          λ                                   h     λ        i
                     J = − (I − a)(I − b),        V = (1 − I) 1 +     (1 − I) ,
                          δ                                         δ
with late-time release V∞ = aB/A. Neither is a typical-cell history: J mixes large loads from a
dwindling productive set with zeros from every path that has finished, and V∞ averages over
realisations that never rupture. Conditional on rupture, the first two burst-size moments match
a geometric law of ratio 1/a; the law itself is derived in Chapter 5. Variances of load and release
are likewise functions of I.
Remark 4.1 (What is classical here, and what is developed here). The Riccati equation for
the non-catastrophe probability, together with its two roots, belongs to the classical theory of
birth–death processes with killing [57]. The process is a special case of the birth–immigration–
catastrophe model of Brockwell, Gani and Resnick [21], and the lower root b is the familiar
extinction fixed point from branching-process theory [36, 35].
   The present treatment keeps the released count Wt alongside the load Xt , which gives its first
two moments and the corresponding conditional burst moments. It also keeps non-catastrophe,
internal extinction and continued productivity separate; this distinction is lost when death
and rupture share a single absorbing state, yet it is needed for the quasi-stationary analysis of
Chapter 5. Finally, expressing the load moments as polynomials in I supplies the age-dependent
quantities used by the population model of Chapter 6.
  Four assumptions are load-bearing, and all four are contestable.
 1. Linear birth. Each particle divides at rate λ whatever the current load: there is no
    intracellular carrying capacity.
 2. Catastrophe hazard δn. Rupture is triggered at rate δn from load n, not δn2 and not a
    threshold at a critical load.
 3. Instantaneous, complete release. The whole load leaves the cell at one instant.
 4. Constant rates. No eclipse phase and no age-dependence within the cell.
   Section 4.2 defines the pair (Xt , Wt ) and fixes the conventions. Section 4.3 derives and solves
the scalar equation for the three cell-fate probabilities. The moment calculation follows in
section 4.4, after which section 4.5 explains the special role of I. Section 4.6 turns the moment
results into statements about burst size and burst time, and section 4.7 collects the formulae for
later use. The final section records the boundary of the calculation and the connection to the
next two chapters; section 4.A records the principal limiting checks.


                                                89
4.2     The process
The birth–death–catastrophe process was introduced in the mathematical introduction as
definition 2.4. We restate it here for the single-cell reading.
Definition 4.2 (Birth–death–catastrophe process). Let Xt take values in {0, 1, 2, . . .} ∪ {H} and
record the intracellular load while the cell remains intact. Each replicative unit independently
gives birth at rate λ, dies at rate µ, and triggers rupture at rate δ. For n ≥ 1, the non-zero
transition rates are
                          qn,n+1 = λn,      qn,n−1 = µn,     qn,H = δn,                       (4.1)
and both 0 and H are absorbing.

   The units counted by Xt are infectious particles inside a host cell, and rupture releases the
entire load at once. Because the total catastrophe rate in state n is δn, a crowded cell is more
likely to burst than a sparsely occupied one. This population dependence is the small addition
that changes the character of the process. The same chain is drawn beside the linear birth–death
process in figure 2.5; figure 4.1 repeats it here so that the two absorbing mechanisms remain in
view.

      birth–death
                                                λ                 2λ              3λ
                    0                 1                  2                  3            ···
                             µ                 2µ                 3µ




      birth–death–catastrophe
                                                λ                 2λ              3λ
                    0                 1                  2                  3            ···
                             µ                 2µ                 3µ

                                               δ             2δ    3δ

                                                        H

Figure 4.1: Linear birth–death dynamics (top) and the birth–death–catastrophe process used
throughout this chapter (bottom). Double circles are absorbing. Ordinary extinction enters 0
only from state 1; catastrophe jumps from any positive count to the distinct state H, at total
rate δn.

                                             91
   Figure 4.2 shows a handful of exact realisations of the pair (Xt , Wt ). Some paths decline to 0;
others grow for a time and end abruptly at H. The corresponding release paths contain only
one possible movement: a jump from zero to the load carried at rupture.
   Colouring the paths by their eventual fate, as in figure 4.3, makes visible what a single
undifferentiated bundle conceals. The exact mean J(t) = E(Xt ) passes through the ensemble,
but it belongs to neither class of path.
Remark 4.3 (The mean load describes no typical path). After a realisation reaches 0 or H, its
numerical load is zero. The late-time mean therefore averages substantial loads from a shrinking
set of productive paths against zeros from every path that has finished. It is neither the load of
a typical cell nor the load of a typical surviving cell. The latter is the conditional mean J/Ifix ,
considered in section 4.6.3.

4.2.2     The released count and the joint process
Rupture creates a second count, the number of particles released into the extracellular medium.
It remains zero while the cell is intact and, in the absence of any imposed extracellular dynamics,
remains fixed after release. Let
                                         τ = inf{t : Xt = H}
be the catastrophe time. The released count is
                                              (
                                                  0,       t < τ,
                                       Wt =                                                     (4.2)
                                                  Xτ − ,   t ≥ τ.

The burst size is the load present an instant before rupture, K := Xτ − , as recorded in (2.25).
The left limit matters: at τ the internal state has become H, while the released load is the
integer present an instant earlier.
   The joint transition list makes the relation between the two counts explicit. Writing ∆Xt =
Xt+∆t − Xt and similarly for Wt , and conditioning on Xt = n ≥ 1,

         P(∆Xt = 1, ∆Wt = 0 | Xt = n) = λn ∆t + o(∆t)                         (birth)
        P(∆Xt = −1, ∆Wt = 0 | Xt = n) = µn ∆t + o(∆t)                         (death)
   P(Xt+∆t = H, Wt+∆t = n | Xt = n) = δn ∆t + o(∆t)                           (catastrophe)     (4.3)
         P(∆Xt = 0, ∆Wt = 0 | Xt = n) = 1 − (λ + µ + δ)n ∆t + o(∆t)           (no event).

This pair (Xt , Wt ) will be followed through its means, variances and covariance. Most of the
work lies in the marginal moments; the joint structure, as we shall see, is unusually simple.

4.2.3     Two conventions for rupture
There is another reasonable way to represent rupture: send the process to 0 rather than to a
separate state H. After all, once a cell has ruptured it contains no replicative units, although
the cell itself has also ceased to exist. We call the transition to 0 killing and the transition to H
catastrophe. Figure 4.4 shows the distinction.




                                                   92
                            (a) killing                                             (b) catastrophe
                       δn



                                                λn                                                     λn
         0         1          ···         n            n+1             0        1        ···     n              n+1
                                              µ(n+1)                                                  µ(n+1)
      absorbing
                                                                                                      δn
         Xt                                                            Xt
                                                                                                                   H

                                                                                                                absorbing
                                                                                                      rupture

                                              rupture to 0
                                                             t                                              H         t



Figure 4.4: Two conventions for rupture. Under killing (left), rupture from state n ends at 0
and is indistinguishable from internal extinction. Under catastrophe (right), used here, the same
transition ends at the separate state H. Birth and death rates remain λn and µn.

   The choice does not alter the observed burst times or burst sizes. It alters what can be
distinguished after absorption. Under killing, internal extinction and rupture arrive at the same
state; their probabilities cannot be recovered separately from the state occupancy. With H
kept apart, we may distinguish the internal-extinction probability D, the rupture probability
1 − I, and the productive probability Ifix = I − D. That distinction is the reason for using H
throughout. Chapter 5 carries both conventions through the generating-function treatment.

4.2.4         Notation
Although H is a label rather than a number, after rupture the intracellular load is zero. Whenever
Xt appears arithmetically—in a sum, product or moment—we use the numerical-load convention

                                              Xt = n 7→ n,            Xt = H 7→ 0.

Thus H remains distinct from 0 as a state, allowing the two absorbing mechanisms to be
distinguished, but contributes zero to E(Xtp ) and to products such as Xt Wt .
                                                                                              
   One possible collision is best removed now: K denotes the burst size, whereas K(t) = E Xt2
will denote the second moment of the load. They are unrelated. The full list of symbols appears
in table 4.1. Unless explicitly stated otherwise, the process begins with one unit, X0 = 1.


4.3          The Riccati equation and its two roots
Three probabilities sit close together in this process and are easily confused: the cell may have
avoided rupture, it may have died internally, or it may still be productive. Their meanings differ,
yet all three can be obtained from one scalar differential equation. We derive that equation first,
then follow its flow from two initial conditions.

4.3.1         First-step decomposition
Let
                                               I(t) = P(Xt ̸= H | X0 = 1)
be the non-catastrophe probability, the chance that rupture has not occurred by time t. A path
counted by I(t) may still be productive or it may already have died at 0; it is excluded only if it
has reached H.

                                                                 93
  Consider the first event experienced by the founding unit during a short interval. Time
homogeneity allows the process left after that event to be followed for a further duration t:
                                                           
                I(t + ∆t) = µ ∆t + 1 − (λ + µ + δ)∆t I(t) + λ ∆t I(t)2 + o(∆t).

If the founder dies, rupture has been avoided for good, giving the first term. A rupture contributes
nothing. If the founder divides, both descendant families must avoid rupture, and the branching
property gives probability I(t)2 . After rearrangement,
                    I(t + ∆t) − I(t)                                  o(∆t)
                                     = µ − (λ + µ + δ)I(t) + λI(t)2 +       .
                          ∆t                                           ∆t
Taking ∆t → 0 gives
                            dI
                               = µ − (λ + µ + δ)I + λI 2 ,       I(0) = 1.                      (4.4)
                            dt
This is a Riccati equation, but its useful feature is simpler than the name suggests: the right-hand
side contains I and the three rates, and nothing else. The equation closes before any moments
have been introduced.

4.3.2    The two roots
The quadratic on the right of (4.4) becomes transparent once its roots are named.
Lemma 4.4 (The characteristic quadratic has one root on either side of unity). Let λ > 0,
µ ≥ 0 and δ > 0, and set
                                                                        λ+µ+δ
                        q(x) = λx2 − (λ + µ + δ)x + µ,            η=          .
                                                                          2λ
Then q has two real roots
                                     r                            r
                                                µ                            µ
                             a=η+        η2 −     ,        b=η−       η2 −     ,
                                                λ                            λ
satisfying b < 1 < a, with ab = µ/λ and a + b = (λ + µ + δ)/λ. Moreover b ∈ [0, 1), with b = 0 if
and only if µ = 0.

Proof. The roots are real and distinct because
                        µ   (λ + µ + δ)2 − 4λµ   (λ − µ)2 + δ(δ + 2λ + 2µ)
                 η2 −     =                    =                           >0
                        λ          4λ2                      4λ2
when δ > 0. Since q(1) = λ − (λ + µ + δ) + µ = −δ < 0, the value at 1 lies below the axis of an
upward-opening parabola, so one root lies on each side of unity. Finally, Vieta’s relation gives
ab = µ/λ ≥ 0. As a > 0, this implies b ≥ 0, with equality precisely when µ = 0.

Remark 4.5 (Standing assumptions). Unless a limiting case is being considered, we take λ > 0,
µ ≥ 0 and δ > 0, and set X0 = 1. The case δ = 0 is the linear birth–death process of
section 2.2.2.4; it returns in section 4.A.4 as a limit. The linear state dependence of the rates
also makes the process non-explosive, with finite moments at every finite time. This permits the
differentiations under expectation used in section 4.4.
   The Riccati equation now reads
                                         dI
                                            = λ(I − a)(I − b).                                 (4.5)
                                         dt
This factorisation gives a phase-line picture before it gives a formula. The initial value I(0) = 1
lies between b and a, where the derivative is negative. Consequently I(t) decreases towards b

When µ > 0, the paths which avoid rupture indefinitely are exactly those which die internally
first. Thus b is the probability of internal extinction before rupture. When µ = 0, death is
impossible, b = 0, and every path eventually ruptures. The lower root is therefore both the
stable point of the differential equation and a probability. The upper root is not visited by I(t),
although it will acquire its own probabilistic role through the burst-size law.

4.3.4    The same equation again: internal extinction
Let
                                  D(t) = p0 (t) = P(Xt = 0 | X0 = 1)
be the probability of internal extinction by time t. The first-step argument has the same shape
as before. Death of the founder contributes certainty; division requires both descendant families
to die; rupture contributes nothing. Consequently
                         dD
                            = µ − (λ + µ + δ)D + λD2 ,               D(0) = 0.                  (4.9)
                         dt
The meanings of I and D differ, but their motion is governed by the same flow. For b > 0,
inserting D(0) = 0 into (4.7) gives the initial ratio a/b and hence the following expression. When
b = 0, internal extinction is impossible and the same formula is recovered directly as D ≡ 0.

Theorem 4.7 (The internal-extinction probability). The probability that the process has died
out internally by time t is
                                          ab(w − 1)

                                           aw − b
  The two solutions approach b from opposite sides: I descends from 1 and D rises from 0.
Their narrowing separation is the probability that the cell is still productive.

4.3.5    The non-fixation probability
Define
                                  Ifix (t) = P(Xt ∈
                                                  / {0, H} | X0 = 1) .
Since 0 and H are the only ways to leave the productive states,

                                         Ifix (t) = I(t) − D(t).

Equivalently, if T0 and TH are the two hitting times, then Ifix (t) = P(t < T0 ∧ TH ). This is the
survival event used for conditioning in Chapter 5: the cell must remain intact and retain a
positive load.
   A direct first-step calculation is useful because it anticipates the multi-founder case. Following
a birth, the combined process is productive at time t provided no descendant family has triggered

                                                   96
catastrophe and the two families have not both died out. Those events have probabilities I(t)2
and D(t)2 , respectively, so the birth branch contributes I 2 − D2 . Thus

                                     dIfix                               

Subtracting (4.9) from (4.4) gives the same equation, as it should.

at time t is

  The boundary values fit the interpretation. At t = 0, the last expression is one; as t → ∞, its
numerator grows as w and its denominator as w2 , so it tends to zero. Evaluating the differential
equation at the origin gives
                                                 ′
                                                Ifix (0) = −(λ + µ + δ) + λ = −(µ + δ),

the combined rate at which the founding unit is lost either to death or to rupture. A birth does
not end productivity.
   If µ = 0, then b = 0 and D ≡ 0, so Ifix = I. When µ > 0, the two functions separate,
and conditioning on non-catastrophe alone admits cells which are intact but already empty.

4.3.6    Several founders
Now suppose that the cell begins with X0 = k. Up to the first catastrophe, the family trees and
trigger clocks descending from the founders may be constructed independently, as described in
section 2.2.2.5. The chance that none of the k families has triggered rupture by time t is

                                                 Ik (t) = P(Xt ̸= H | X0 = k) = I(t)k ,


                                                                    97
whereas extinction of the whole population requires every family to die, giving Dk (t) = D(t)k .
Their difference is

                                           Ifix,k (t) = P(Xt ∈
                                                             / {0, H} | X0 = k) = I(t)k − D(t)k .                            (4.13)

Remark 4.9 (Non-fixation does not factorise). For µ > 0, the tempting identity Ifix,k = Ifix  k is

false. If one family dies while another remains productive, the cell has not fixed; these mixed
configurations are retained by I k − Dk but omitted by (I − D)k . Catastrophe is different from
the death of one lineage: the first trigger ruptures the cell and stops all lineages at once. Only
when µ = 0, so that D ≡ 0, do the two expressions coincide.
   Figure 4.7 makes the omitted configurations visible. For two founders the naive power lies
below the exact probability, and the gap is the probability that one lineage has died while the
other is still productive.

4.4               Moments
We now turn from the fates of the cell to the numbers it carries and releases. At first sight
their moments appear awkward. Since the catastrophe rate grows with the population, the
equation for each load moment calls for the moment above it. One would normally expect an
open hierarchy and, sooner or later, a closure approximation. The Riccati equation offers a way
around this.
                                                                                                             
Remark 4.10 (Why closed forms exist). Let J(t) = E(Xt ) and K(t) = E Xt2 . Their moment
equations begin
                                                                                                            
                                    J˙ = (λ − µ)J − δK,         K̇ = 2(λ − µ)K + (λ + µ)J − δE Xt3 ,

and the pattern continues. The hierarchy is genuinely open. However, the non-catastrophe
probability obeys the autonomous equation (4.5), while J = −I ′ /δ. Differentiating that scalar
equation produces K in terms of I and J, without introducing the third moment. Repeated
differentiation does the same at higher orders. Thus the open hierarchy remains present, but it

                                                                          98
need not be truncated; it can be approached from the closed equation for I. Proposition 4.17
later states the general result.
   The calculation has a natural order. We first recover the mean load from I, then use its
derivative to obtain the second moment. The release moments follow from balance equations in
which the same higher-order terms appear and can be eliminated.

4.4.1    The mean load
The link between I and the load is especially direct. Since H is entered from state n at rate δn,
the forward equation for the catastrophe state is
                              d                X
                                 P(Xt = H) = δ     n pn (t) = δJ(t).
                              dt               n≥1

Because I(t) = 1 − P(Xt = H),
                                             dI
                                                = −δJ.                                        (4.14)
                                             dt
The slope of the non-catastrophe curve is therefore the negative mean load, scaled by the per-unit
catastrophe rate. Differentiating (4.8), or substituting the Riccati equation into (4.14), gives the
closed form.

Theorem 4.11 (The mean load). The expected number of replicative units present at time t is

                                           (a − b)2 w    λ
                        J(t) = E(Xt ) =               = − (I − a)(I − b).                    (4.15)
                                          (B + Aw)2      δ

   The second expression will be the more useful one: it places J directly on the Riccati phase
line. From the initial load of one, the mean may first rise as productive paths grow, but it
eventually returns to zero as absorption becomes certain. This fall does not mean that the
remaining productive cells are empty. It means that there are progressively fewer of them, as
remark 4.3 made clear.
   To expose the second moment, consider the expected change of Xt over ∆t. A birth adds one
at rate λXt , a death removes one at rate µXt , and a catastrophe removes the whole numerical
load at rate δXt . Hence
                                                                      
                 E(Xt+∆t ) = E(Xt ) + E λ∆t Xt − µ∆t Xt − δ∆t Xt2 + o(∆t),

and therefore
                         dJ                                      
                             = (λ − µ)J − δK(t),      K(t) := E Xt2 .                 (4.16)
                         dt
The term −δK is the distinctive one. A large load is both costly when removed and more likely
to be removed, so its contribution is quadratic.

4.4.6                           The pair (Xt , Wt )
The two components have now been treated separately. Their joint behaviour has a useful
degeneracy: internal load and released load cannot be positive at the same time.

Corollary 4.15 (Exclusion, and the covariance). At every t, Xt Wt ≡ 0 almost surely under the
numerical-load convention of section 4.2.4. Before rupture Wt = 0, and after rupture the state is
H with numerical load zero. Hence E(Xt Wt ) = 0 and

                                                                  Cov(Xt , Wt ) = −J(t)V (t) ≤ 0.                                                                        (4.27)

   The sum Xt + Wt has a similarly plain interpretation. It counts particles currently held inside
the intact cell together with particles already released. It does not count particles lost through
internal death, and it is not a cumulative count of all births.

Corollary 4.16 (Current internal and released yield). The mean number of particles either still
inside or already released is
                                                                                                   λ−µ
                                                               E(Xt + Wt ) = 1 +                       [1 − I(t)].                                                       (4.28)
                                                                                                    δ
   Algebraically, (4.28) is a rearrangement of (4.22). Probabilistically, it describes the surviving
inventory of the process: the founder, together with the net contribution of births and deaths
accumulated before time t or rupture. This is the quantity whose late-time limit underlies V∞ .


4.5                           The I-economy
The formulae of the previous section share a curious economy: although the process evolves
in time, each moment can be written as a function of I alone. This is more than a change of
variable. It says that the stochastic dynamics move along a family of algebraic curves already
fixed by the Riccati equation.
   On the interval (b, a), the right-hand side of (4.5) is negative. Beginning at I(0) = 1, the
function I(t) therefore decreases strictly towards b, giving a bijection from [0, ∞) to (b, 1]. It
may be used as a clock, albeit one which runs backwards: once a quantity is known as a function


                                                                                       102
of I, its dependence on time is determined by the speed
                                       dI
                                          = λ(I − a)(I − b).
                                       dt
  The first two load moments suggest that the polynomial structure continues, and indeed it
does.
Proposition 4.17 (Every load moment is a polynomial in I). For each p ≥ 1, there is a
polynomial Qp such that                          
                                E(Xtp ) = Qp I(t) .
It has degree p + 1, satisfies Qp (a) = Qp (b) = 0, and has leading coefficient (−1)p p!(λ/δ)p .

Proof. Write mp (t) = E(Xtp ). Applying the generator associated with (4.1) to x 7→ xp gives
                                                                             
                      ṁp = −δmp+1 + a linear combination of m1 , . . . , mp .
The highest moment comes from catastrophe: Xtp is replaced by zero at rate δXt . Solving for
mp+1 expresses it in terms of ṁp and lower moments. Along the Riccati trajectory,
                                     d                   d
                                        = λ(I − a)(I − b) ,
                                     dt                  dI
so, if mp = Qp (I),
                   λ                       1                                       
           mp+1 = − (I − a)(I − b)Q′p (I) + a linear combination of Q1 , . . . , Qp .
                   δ                       δ
By induction, every term contains the factor (I − a)(I − b). The derivative term raises the degree
from p + 1 to p + 2 and multiplies the leading coefficient by −(λ/δ)(p + 1); the remaining terms
have degree at most p + 1. The base case
                                                 λ
                                Q1 (I) = J(I) = − (I − a)(I − b)
                                                 δ
has degree two and leading coefficient −λ/δ. The stated degree, roots and coefficient follow.

   Each new moment is therefore obtained by the same operation: differentiate the previous
polynomial along the Riccati flow, then add lower-order terms from births and deaths. The
cases p = 1 and p = 2 are precisely (4.15) and (4.20).
   The variance retains this algebraic dependence. Substituting (4.15) and (4.20) into (4.25)
gives
                                2λ2                          hλ                i2
                     Var(Xt ) = 2 (I − κ)(I − a)(I − b) − (I − a)(I − b) .                    (4.29)
                                 δ                            δ
This quartic has the common factor (I − a)(I − b); the two roots are generally simple, not double.
It also vanishes at I = 1, as it must because X0 = 1 is deterministic. Along the part of the
curve that has probabilistic meaning, I ∈ (b, 1], the variance is positive for t > 0 and tends back
to zero as I ↓ b. Values on the algebraic continuation from 1 towards a are not visited by the
process and need not admit a probabilistic interpretation.
   The release variance is likewise a polynomial in I: substitute (4.20) and (4.22) into (4.26). Its
expanded form is long but adds no new structure, so we leave it factored through the quantities
already defined.
   Figure 4.10 removes time from the horizontal axis. The curves are fixed algebraically, while the
Riccati equation determines how quickly they are traversed. This representation becomes useful
in Chapter 6, where the single-cell formulae act as age-dependent kernels in a renewal system.
Since those kernels are polynomials in one monotone coordinate, they can be manipulated
analytically. If a higher-order kernel is needed, proposition 4.17 supplies it by the same recursion
rather than by a fresh closure argument.

4.6     Burst size and burst time
The moments just derived already tell us something about the rupture event itself. There are
two quantities of interest: the burst size K = Xτ − , which is the load carried out of the cell, and
the burst time τ . Neither is a marginal moment of Xt alone, but both can be reached from the
functions now in hand.

4.6.1    Conditioning on rupture
The mean release V (t) includes paths which have died internally and will never release anything.
To obtain the mean load released by cells which have ruptured by time t, split the expectation
according to the event {τ ≤ t}:

                                        E(Wt ) = E(Wt | τ > t) I(t) + E(Wt | τ ≤ t) [1 − I(t)].

The first conditional expectation is zero because Wt = 0 before rupture. Hence

                                                                                 V (t)
                                                        E(Wt | τ ≤ t) =                 .
                                                                               1 − I(t)

Letting t → ∞ conditions on eventual rupture, irrespective of when it occurs.

Corollary 4.18 (The mean burst size). Conditional on eventual rupture,

                                                         V (t)     V∞   λ−µ    1     a

                                                   t→∞ 1 − I(t)   1−b    δ    1−b   a−1

When µ = 0, every realisation eventually ruptures and this conditional mean equals V∞ .

   The second moment follows in the same way. As t → ∞, we have I → b and K → 0, so (4.24)
gives

the variance.

4.6.2    The burst time
The distribution of τ is contained in I:
                                                   P(τ ≤ t) = P(Xt = H) = 1 − I(t).
Its density on finite times is therefore
                                              dI

The identity has a simple reading. Since any unit can trigger rupture, the instantaneous rupture
density is proportional to the current mean load.
  When µ > 0, this density is defective:
                                           Z ∞

                                               0                 t→∞

The missing mass b belongs to cells which die internally and consequently have τ = ∞. Only in
the no-death case is φ a proper density on [0, ∞). The full law of τ , and its joint law with K,
are derived in Chapter 5.

                                                                 105
4.6.3                The conditional mean and its plateau
There is a companion to the mean burst-size calculation. Although the unconditional load J(t)
tends to zero, the mean among cells which are still productive,

                                                                              J(t)
                                                                                      ,
                                                                             Ifix (t)

approaches the finite value a/(a − 1). This is the same value as the conditional mean burst size
in (4.30). The coincidence is suggestive: late-rupturing cells release the load they carry, and the
conditional load itself is approaching a plateau. It is not, by itself, proof that the two limiting
distributions agree. That stronger statement belongs to the quasi-stationary analysis [59, 46];
Chapter 5 establishes it by deriving the limiting conditional law.
   Figure 4.12 separates the no-death and death cases. Without death, conditioning on non-
catastrophe is the same as conditioning on productivity. With death, an intact cell may already
be empty, and the two normalisations no longer agree.

4.7                  Summary of results
For reference, table 4.1 collects the notation, table 4.2 the principal closed forms, and table 4.3 the
four differential equations from which they are obtained. Together with I(0) = J(0) = K(0) = 1
and V (0) = 0, these equations are enough to reconstruct the moment calculation.
    The roots also give a compact view of parameter dependence. Figure 4.13 shows b, the
probability of internal extinction before rupture, and V∞ , the mean release over all realisations,
across the (µ, δ) plane with λ fixed. Increasing the death rate makes internal extinction more
likely; increasing the catastrophe rate makes it less likely. Mean release is largest when catastrophe
is rare enough to permit growth, although internal death continues to compete with that growth.

4.8                        Conclusion
We have described the BDC process through its cell-fate probabilities and first two moments. The
resulting formulae cover the intracellular load, the released count, and the first two conditional
moments of the burst size, all through the single coordinate I(t).
   The calculation closes because the non-catastrophe probability obeys an autonomous Riccati
equation. The open moment hierarchy is still present, but it need not be truncated: it can be
approached from I. The two roots of the characteristic quadratic then make the closed forms
readable, and they retain their probabilistic meaning at the boundaries recorded in section 4.A.
   One reading takes rupture as the lysis of an infected macrophage. The formulae do not
depend on that reading. They do depend on the four assumptions of section 4.1: linear birth,
catastrophe hazard δn, instantaneous complete release, and constant rates. Measurement of
(λ, µ, δ) and the full burst law lie outside this chapter.

4.8.1                          Completing the single-cell theory
Chapter 5 solves the probability generating function of the process. This yields the state
probabilities pn (t), rather than only their sums I, D and Ifix , and identifies the quasi-stationary
distribution of the intracellular load conditioned on continued productivity. It also gives the full



                                                                                            108
burst-time and burst-size laws, including the defective finite-time mass
                                                       δ −k
                          Pk = P(K = k, τ < ∞) =         a ,      k ≥ 1,
                                                       λ
and the conditional laws of size given time and time given size. The several founder formulae
of section 4.3.6 then describe cells infected by more than one particle. Finally, the immediate-
transfer model chains successive cells together by allowing one burst to found the next infection
without delay.
   What passes from the present chapter to that analysis is deliberately limited. We know the first
two conditional burst moments, and they point towards a geometric distribution. The generating
function supplies the distribution itself and explains why it agrees with the quasi-stationary
load.

4.8.2    Embedding the cell in a population
Chapter 6 turns from one infected cell to many. A burst is no longer an endpoint: released
particles may infect fresh cells, each of which begins its own BDC trajectory. The quantities
derived here then become age-dependent kernels. The survival kernel Ifix (α) gives the probability
that a cell infected α time units ago remains productive, while

                                          g(α) = δK(α)

gives its expected rate of release. Because these functions can be written in the I-coordinate,
the resulting renewal equations retain much of the analytic structure of the single-cell model.
   The renewal formulation belongs to the classical theory of age-structured populations [60, 61].
It also makes clear what is hidden by the constant-release ordinary differential equations often
used in viral dynamics [12, 13]: release from a lytic cell has an age profile and occurs in a single
burst. In regimes where that timing matters, a constant-release approximation can be poor. The
comparison with continuous budding release is developed in the population chapter [27, 62, 63].


4.A      Checks and limiting cases
The main derivations assume positive birth and catastrophe rates and begin from one founder.
This appendix checks the endpoints of the closed forms and then approaches the boundaries at
which the two-root description degenerates.

4.A.1    Initial values
At t = 0, the process contains one unit and no event has occurred. Thus

         I(0) = 1,      D(0) = 0,      Ifix (0) = 1,    J(0) = K(0) = 1,       V (0) = 0.

Setting w = 1 recovers each value from the corresponding closed form. The initial slopes also
have a direct interpretation. Equation (4.14) gives

                                      I ′ (0) = −δJ(0) = −δ,

the rate at which the founder triggers rupture. Similarly,
                              ′
                             Ifix (0) = −(λ + µ + δ) + λ = −(µ + δ).

Birth is absent from this loss rate because division leaves the cell productive; only death or
rupture can remove it at the first instant.

                                                109
4.A.2     Late-time values
As t → ∞, w → ∞, and
                                                                                         aB
         I → b,       D → b,       Ifix → 0,     J → 0,       K → 0,        V → V∞ =        .
                                                                                          A
The equality of the first two limits is not accidental. At late time, a path which has avoided
rupture must have died internally; the productive probability is the vanishing difference between
those two events.

4.A.3     No death
Set µ = 0. From ab = µ/λ we have b = 0, while a = (λ + δ)/λ. Internal extinction is then
impossible: D ≡ 0 and Ifix = I. Every path ruptures eventually, and
                                               λ
                                    V∞ = 1 +     = E(K | burst) .
                                               δ
This is the simplest setting in which to check the geometric burst-size moments, as done in
section 4.6.1.

4.A.4     No catastrophe
Setting δ = 0 removes rupture and returns the classical linear birth–death process. Since the
main formulae contain δ in their denominators, we take the limit δ ↓ 0 in the supercritical regime
λ > µ. Then
                                     µ
                     a → 1,      b→ ,       A = a − 1 → 0,        I(t) → 1.
                                     λ
The internal-extinction probability approaches
                                         µ w−1
                               D(t) −→             ,       w = e(λ−µ)t ,
                                         λ w − µ/λ

which is the standard finite-time extinction probability of the linear birth–death process from one
founder. At (λ, µ, t) = (1, 0.3, 3), the limiting formula gives 0.27330340. Meanwhile V∞ → ∞:
for positive but increasingly small δ, a supercritical population can grow for an increasingly long
time before release.

4.A.5     Critical births and deaths
When λ = µ, Vieta’s relation gives ab = 1, and hence
                                                     1
                                                b=     .
                                                     a
At criticality, the probability of internal extinction before rupture equals the ratio of the geometric
burst-size law. For (λ, µ, δ) = (1, 1, 0.05), the two roots are a = 1.25 and b = 0.8. Away from
criticality the equality generally disappears.

4.A.6     No births
Finally, let λ ↓ 0. The upper root escapes to infinity and the quadratic Riccati equation becomes
linear:
                                dI
                                    = µ − (µ + δ)I,       I(0) = 1.
                                 dt


                                                 110
The remaining finite root tends to
                                                                µ
                                                        b −→       .
                                                               µ+δ
This is exactly the chance that a non-replicating founder dies before it triggers rupture: the two
exponential clocks divide the outcome in proportion to their rates. The two-root machinery has
degenerated, but its surviving root retains the correct probabilistic meaning.

4.A.7    The second moment of the release
At rupture from state n, Wt2 increases from zero to n2 , and this happens at rate δn. Therefore

                                               d  2       
                                                  E Wt = δE Xt3 .                                     (4.33)
                                               dt
The third moment has appeared, but the equation for K contains the same term. A unit change
in Xt does not produce a unit change in Xt2 .

                                                             111
Chapter 5

The birth–death–catastrophe process:
distribution theory, quasi-stationarity,
and burst statistics

5.1     Introduction
The standard models of within-host infection treat the infected cell as a black box. In the Basic
Model of Viral Replication, a cell that has been infected releases new particles at a constant rate
p until it is removed at a constant rate dI ; nothing about the cell’s interior enters the dynamics,
and the two constants are phenomenological — parameters to be fitted, not quantities to be
derived [12, 13, 11]. For a budding virus this is at least a defensible caricature. For a lytic
pathogen it is plainly wrong in mechanism, whatever it may achieve by fitting: such a pathogen
grows inside the cell, and releases nothing until the cell ruptures, at which point it releases
everything at once. Y. pestis in the macrophage, F. tularensis in the macrophage, the lytic
phage in its bacterium — all follow the intracellular replication-and-lysis picture, and all are
poorly served by a constant drip.
   Any replacement of that black box must begin with a complete description of a single infected
cell, and that description is the subject of this chapter. The intracellular model was set up in
the chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe process: the birth–death–catastrophe process, in
which each pathogen particle inside one cell divides at rate λ, dies at rate µ, and triggers the
rupture of the whole cell at rate δ. That chapter derived the principal single-cell quantities —
the no-rupture probability I(t), the non-fixation probability Ifix (t), the mean and second moment
J(t), K(t), and the cumulative mean release V (t) — each in closed form. What it did not derive
is the full distribution of the process, or the statistics of the rupture event itself.
   One question organises what follows. What is the complete law of a single bursting cell? The
answer is a single geometric family whose ratio slides towards a fixed endpoint, and the burst
inherits that endpoint. That inheritance is the chapter’s central identity, and it is not obvious:
one law comes from conditioning a surviving trajectory on never being absorbed, the other from
averaging over the instant each trajectory ruptures, and the two constructions share no step.
Section 5.6 resolves it. Two probes then ask how far the answer reaches: several founders sharing
one cell, and one cell’s burst founding the next. Both are stress tests rather than extensions,
and both mark a boundary.




                                               112
  The derivation proceeds as follows.
• The load is geometric at every time, with a ratio P (t) that slides from 0 to 1/a.
• The quasi-stationary law is that endpoint.
• The burst, averaged across rupture times, is the same law.
• Two probes — several founders sharing one cell, then one cell’s burst founding the next —
  test how far the structure reaches.
   The chapter is organised around that derivation. Section 5.2 recaps the chapter on the
birth–death–catastrophe process. Sections 5.3 and 5.4 produce the generating function and the
geometric family. Sections 5.5 and 5.6 take the endpoint and the identity. Sections 5.7 and 5.8
give the times and the three means. Sections 5.9 and 5.10 run the two probes. Section 5.11
returns to the constant drip, at the level where the objection was made: one cell.
   Two conventions for the rupture transition are current. The ruptured process may be sent
to state 0, which we call killing, or to a separate absorbing state H, which we call catastrophe.
This chapter works in the catastrophe convention throughout, and nothing is lost by doing
so: the two generating functions differ by a function of time alone, exactly and for every z, as
proposition 5.3 records.

5.1.1    Standing assumptions
Five assumptions are load-bearing, and all five are contestable.

1. Linear birth. Each particle divides at rate λ whatever the current load: there is no
   intracellular carrying capacity. Without it the Riccati equation (5.7) is not autonomous and
   no closed form survives.
2. Catastrophe hazard exactly proportional to load. Rupture is triggered at rate δn from
   load n — not δn2 , not a threshold at a critical load, not a maturation-timed lysis. This is the
   strongest assumption in the chapter and it is what makes everything solvable: the burst-size
   law of theorem 5.9 is an integral against the hazard δk, and its telescoping depends on that
   hazard being linear.
3. Instantaneous, complete release. The whole load leaves the cell at one instant, which is
   what makes the burst size a single random variable K rather than a release process.
4. Constant rates. No eclipse phase and no age-dependence within the cell, so that the process
   is time-homogeneous and the generating function depends on t only through w = eθt .
5. Independent lineages. Descendants of distinct particles evolve independently until the
   shared catastrophe clock rings. This is used decisively at Gk = Gk in (5.23) and throughout
   section 5.9.

   The first is revisited as an open problem in section 5.12.4; the second is what remark 5.11
isolates as the criterion behind the chapter’s central identity.

5.1.2    Contributions
Not all of this chapter is background. The mathematical introduction defines quasi-stationarity
and the Yaglom limit, takes existence from the literature, and computes the limiting conditional
mean, noting explicitly that convergence of moments does not establish convergence of the full
conditional law. It leaves open whether a quasi-stationary distribution survives when the process
is conditioned on neither extinction nor rupture. Five results are new to this chapter.


                                               113
1. The full conditional law exists in closed form for all µ ≥ 0 and is geometric with ratio 1/a
   (theorems 5.4 and 5.6).
2. Its mean was already known to equal the mean burst size. The two laws are identical, and
   the identity has a structural proof: size-biasing telescopes the occupation integral onto the
   endpoint of the process’s own geometric ratio (theorem 5.10).
3. The chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe process gives the marginal law of the burst
   time. Conditioned on the burst size, that law is a harmonic-number law with a Gumbel limit
   (proposition 5.12).
4. The single-founder release kernel was already in hand. For k founders sharing one cell the
   kernel gk is superlinear in k, while the lifetime yield is sub-extensive, (5.72) and (5.74).
5. Chained immediate transfer has an exact negative-binomial structure, (5.79).

   Everything else is standard, or is quoted from the chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe
process, and section 5.A marks which is which. One argument in the chapter is heuristic rather
than complete — the interchange of summation and integration in the hypergeometric machinery
of section 5.C.2 — and it is marked where it occurs.

5.1.3    What this chapter exports
The chapter is a supplier as well as an argument. The chapter on population dynamics needs
five things from here: Ifix (a) and g(a) = δK(a) as the survival and release kernels of a renewal
model; gk as the kernel for a cell founded at multiplicity k; the mean productive lifetime
                                                                                         
                                                                                            E[Tprod ]
together with the matched constant-rate death rate dI = 0.351 it implies; and E Wt2 , for the
between-well variance that a limiting-dilution assay measures. Each is derived below and flagged
where it is derived.
   One parameter set recurs. Unless stated otherwise, illustrations use (λ, µ, δ) = (1, 0.2, 0.05),
for which a = 1.0616, so b = 0.1884 and E[K | burst] = 17.23: most cells burst, and the bursts
are large. Those rates sit on the F. tularensis side of the model’s range, where a = 1.001, rather
than the B. anthracis side, where a = 2.66, with the numbers compressed for legibility. The
figures may therefore be read as a rescaled lytic cell rather than an arbitrary choice.


5.2     The process, its roots, and its closed forms
The birth–death–catastrophe (BDC) process was defined and solved in the chapter on the
birth–death–catastrophe process. Four things are needed from it here: the notation, the process
and its release counter, the roots, and the identity relating them. Section 5.A collects the closed
forms.

Table 5.1 fixes the notation, taken unchanged from the chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe
process. Every symbol appearing in more than one section is listed; those local to a single
derivation are introduced where they occur.

Definition 5.1 (Birth–death–catastrophe process). The load Xt is a continuous-time Markov
chain on {0, 1, 2, . . .} ∪ {H} which, from any state n ≥ 1, jumps

                           n→n+1                                at rate λn          (birth),
                           n→n−1                                at rate µn          (death),
                           n→H                                  at rate δn (catastrophe).

Both 0 and H are absorbing: the population either clears itself internally, or the cell ruptures.
Unless stated otherwise X0 = 1.

   The classical treatments [21, 57, 58] allow immigration and distributed catastrophe sizes; this
specialisation is the one with the closed-form family we need. Rupture is what matters, so the
release count runs alongside the load: with τ the catastrophe time,
                                                       (
                                                           0,     t < τ,
                                                Wt =                                                             (5.1)
                                                           Xτ − , t ≥ τ,
so that at rupture the whole pre-burst load is dumped and then held. Figure 5.1 shows one path
of the pair; every single-cell release quantity below is a functional of (Xt , Wt ).

5.3     The generating function
Everything in this chapter is read off one object. The PGF of the load,
                                        X
                            G(z, t) =         pn (t)z n ,     pn (t) = P{Xt = n} ,                     (5.14)
                                        n≥0

packs the entire state distribution into a single function of two variables and satisfies a first-order
partial differential equation that can be solved exactly. We derive that equation, solve it, and
record what the two rupture conventions do to the answer.
   One feature of the catastrophe convention should be named before it is used. Ruptured
realisations are absorbed into the separate state H and no longer contribute to the count, so
G(1, t) = I(t) ≤ 1: the PGF is defective, and its mass decays as cells burst. Under killing the
rupture transition lands in state 0 and is counted, so G(1, t) = 1 for all t.

5.3.2    Solving by characteristics
(5.18) is first order and linear, so it reduces to a family of ordinary differential equations along
characteristic curves. The generic construction — the change of variables (z, t) → (s, ξ), the
admissible initial curves, and the transport equations dG                 dt
                                                             dξ = 0 and dξ = −1 — is set out in
section 2.3.3, with the generic system at (2.80); we take it from there and record only what is
specific to this process. With the initial curve z(s, 0) = s, t(s, 0) = 0, transport gives t = −ξ
and G constant on curves of fixed s, so that the initial condition G(z, 0) = z — a single founder,
p1 (0) = 1 — fixes G(s, ξ) = s.
    That leaves one equation, and it is the one that is not generic:

                                                 118
5.3.3    Several founders
The solution extends to any initial load by the branching property. If the cell begins with k
independent founders, the k lineages evolve independently until the shared catastrophe clock
rings and the total count is their sum, so the PGF is the kth power of the single-founder one:
                                                             k
                                        Gk (z, t) = G(z, t) .                                 (5.23)
Under catastrophe this is defective, with Gk (1, t) = I(t)k ; under killing it remains proper. (5.23)
is what sections 5.9 and 5.10 are built on, and it is where the independence assumption of
section 5.1.1 does its work.

The killing convention needs no second derivation. Its PDE (5.17) carries the inhomogeneous
term δJ(t), which is − dI
                       dt ; along a characteristic that term integrates to I alone, with no z in it.
The conclusion is exact and immediate.
Proposition 5.3 (The two rupture conventions). For every z and every t,

   Three consequences follow in a line each. The state probabilities agree, pkill     cat
                                                                                n = pn for every
                                                         kill   cat
n ≥ 1. The difference is confined to the empty state, p0 = p0 + (1 − I). Every moment of Xt
restricted to n ≥ 1 is therefore convention-independent, so nothing in the rest of this chapter
would change had killing been chosen instead. One reason to prefer catastrophe nonetheless
survives the equivalence: it keeps G(1, t) = I(t), so that the defect of the generating function is
itself the biological observable, the probability that the cell has not yet ruptured. That is why
the convention was chosen, and why the parallel track need not be carried further.
   With the convention settled, the generating function can be read for what it contains.
Extracting its coefficients is mechanical; what comes out of them is not.


5.4     The load is geometric at every time
The generating function (5.22) is a ratio of two linear functions of z, and that fact alone decides
the shape of the whole distribution. Extracting the coefficients returns not an unstructured
sequence but a geometric law, at every time, with a ratio that moves. Naming that ratio is the
single most useful thing this chapter does, because every result after this section is a statement
about where it goes.
                                                                                         1 n
   Coefficients come out of a PGF by repeated differentiation at the origin, pn = n!      ∂z G|z=0 ;
the construction is given in section 2.D at (2.132). Applied to (5.22), the derivatives close on
themselves,
                          ∂nG               2         (1 − w)n−1
                                = n! (a − b)  w                    n+1 ,                    (5.26)
                          ∂z n                  b − aw − (1 − w)z

so that setting z = 0 and dividing by n! gives, for n ≥ 1,
                                                     2             n−1
                                              a−b             1−w
