                                             b − aw          b − aw

The empty state is the only one that sees the convention. Under catastrophe p0 = G(0, t) =
ab(1 − w)/(b − aw), the probability of reaching 0 by the death of every individual; under killing
it carries the extra 1 − I(t) of proposition 5.3, and nothing else changes.

5.4.1    The sliding ratio
There is more structure in (5.27) than it first shows. The entire dependence on n sits in one
factor. Write
                                          1−w
                                P (t) :=         ,    w = eθt ,                         (5.28)
                                         b − aw
and the state probabilities become pn (t) = p1 (t)P (t)n−1 .

Theorem 5.4 (The load is geometric at every time). For every t > 0, conditional on the load
being non-zero, Xt is geometrically distributed on {1, 2, . . .} with ratio P (t):

                                  pn (t) = p1 (t) P (t)n−1 ,     n ≥ 1.                    (5.29)

The ratio increases monotonically in t, from
                                                                       1
                                  P (0) = 0        to        P (∞) =     .                 (5.30)
                                                                       a
Proof. Equation (5.29) is (5.27) read with the n-dependent factor named. For the limits,
w = 1 at t = 0 gives P (0) = 0, and w → ∞ gives P → 1/a; monotonicity follows from
P ′ (t) = λ(a − b)2 w/(b − aw)2 > 0, since b − aw never vanishes for t ≥ 0.

   What P is, is worth saying plainly. It is the price in probability of one more particle:
pn+1 /pn = P (t) for every n ≥ 1, so each additional unit of load is the same factor less likely
than the one below it, whatever that load happens to be. At founding P = 0 and the cell holds
its single particle with certainty. Thereafter P rises, the geometric flattens, and the mean load
conditional on non-fixation, 1/(1 − P ), rises with it — from 1 at founding towards a/(a − 1).
That the rise never reverses is what theorem 5.4 establishes, and it is what makes the endpoint
well defined: a cell that has survived longer is spread over more states, never fewer.
   So the transient laws form a one-parameter family of geometrics sliding towards a fixed limit,
and P is the only thing in them that moves. Figure 5.3 shows the slide: on a logarithmic scale
each law is a straight line, and the lines rotate onto a limiting slope rather than deforming.
The limit 1/a is where the next three sections live — section 5.5 takes it, section 5.6 integrates
against it, section 5.7 conditions on it. Figure 5.2 is that sentence drawn.

One consequence is immediate, and it checks the closed forms of section 5.2 against a derivation
that shares nothing with them.

  The sum of the positive state probabilities is exactly the probability of being neither ruptured
nor extinct. The chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe process reached the same Ifix by
decomposing absorption rather than by summing a distribution; that two such different routes
agree is a check on both.


5.5     The quasi-stationary distribution
The BDC process is absorbed in the end, either at the rupture state H or by death at 0; the
theory of what a process looks like before that happens is quasi-stationarity [64]. A realisation
which has, by chance, not yet reached fixation has had to hold a middle course. Fly too low and
depletion to zero becomes more and more probable; fly high, a population booming in number,
and the catastrophe clock has all the more to ring against. What survives hovers, on Daedalus’s
instruction exactly: neither so low that the spray soaks the wings, nor so high that the sun
reaches them.
   A rogue process still going in defiance of the odds is described by the quasi-stationary
distribution: a set of limiting conditional probabilities, each qualified by non-fixation. The
general definition, the Yaglom limit and the classical existence theory are in definition 2.7 and
section 2.3.2.6, resting on the classification of birth–death processes [56]. Here the limit is quoted
from nowhere: it is read off theorem 5.4 by letting P reach its endpoint.

5.5.1    Conditioning on survival
Let F (t) be the probability of fixation by time t, whether by death or by collapse. The
decomposition of section 5.2.4 settles it immediately: a realisation is fixed by t precisely when it
is not productively infected, so
                                F (t) = 1 − Ifix (t) = 1 − I(t) + D(t),                        (5.32)
which reduces to 1 − I(t) when µ = 0. Conditional state probabilities decompose as
          P{Xt = i} = P{Xt = i | fixation} F (t) + P{Xt = i | non-fixation} (1 − F (t)) ,
and for i ∈
          / {0, H} the process has not been absorbed, so the first term vanishes and
                                                          pi (t)     pi (t)
                          P{Xt = i | non-fixation} =              =          .                 (5.33)
                                                        1 − F (t)   Ifix (t)
The quasi-stationary distribution is the limiting form of these:

                   qi := lim P{Xt = i | Xt ∈
                                           / {0, H}} = lim              ,        i ≥ 1.        (5.34)
                         t→∞                               t→∞ Ifix (t)

(1, 0.2, 0.05) it is 13.99 against ⟨X⟩QS = 17.23, because V∞ averages in the cells that cleared
internally and released nothing. Figure 5.4 traces all three across µ. That the first two agree is
not a coincidence of first moments: section 5.6 shows the entire burst-size distribution is the
quasi-stationary distribution.

5.5.3    The decay rate, and what it validates
Quasi-stationarity has a spectral reading, and carrying it out closes a question left open in
the mathematical introduction. Restricting the generator to the positive states gives the killed
subgenerator Q, with Qi,i+1 = λi, Qi,i−1 = µi for i ≥ 2, and Qi,i = −(λ + µ + δ)i; its missing
row mass is the instantaneous absorption rate, δi for i ≥ 2 and µ + δ for i = 1. Writing Tfix for
the first of internal extinction and rupture and summing the positive-state forward equations,
                                             d                              X
                                                P{Tfix > t} = −µP{Xt = 1} −     δi P{Xt = i} ,                                     (5.38)
                                             dt                             i≥1


                                                                            123
all probabilities on the right understood before absorption. Ordinary death drains survival mass
from state 1 only; catastrophe drains it from every positive state.

Proposition 5.7 (Quasi-stationary decay rate). If ν is a quasi-stationary distribution on the
positive states and the sums below are finite, then for some ϑ > 0
                                                                                   X
                  νQ = −ϑν,            Pν {Tfix > t} = e−ϑt ,          ϑ = µν1 +         δi νi .   (5.39)
                                                                                   i≥1

Proof. Quasi-stationarity writes the unnormalised positive-state law at time t as α(t)ν; the semi-
group property forces α(s + t) = α(s)α(t), and right-continuity gives α(t) = e−ϑt . Differentiating
at zero yields the left-eigenvector relation, and summing its coordinates against (5.38) identifies
the decay rate.

   The state-dependence matters. Were the catastrophe rate a constant, independent of the
load, its clock would run independently of the birth–death process, the killed semigroup would
factorise into that clock’s exponential times its birth–death counterpart, and the extra clock
would shift the decay rate by that constant while leaving the conditional law untouched. A rate
proportional to the load weights paths by their accumulated exposure and generally changes
the quasi-stationary law itself. It is therefore no formality that a quasi-stationary distribution
survives conditioning on neither extinction nor rupture; the mathematical introduction leaves
that open, and theorem 5.6 settles it in closed form.
   Substituting that law, νn = (a − 1)a−n , into (5.39) gives a check on everything together.
                       P
Using ν1 = (a − 1)/a, i iνi = a/(a − 1), and then µ = λab and δ = λ(a − 1)(1 − b) from (5.4),
      a−1        a            a−1                      a
ϑ=µ          +δ       = λab        + λ(a − 1)(1 − b)       = λb(a − 1) + λa(1 − b) = λ(a − b) = θ.
         a      a−1            a                     a−1
                                                                                            (5.40)
The eigenvalue of the killed subgenerator is the time constant that has appeared in every formula
since (5.3). One line validates the quasi-stationary law, the decay-rate formula and AB = δ/λ
together.

   When µ = 0 this is λ−1 log(1+λ/δ). The form of the answer interprets itself. Here log⟨X⟩QS /λ
is the time that exponential growth at rate λ would take to reach the quasi-stationary level —
the productive lifetime is precisely the time needed to climb to the height at which the process
can hover. Figure 5.5 maps it across the rate plane; at the working parameters E[Tprod ] = 2.847,
implying a matched constant-rate death rate dI = 0.351.
   That is the cell while it lasts. What it releases when it stops is a separate question, and the
law answering it has already appeared in this section.


5.6                 Burst size, and why it is the quasi-stationary law
A cell presents two quantities to the outside world: when it ruptures, and how much it releases.
The second is settled first, because it is the law the previous section has just computed.

5.6.1                The burst-size law
The burst size K is the load immediately before rupture. For a burst in state k the process must
be at k and then rupture from there, which happens at rate δk, so
                                                                                                                                     Z ∞
                                                              P{K = k} = δk                                                                    pk (t) dt.                                                     (5.43)
                                                                                                                                     0



                                                                                                      125
A discretisation makes this plain: over an interval of width ∆t in which the process is at k,
the probability that the burst occurs there is P{Xt = k} δk ∆t, and summing over intervals as
∆t → 0 gives (5.43).
   The integral is elementary once the sliding ratio is in view. With P as in (5.28),
                                                                     2
                 d h P (t)k i 1                               a−b

as one verifies from P ′ (t) = λ(a − b)2 w/(b − aw)2 . Since P (0) = 0 and P (∞) = 1/a,
                                         Z ∞

and (5.43) gives the law.

                                          δ

5.6.2    Why the two laws agree
Set (5.47) beside (5.35). They are the same law.
Theorem 5.10 (The burst size is the quasi-stationary law). The burst-size distribution condi-
tioned on bursting is exactly the quasi-stationary distribution of the load: both are geometric on
{1, 2, . . .} with ratio 1/a.
   That the algebra agrees is not by itself an explanation, because the two constructions share no
step. On one side, condition the load on having avoided both absorbing states and let t → ∞: a
limit along a single trajectory, in which nothing is averaged and no rupture occurs. On the other,
weight each state by its rupture hazard δk and integrate over all rupture times: an average across
trajectories, in which every realisation contributes at the instant it dies and never afterwards.
Nothing forces a conditional limit and an occupation integral to agree, and for a killed process
whose hazard was not linear in the load they would not. Figure 5.6 sets the two side by side.

                                                     126
Proof. Read (5.44) as a change of clock rather than as an antiderivative. Its k = 1 case is
                                                        P ′ (t)
                                             p1 (t) =           ,                              (5.48)
                                                          λ
and with σ = P (t), which runs monotonically from 0 to 1/a by theorem 5.4, the occupation
density of state k becomes a perfect differential:
                                                       1 k−1
                                        pk (t) dt =      σ   dσ.                               (5.49)
                                                       λ
Hence                           Z ∞               Z 1/a
                                              δ                         δ h k i1/a  δ
                P{K = k} = δk         pk dt =             kσ k−1 dσ =      σ       = a−k ,     (5.50)
                                 0            λ    0                    λ      0    λ
which normalised on the bursting event is (a − 1)a−k , the law of (5.35).

   The proof says more than the statement. The size-bias weight k is exactly the Jacobian
that turns the occupation density of state k into a perfect differential, and integrating a perfect
differential returns only its endpoints. The burst-size law therefore depends on the whole
trajectory only through the endpoint σ∞ = 1/a that P reaches, which is by definition the
quasi-stationary ratio. That is why the two laws coincide: not a coincidence, and not a Yaglom
limit, but size-biasing telescoping the time integral onto the terminal value of P , the process’s
own geometric ratio.
Remark 5.11 (What the argument needs). Nothing in the proof used the birth–death structure.
The identity holds for any killed process whose transient conditional law is geometric in a single
sliding ratio P (t) satisfying p1 = P ′ /λ, with a killing hazard linear in the load. Whether that
criterion characterises the class of processes for which burst size equals the quasi-stationary law
is not settled here; see section 5.12.4.


5.6.3    Size-biasing and the size of a late burst
The size-biasing that drives the telescoping has a reading of its own: it is what an experiment
sees when it times its observations. A cell bursts at rate δXt , so given a burst at time t its size
is a size-biased draw from p· (t):
                                k pk (t)                            K(t)     2λ
           P{K = k | τ = t} =            ,      E[K | τ = t] =           =1+    (1 − I(t)) .   (5.51)
                                 J(t)                               J(t)      δ
Letting t → ∞,
                                                  2λ           ⟨X 2 ⟩QS   a+1
                      E[K | τ = t] −−−→ 1 +          (1 − b) =          =     ,                (5.52)
                                     t→∞           δ            ⟨X⟩QS     a−1
the second equality from (5.37). A late burst is therefore larger than an average one by the
factor
                                     (a + 1)/(a − 1)    a+1
                                                      =       ,
                                        a/(a − 1)          a
which is 1.94 at the working parameters but only 1.38 at the B. anthracis parameters of
section 5.12.2: the often-quoted factor of two is the a → 1 limit, not a general fact. Figure 5.7
shows the rise.
   The mechanism is a selection effect on the geometric law, not on individual cells. Conditioning
on a late rupture makes no cell grow; it discards the cells that ruptured early, and early ruptures
come disproportionately from small loads, the hazard δk being proportional to the load. What
survives is a sample already weighted towards large loads, which (5.51) then weights once more
at the moment of rupture. Figure 5.8 makes the tilt visible.
   The size of a burst is now fully described. Its timing is not, and the two are not independent
— which is what makes the remaining conditioning worth doing.

5.7     Burst time
Knowing what a burst is makes it possible to say when one happens. The marginal law of the
rupture time is a one-line consequence of the survival function derived in the chapter on the
birth–death–catastrophe process, and is taken first. The conditional law is the interesting one,
and it runs the other way from section 5.6.3: there the burst size was conditioned on the burst
time, here the burst time on the burst size, which is what an experiment measures when it
records both. By theorem 5.10 that size is a draw from the law at the endpoint of P , the last of
the three readings of that endpoint promised in section 5.4.

5.7.1   The marginal law, and its defect
Since I(t) = P{τ > t}, the density of the burst time is

                                                  φ(t) := −I ′ (t) = δJ(t),                                                                    (5.53)

using I ′ = −δJ from section 5.2.4: the rupture flux at time t is the catastrophe rate times the
expected load. The density is defective, because I(t) → b rather than 0:
                                                      Z ∞
                                                            φ(t) dt = 1 − b,                                                                   (5.54)
                                                      0

the remaining mass b sitting at τ = ∞ — the realisations that clear internally without ever
bursting. Conditioned on eventual burst the density is φ(t)/(1 − b). Figure 5.9 shows both, with
the missing mass marked.

5.7.2   Burst time given burst size, without death
When µ = 0 the conditional law of τ given K is completely explicit. Write θ = λ + δ for the rate
                                                                           P
combination governing this case — it is λ(a − b) at b = 0 — and Hn = nj=1 1/j for the nth

the conditioned rupture time converges to the standard Gumbel law. Burst sizes select burst
times from a travelling window of width of order 1/θ around (log n)/θ, and the window does not
widen as n grows.

5.7.3   The mean burst time when death is possible
When µ > 0 the burst time has no finite mean, because the mass b at τ = ∞ makes the integral
diverge. Conditioned on bursting it is finite and explicit.
Proposition 5.13 (Mean burst time conditioned on bursting). For µ > 0,

                                                                    of proposition 5.8, and the
distinction should be kept visible: E[τ | burst] averages the rupture time over the cells that
burst, while E[Tprod ] averages the time spent productive over all cells, the internally cleared
ones included with their shorter productive spans. When µ = 0 the two coincide, both reducing
to λ−1 log(1 + λ/δ); that coincidence supports less than it appears to, and section 5.8 shows
what goes wrong when it is leaned on.


5.8       Conditional burst means
Three different averages of the burst size appear in this chapter. They differ by factors of nearly
two and a half, and they are easy to confuse, because in ordinary usage all three are written
E[K | ·]. This section separates them and quantifies the difference; the results are collected in
table 5.2 and plotted in figure 5.11, and the chapter on population dynamics draws on all three.

5.8.1     The overall expected burst size
Whether a catastrophe has occurred by t is recorded by the truth of Wt > 0, the minimum
burst size being 1; that event has probability 1 − I(t). Decomposing E[Wt ] on it, and using
E[Wt | Wt = 0] = 0,
                                                     V (t)
                                 E[Wt | Wt > 0] =           .                          (5.61)
                                                   1 − I(t)
This is the mean burst size given that a burst has happened by t. The expected size of an
arbitrarily generated burst is its late-time value,

                                   V (t)        V∞   λ−µ    1     a
                    E[K] = lim             =       =     +     =     ,                      (5.62)
                            t→∞ 1 − I(t)       1−b    δ    1−b   a−1

the last two forms from (5.12). The distinction from V∞ itself matters: V∞ averages in the
realisations that cleared internally and released nothing, so it is not the mean burst size unless
µ = 0.

5.8.2     The apparent circularity
What is the expected burst size of a cell that has not yet burst? Decomposing E[K] on the event
{τ > t},

                      E[K] = E[K | τ > t] P{τ > t} + E[K | τ < t] P{τ < t}
                                                   V (t)
                           = E[K | τ > t] I(t) +          (1 − I(t)) ,                      (5.63)
                                                 1 − I(t)

so that
                                        E[K] − V (t)   V∞ /(1 − b) − V (t)
                       E[K | τ > t] =                =                     .                (5.64)
                                           I(t)               I(t)
Letting t → ∞ in (5.64) appears to give

                                                   V∞ /(1 − b) − V∞
                              lim E[K | τ > t] =                    ,
                             t→∞                           b
which after a little algebra returns E[K] itself. That cannot be right. Conditioning on τ > t as
t → ∞ selects the realisations that never burst at all, and their burst size is zero, not typical.


                                                132
      Quantity                   Definition                                         At (1, 0.2, 0.05)

Table 5.2: Three means, three conditionings. The first two differ by the factor 1 − b, the
probability of eventual rupture; the second and third by the size-biasing factor (a + 1)/a of
section 5.6.3.


  The resolution is that (5.64) mixes two conditionings. Its numerator E[K] − V (t) is the
contribution to the burst size from realisations that burst after t; its denominator I(t) is the
probability of not having burst by t, which includes the mass b of realisations that never will
and whose burst size is zero. Written consistently,

                                                  V∞ − V (t)      V∞ − V∞
                      E[K 1{burst} | τ > t] =                −−−→         = 0,                     (5.65)
                                                    I(t)      t→∞    b

which is correct and unmysterious. The genuinely interesting limit conditions on eventual burst
as well:
                                                          V∞ − V (t)
                        E[K | τ > t, burst eventually] =             ,                   (5.66)

of the form 0/0 as t → ∞. By l’Hôpital’s rule, with V ′ = δK and I ′ = −δJ,

                                                       K(t)   ⟨X 2 ⟩QS     2λ
           lim E[K | τ > t, burst eventually] = lim         =          =1+    (1 − b),             (5.67)
        t→∞                                        t→∞ J(t)    ⟨X⟩QS        δ

in agreement with (5.52): the eventual burst size of a very patient cell is the size-biased
quasi-stationary mean.
   No interchange of limits is needed to reach it. The defect cancels in the ratio, so K/J is at
every time the ratio of the second conditional moment to the first, and by (5.36) that conditional
law is geometric with ratio P (t); hence

                                              K(t)   1 + P (t)
                                                   =           ,                                   (5.68)
                                              J(t)   1 − P (t)

an identity holding at every t, not only in the limit. (5.67) is the value of (5.68) at the endpoint
P = 1/a, and the only limit taken anywhere in the argument is P (t) ↑ 1/a from theorem 5.4.
Remark 5.14 (Where the circularity was). The circularity was never in the mathematics; it was
in the conditioning. Each of the three quantities in table 5.2 is a correct answer to a different
question, and only the middle one is what is usually meant by “the mean burst size”. An assay
that observes a cell because it ruptured measures the second; an assay that observes cells still
infected at a late time and waits measures the third.
   Three means, and with them the cell founded by one particle is completely described. The
two sections that follow ask how much of that survives when the founder is not alone, and when
the cell is not.


5.9        Several founders
The single-cell law of sections 5.4 and 5.6 was derived from one founder. The first probe of how
far it reaches asks what happens when there are several.

                                40

              mean burst size

                                20

                                10
                                                                          V(t) → V∞ = 13.99
                                 5                                         [K | burst] = 17.23
                                                                           [K | τ > t, burst] → 33.46
                                 0
                                     0   5   10     15      20       25       30         35             40

Figure 5.11: Three distinct averages at (λ, µ, δ) = (1, 0.2, 0.05), drawn as trajectories. The
unconditional mean release V (t) approaches V∞ = 13.99 and includes cells that never burst; the
burst mean V∞ /(1 − b) = a/(a − 1) = 17.23 is the horizontal line; conditioning on a very late
eventual burst approaches the size-biased limit (a + 1)/(a − 1) = 33.46. Keeping the conditioning
explicit removes the apparent circularity.

   In applications a single founder is not always the right picture: a macrophage may be invaded
by several bacteria at once, and in the laboratory the multiplicity of infection is a control
parameter. Nothing essential changes — the process is still linear and Gk = Gk still holds — but
the bookkeeping does, and two plausible guesses turn out to be wrong. Throughout, k founders
share one cell and hence one catastrophe clock: rupture occurs at rate δ times the total load,
and dumps the whole cell.

5.9.1    Fixation functions for k founders
By the branching property the k founder lineages evolve independently until the shared clock
rings. No burst occurs by t precisely when no lineage has burst; internal extinction occurs
precisely when every lineage has cleared internally. Hence
                          Ik (t) = I(t)k ,   Dk (t) = D(t)k ,      Ifix,k (t) = I(t)k − D(t)k .              (5.69)
Remark 5.15 (A guess that fails). The tempting guess is Ifix,k = Ifix
                                                                    k : each lineage stays productive

with probability Ifix , independently. But “productive” for the whole cell means not fixed, and
the intersection of the independent events “lineage i avoids bursting and does not go extinct” is
not the event “the cell is not fixed”: the cell also stops being productive when every lineage dies
out. Correctly, Ifix,k = I k − Dk , whereas Ifix
                                               k = (I − D)k ; already for k = 2 the two differ by
 2     2            2
I − D − (I − D) = 2D(I − D) ̸= 0 whenever µ > 0. Numerically, at (λ, µ, δ) = (1, 0.2, 0.05)
and t = 1: the correct Ifix,2 = I 2 − D2 ≈ 0.8458, while Ifix2 ≈ 0.6542. When µ = 0, D ≡ 0 and

the two coincide — again the invisible case.

5.9.2    Moments and the release kernel
The moments fall out of the k-founder PGF of section 5.3.3, Gk = Gk , where G is the single-
founder defective PGF. Differentiating at z = 1, with G(1, t) = I(t), ∂z G|z=1 = J(t) and
∂z2 G|z=1 = K(t) − J(t),
                                   Jk (t) = k I(t)k−1 J(t),                           (5.70)

                                                         134
and, from the second factorial moment,

             Kk (t) = ∂z2 Gk z=1 + ∂z Gk z=1
                   = k(k − 1)I(t)k−2 J(t)2 + kI(t)k−1 (K(t) − J(t)) + kI(t)k−1 J(t)
                   = k K(t)I(t)k−1 + k(k − 1)J(t)2 I(t)k−2 .                                        (5.71)

Multiplying by the catastrophe rate turns Kk into the expected release flux — the second item
of the consignment of section 5.1.3, and the kernel on which the renewal model of the chapter
on population dynamics is built:
                                                                                    
                  gk (t) = δKk (t) = δ k K(t)I(t)k−1 + k(k − 1)J(t)2 I(t)k−2 .                      (5.72)

Figure 5.13(a) plots gk against age for k = 1, . . . , 4. The ordering in k is superlinear early and
reverses late, so the enhancement is a property of the cell’s age rather than a fixed multiplier.
                                                                                             
Remark 5.16 (The free-sum trap). It is tempting to write gk = δ kK + k(k − 1)J 2 , the second
moment of a free sum of k independent copies. That form omits the powers of I: the second-
moment contribution of a configuration is counted only while the shared clock has not rung.
At (λ, µ, δ) = (1, 0.2, 0.05), t = 1, k = 2: the free-sum form gives K2 ≈ 23.39, while (5.71)
gives K2 ≈ 22.264, which agrees with a direct master-equation computation. The difference
is the superlinearity of gk in k wearing a disguise: even the free-sum form is superlinear, but
the true kernel is superlinear and tempered by survival. That a cell’s expected release flux
depends superlinearly on its founding multiplicity is a genuine prediction of the model — one
that classical constant-p models cannot express, and that low-multiplicity single-cell assays can
test.

bigger. The two statements are consistent because the first is unconditional and the second is
not, which is table 5.2’s distinction appearing again in a new place.
   The geometric structure of theorem 5.4 survives all of this untouched, the load in a multiply
infected cell being a sum of independent copies of the single-founder load. Multiplicity changes
the bookkeeping, not the shape; section 5.10 finds the boundary the other way.


5.10                       Chained immediate transfer
The second probe couples cells together in the most austere way available. Take M host cells,
all empty but one, which holds a single particle. Each runs the µ = 0 birth–catastrophe process
of the previous sections, and on rupture the released load does not enter an extracellular pool:

                                                                                                   136
it passes immediately and wholesale into a fresh cell, which begins the process again with that
load as its founding census, and so on until every cell has ruptured. There is no extracellular
phase, no clearance and no re-infection, only a chain of bursts; and since µ = 0, all M ruptures
occur almost surely.
   Serial rupture of this kind is not a fiction: the chapter on bursting and budding pathogenesis
gives the biological account, with its Brownian-drift and macrophage-motility caveats. Here it is
a tractable laboratory for multi-cell burst statistics, interpolating between the single-cell theory
above and the populations of the chapter on population dynamics.
   Let ri be the load at the ith rupture, ti its time, and Ti = ti − ti−1 the ith inter-rupture
interval, with t0 = 0. Write
                                      δ                   λ
                               s=        ,         ρ=        = 1 − s.                            (5.76)
                                     λ+δ                 λ+δ

5.10.1    The key observation: event types ignore the load
When the load is n, the next event is a birth with probability λn/(λn + δn) = ρ and a
catastrophe with probability s, independent of n; only the holding time depends on the load,
being Exp ((λ + δ)n). The sequence of event types is therefore independent coin flips, and the
number of births G before the catastrophe in a cell founded with any load is

                    G ∼ Geom0 (s),          P{G = g} = s ρg ,        g = 0, 1, 2, . . . ,        (5.77)

the number of failures before the first success. Each cell in the chain contributes an independent
copy Gi , since the process restarts afresh at every transfer, and the rupture size of cell i is its
founding load plus its own births. The first cell being founded by a single particle,

                                        rk = 1 + G1 + · · · + Gk ,                               (5.78)

with G1 , . . . , Gk independent and identically distributed. Everything about the rupture sizes
follows from this decomposition, and something has quietly happened in reaching it: the load,
the whole subject of section 5.4, has dropped out of the argument.

5.10.2    Rupture-size laws
A sum of k independent geometric variables is negative binomial, so (5.78) gives immediately
                                                                             !
                              (n)                     (n)       n+k−2
                 P{rk = n} = Ak sk ρ n−1 ,           Ak =             ,            n ≥ 1.        (5.79)
                                                                 k−1

The first few cases show the pattern:

                                          P{r1 = n} = sρn−1 ,                                    (5.80)

geometric, matching the one-cell law of (5.46) at µ = 0, and then

  P{r2 = n} = n s2 ρn−1 ,     P{r3 = n} = n(n+1)
                                             2   s3 ρn−1 ,           P{r4 = n} = n(n+1)(n+2)
                                                                                      6      s4 ρn−1 .
                                                                                                  (5.81)
Equivalently the coefficients satisfy
                                      Xn
                                  (n)      (i)                (i)
                                 Ak =     Ak−1 ,            A1 = 1,                              (5.82)
                                      i=1
                                                                         P
which is (5.79) conditioned on the value of rk−1 : P{rk = n} = i P{rk−1 = i} P{Gk = n − i}.
The closed form then follows by induction from the hockey-stick identity.

                                                   137
Remark 5.17 (The coefficient). The coefficient in (5.79) is easy to miscount. The superficially
                   1 (n+k−1)!  n+k−1
similar expression k! (n−1)! =   k    is off by one shift, and is already contradicted by the r2
                                                                                                 
and r3 cases displayed above, which (5.79) reproduces exactly. The correct coefficient is n+k−2
                                                                                            k−1 ,
and it has a direct reading: it counts the ways of distributing the n − 1 births accumulated along
the chain among its k cells, each cell receiving any non-negative number — the stars-and-bars
count for (5.78).
   Further consequences, all verified numerically in section 5.B:
                                 ρ     λ                                            ρ
                 E[rk ] = 1 + k    =1+k ,                            Var(rk ) = k      ,     (5.83)
                                 s     δ                                            s2
                              s k                          X
                E[z rk ] = z         ,                             P{rk = n} = 1.            (5.84)
                              1 − ρz                         n≥1

The mean rupture size grows linearly along the chain: each cell adds one expected burst’s worth
of births, λ/δ, to what it inherited. Figure 5.14 shows the laws against exact simulation, with
the inter-rupture intervals, which shorten as the inherited load grows.

5.10.3     Rupture times
The first rupture time inherits the single-cell flux. With m founders the no-rupture probability
is I(t)m by (5.69), since D ≡ 0 at µ = 0, so
                                       (m)
              P{t1 > t} = I(t)m ,     ft1 (t) = −m I(t)m−1 I ′ (t) = m I(t)m−1 δJ(t).        (5.85)
For m = 1 this is the burst-time density φ = δJ of (5.53), proper here because b = 0. When
µ > 0 the same reasoning must distinguish the two fixation routes: the burst-only time has
distribution function (1 − I(t))/(1 − b) and density δJ(t)/(1 − b), while the general-fixation time
— rupture or internal extinction — has density
                                         δJ(t) + µ p1 (t),                                   (5.86)
the extra term being the flux through 1 → 0: only a population of size exactly one can die out
in a single death event.
   The intervals, their Laplace transforms and the joint structure of the second rupture time are
collected in section 5.C.3, beside the hypergeometric machinery they need.

5.10.4     Where the structure breaks
That extra term in (5.86) is the whole difficulty. The µ = 0 chain rests on (5.77), and the
event-type decomposition works precisely because a cell then has one way to stop: it ruptures.
Once µ > 0 a lineage can vanish internally, event types stop being independent of the load,
and (5.78) has no analogue. The joint laws of (tk , rk ) for general µ > 0 therefore remain open;
section 5.C.2 sets out the available route and section 5.12.4 states what is missing. For µ = 0, by
contrast, (5.78) extends the size laws to all M cells at once, nothing in it depending on where a
cell sits in the chain.
   This contrast is why both probes were run. Gk = Gk needs only independence between
lineages, and multiplicity supplies it; the chain’s decomposition needs the stopping mechanism
to be unique, and internal extinction destroys that.


5.11      Bursting against budding
This chapter opened by objecting to the constant drip. It can now say precisely what that
objection amounts to, and at the level where it was made: one cell.

   The comparator is the budding cell of the constant-rate models. In its simplest stochastic
form a budding infected cell experiences two independent event types — release of one infectious
particle at rate p, and death of the cell at rate dI — and the two continue until the cell dies. Its
survival and mean release are
                                                                                               p            
                                           Ibud (t) = e−dI t ,     Vbud (t) =                      1 − e−dI t ,                              (5.87)
                                                                                              dI
the same one-cell limit the Basic Model of Viral Replication reaches at the population level. The
release count of a budding cell is geometric on {0, 1, 2, . . .}:
                                                                                        k
                                                    dI                   p
                                    P{Kbud = k} =                                             ,    k = 0, 1, 2, . . . ,                      (5.88)
                                                  dI + p              dI + p

since each successive event is a release with probability p/(dI + p) and the terminating death
with probability dI /(dI + p).
   The structural contrast with bursting is exact: in budding, release and death are independent
events, and a cell may release many particles and then die with nothing left to release; in bursting,
release and death are the same event, and whatever has accumulated is released at once. That
difference is why a bursting cell’s release law is a statement about its accumulated load, and
why section 5.6 has no budding analogue: a cell that releases continuously holds nothing to be
quasi-stationary about. Table 5.3 sets the two descriptions side by side for the µ = 0 bursting
case.
   Two rows of the table carry the comparison. The mean cell lifetime of a bursting cell grows
only logarithmically with λ/δ while its mean release grows linearly; budding offers no such
coupling, its lifetime and its release being controlled by separate parameters. And the release
laws, both geometric, differ in their support: budding can release nothing at all from a cell that
died early, while bursting never releases nothing from a cell that ruptured. A trade-off along an
axis of this kind is a familiar theme in the evolution of virulence [62].
   Matching the means does not repair the difference. Figure 5.15 sets the two release laws against
each other with the budding mean tuned to the BDC conditional mean V∞ /(1 − b) = 17.23: the
budding law still holds probability 0.055 of releasing nothing. Matching one moment matches
neither the support nor the tails. That is the concrete form of the objection this chapter opened

with: a fitted constant-rate model can reproduce the average yield of a lytic cell and still be
wrong about the quantity that decides whether a small inoculum establishes at all.
   The comparison is deliberately confined to one cell. What happens when these descriptions
are embedded in a population, where the timing of release governs establishment and invasion
speed, belongs to the chapter on population dynamics; the pathogenesis-level comparison belongs
to the chapter on bursting and budding pathogenesis. All three chapters return to this contrast,
and answer different questions with it.


5.12                Discussion
The single-cell description is now complete in distribution: geometric states at every time, a
geometric quasi-stationary limit, and a burst-size law identical to it, with the conditional laws of


                                                                          140
burst time and burst size, the multi-founder extension and the chained-transfer model alongside.
What follows reads that description into measurement, into two published parameter sets, and
into what is still open; the population consequences are taken up in the chapter on population
dynamics.

5.12.1    Single-cell assay predictions for lytic systems
For a genuinely lytic system — Y. pestis, F. tularensis, lytic phage — a limiting-dilution assay,
one infected cell per well with counts followed in time, measures the single-cell process directly,
with no population model interposed. The number of new infectious particles released per cell is
an integer-valued random variable, and its full distribution, not merely its mean, determines
whether a small inoculum grows into a detectable infection [55, 27]. The theory makes five
predictions, all parameter-free given (λ, µ, δ):

1. a fraction b of wells show no release ever — the mass of internal extinction;
2. the cumulative release in a well is a step function: zero, then a single terminal jump;
3. the jump times follow the density δJ(t)/(1 − b) of (5.53);
4. the jump sizes follow the geometric law with ratio 1/a — the quasi-stationary distribution,
   by theorem 5.10;
5. the between-well variance of total release is Var(W∞ ) from (5.13), a quantity no deterministic
   constant-rate model can produce at all.

The five are not all of a kind. The second tests a standing assumption rather than a derived
result — instantaneous complete release is assumption 3 of section 5.1.1, and an assay that
saw graded release would refute the premise before reaching the theory — and the first is a
one-parameter consequence of the roots alone. Predictions 3 to 5 are where the chapter’s own
content is exposed: a density, a distribution and a variance, each fixed by (λ, µ, δ) with nothing
left to tune. Stated as literally as that, the predictions are too strong for a budding virus:
intermittent budding is not a single terminal burst [14]. They belong to lytic hosts, or to the
bursting endpoint of the spectrum of release models the chapter on population dynamics lays
out.

5.12.2    The same theory at two extremes
The theory has already been fitted to two intracellular pathogens whose parameter estimates sit
at opposite ends of what the model allows, and the contrast shows how much behaviour is fixed
by (λ, µ, δ) alone. Table 5.4 evaluates the closed forms at both.
   For F. tularensis strain SCHU S4, modelling of murine challenge data gave λ = 0.15, µ = 0.01
and δ = 1.5 × 10−4 per hour [16]. At those rates fewer than 7% of infected cells fail to burst in
the model, and those that rupture release a geometric number of bacteria with mean 934 and
median 648. Bursts of that size sit comfortably with the observed ability of doses as low as ten
colony-forming units to establish infection [52]: on the model’s account each rupture floods the
local environment heavily enough that a small inoculum needs only a few rounds of the cycle to
reach large numbers.
   For Bacillus anthracis, approximate Bayesian computation against published time series
produced very different estimates: λ ≈ 0.64, µ ≈ 1.64, δ ≈ 0.04 per hour [54]. Here death
dominates birth, and the model has more than 96% of phagocytes clearing their infection without
rupturing, the rest releasing about 1.6 bacteria on average. The reported infectious dose, 8 × 103
to 5×104 spores [54], is orders of magnitude above the tularaemia figure, and matches a pathogen
whose intracellular dynamics destroy far more than they release.


                                               141
                                           F. tularensis SCHU S4         B. anthracis
                (λ, µ, δ), h−1             (0.15, 0.01, 1.5 × 10−4 )   (0.64, 1.64, 0.04)
                a                                    1.001                    2.66
                b, never bursts                     6.66 %                  96.24 %
                E[K | burst] = a/(a − 1)              934                     1.60
                median burst size                     648                       1
                E[Tprod ]                           45.6 h                   0.74 h
                E[τ | burst]                        48.4 h                   0.93 h

Table 5.4: The same three-rate theory at two published parameter sets, from [16] and [54]. Every
entry is a closed form of this chapter evaluated at those rates: (5.2), (5.12), (5.35), (5.42) and
(5.60). The digits shown are those of the evaluation, not of the estimate: each entry is exact
given the rates in the first row, and inherits whatever uncertainty those rates carry. The two
columns come from different methods applied to different data, so the comparison between them
is qualitative even where the arithmetic within a column is not.


   Two rows of table 5.4 are directly falsifiable, and they are the rows the chapter had not
previously computed. On these estimates a F. tularensis-infected macrophage should rupture
at around two days post-infection, and an anthrax-infected phagocyte, in the rare event that
it ruptures at all, within the hour. Neither number has a measurement to sit beside it here.
What would settle them is time-lapse imaging of infected macrophages held to rupture, with
the time of the release event recorded and the payload counted directly; the prediction is sharp
enough that a handful of such recordings would be decisive, and sharp enough to be wrong. Both
timings are only as good as the rates they condition on, and those rates came from different
methods and different data. What the pair shows regardless is one three-rate theory spanning
the space from near-certain large bursts to near-certain clearance with minimal release.

5.12.3    What the two probes showed
The two probes of sections 5.9 and 5.10 were asked the same question — how far does the
geometric structure reach — and returned opposite answers, which is the more informative
outcome.
   Multiplicity leaves the structure intact. Lineages are independent until the shared clock
rings, so Gk = Gk and the load in a multiply infected cell is a sum of independent copies of the
single-founder load; the bookkeeping changes, and two natural guesses about it fail (remarks 5.15
and 5.16), but the geometric shape does not. Chaining destroys it as soon as internal extinction
is possible, because the µ = 0 decomposition needs a cell to have exactly one way to stop and the
death rate gives it two. The geometric structure is robust to multiplicity and fragile to internal
extinction in the chained setting, and the two failures are failures of different things — a counting
identity in one case, a decomposition of the process in the other.

5.12.4    Open problems
1. How far the telescoping criterion reaches. Theorem 5.10 closes what was an open
   problem: the identity between burst size and the quasi-stationary law has a structural proof,
   and remark 5.11 states the property that proof uses. Two questions survive it. Does that
   property characterise the processes for which the identity holds, or is it merely sufficient?
   And can the identity be obtained independently by a Yaglom-type argument [59], which
   would connect it to the existence theory rather than to the calculus?



                                                  142
2. Chained joint laws for general µ. The joint laws of (tk , rk ) for µ > 0, and the joint law
   of (tM , rM ) for the full M -cell chain, remain open. The coefficient machinery of section 5.C.2
   is the available route, and section 5.10.4 says why the elementary route is not.
3. Logistic intracellular growth. The standing objection to linear birth is that it lets the
   conditional load grow without bound. The defence is that the burst hazard is proportional
   to load and so regulates it — theorem 5.6 is precisely the statement that the conditional
   load stabilises at a/(a − 1) — but the sensitivity of the burst statistics, and of the kernels
   the chapter on population dynamics will use, to a logistic birth rate remains to be checked
   numerically.

5.12.5       What comes next
Everything in this chapter is single-cell. The released particles have so far been counted and
then forgotten, and that is the approximation the chapter on population dynamics removes.
There the survival and release statistics derived here become the age-structured kernels of a
population-level renewal model: cells infected at different times contribute to the infected-cell
and free-particle pools according to Ifix (a) and g(a) = δK(a), in the tradition of age-structured
renewal running from [60, 61]. The constant-rate models of viral dynamics then appear as a
projection of that renewal system, and bursting is compared against budding at the level where
the comparison matters — establishment, invasion speed, and what data can and cannot identify.
   One notational matter is left for later. The two-type chapter solves a Riccati equation
structurally identical to (5.7) under unrelated names, as section 5.2.3 noted; aligning the two
would mean renaming a and b throughout this chapter, the chapter on the birth–death–cata-
strophe process and the chapter on population dynamics, and is a thesis-wide decision rather
than a local one.

birth–death–catastrophe process, M for one taken from the mathematical introduction, S for
one standard in the literature, and N for one established in this chapter. Table 5.5 collects them.

The µ = 0 specialisation. b = 0, a = 1 + δ/λ, D ≡ 0, Ifix = I, V∞ = 1 + λ/δ = ⟨X⟩QS ,

E[Tprod ] = λ−1 log(1 + λ/δ), V∞ = k + λ/δ, and P{K = k} = sρk−1 with s = δ/(λ + δ), ρ = 1 − s:
the burst size is geometric on {1, 2, . . .} with success probability s, and every realisation bursts.

none of them survives µ > 0.


5.B      Verification record: chained immediate transfer
Every quantitative claim of section 5.10 and section 5.C.3 was checked by exact simulation of
the chained process. Sampling is direct, because the event-type decomposition of section 5.10.1
makes the sequence of event types a sequence of independent coin flips and the holding times
exponential of known rate. This appendix records the suite, its tally, and how to reproduce it.
   The suite verify_chained_transfer.py samples the chained process exactly and checks
every quantitative claim across three parameter sets, (λ, δ) ∈ {(1, 1), (1, 0.1), (2, 0.5)}: 28 checks,
all passing.

(5.78) extends the size laws to all M at once, since nothing in it depends on where a cell sits in

termwise for t bounded, but in the limit it is asserted rather than proved.

5.C.3     Inter-rupture intervals and the second rupture time

                                                       147
Chapter 6

A birth–death–catastrophe extension
of the standard within-host model,
and what it says about bursting

6.1     Introduction
The standard model of within-host virus dynamics describes an infected cell with two numbers:
it releases virions at a constant rate p and is removed at a constant rate dI [11, 13, 12]. Both
are fitted to viral load data and then reported as though they characterised the pathogen. They
are, however, phenomenological. Nothing in the standard model says what the cell is doing
between infection and release, and the constants absorb whatever it is.
   For one class of pathogen this costs nothing. A budding virus really does experience release
and death as separate, independent events, and section 6.2.2 shows that the standard model is
then an exact description of the cell, not an approximation to it. For a lytic pathogen it is wrong
in mechanism. Such a pathogen releases nothing while it replicates inside the cell and then
releases everything at once, in the event that ends the cell’s productive life. Release and death
are not independent; they are the same event. Y. pestis in the macrophage, F. tularensis in the
macrophage and the lytic phage in its bacterium are all of that kind, and none is a constant drip.
   That the standard model needs intracellular structure is not a new observation, and the
modelling programme built on it is reviewed by Ciupe and Conway [65]. Two routes are
established. The first inserts a delay between infection and production, either as a distributed
kernel or as one or more explicit eclipse compartments, an approach going back to Herz et al.
[66] and now standard [67, 68, 69]. The second, multiscale, route couples an age-structured
population equation to an explicit intracellular pool: in the hepatitis C models of Guedj et
al. and Rong and Perelson [70, 71] an intracellular viral RNA level  R∞
                                                                         R(t, a) is tracked alongside
infected cells I(t, a) of infection age a, and free virus is fed by ρ 0 R(t, a)I(t, a) da. Both routes
have paid off. The review’s two headline returns are sharper parameter estimates — neglecting
the pre-productive period biases R0 [72] — and the ability to locate a drug’s mode of action at
a named step of the viral life cycle.
   What neither route supplies is a stochastic intracellular mechanism. The delay literature
assumes a distributional family for the age structure and fits it; the multiscale literature derives
the age structure, but from a deterministic pool, so it yields a release schedule and nothing more.
Yet for a lytic pathogen the questions that matter early in infection are distributional. Whether
a founder cell establishes an infection at all depends on how many particles it releases, not on
how many it releases on average, and a cell whose internal population dies out before rupture
releases none.
   This chapter closes that gap for one mechanism. The chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe

                                                148
process and the chapter on its distribution theory developed a birth–death–catastrophe process
for the intracellular replicative units — birth at rate λ per unit, death at rate µ per unit, and
catastrophe at rate δ per unit, at which the cell ruptures and releases its entire contents — and
obtained its survival, release and burst-size laws in closed form. Both stop at the cell boundary.
Here that theory is carried across the boundary into a population.

Contributions
 1. A renewal model forced by the intracellular process (section 6.4). The population
    equations need exactly two functions of cell age: the survival kernel S and the release kernel
    g. Both are already in closed form, so the population model has no free age structure to fit.
    The equations are the first moments of the underlying stochastic process exactly, not an
    approximation to them (proposition 6.2).

 2. The standard model is a projection (section   
                                                        6.5). For each epidemic growth rate r there
    is a unique constant pair peff (r), dI,eff (r) reproducing the renewal system’s exponential
    solution exactly (theorem 6.3). A fitted release rate is therefore a property of the infection’s
    phase, not of the pathogen: over the whole map it moves by the factor E X 2 QS , a pure
    number fixed by the intracellular rates, which is 231 at (λ, µ, δ) = (1, 0, 0.1). No constant pair
    serves a transient, and no fit of (p, dI ) recovers the three intracellular rates (section 6.5.6).

 3. A closed-form answer to whether bursting pays (section 6.6). This is the result the
    stochastic treatment exists to deliver, since it turns on the offspring distribution rather than
    its mean. Write L = a(1 − b) for the flooding parameter (definition 6.7). At fixed total
    output per cell, bursting leaves R0 exactly unchanged (theorem 6.6); lowers the extinction
    probability at establishment if and only if L > 1, equivalently
                                               1  1 1
                                                 < + ,
                                               δ  λ µ

    by the exact amount (L − 1)(1/m − 1) (theorem 6.8); and slows deterministic invasion in
    every regime examined. Threshold, establishment risk and invasion speed are three questions
    with three different answers.

 4. Commensurability (section 6.7). Because only the pair (S, g) enters, budding, bursting,
    recurrent partial release, multiply infected cells and the multiscale hepatitis C model of
    [70, 71] are all instances of one skeleton, differing only in the kernels they supply (table 6.4).

   Table 6.7 collects every population-level formula of the chapter in one place, for use as a
reference card. Throughout, λ, µ and δ are the intracellular birth, death and catastrophe rates
per replicative unit; the roots a, b, the derived constants A, B, θ, κ and the single-cell functions
I, D, Ifix , J, K, V are those of the chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe process, collected in
section 6.A. Table 6.1 fixes the population symbols. Two naming decisions are made once and
kept: the burst-time density written φ in the chapter on its distribution theory is written δJ
here, because φ is reserved for the partial-release fraction of section 6.7.2; and the survival kernel
S is the non-fixation probability Ifix , written S where it acts as a kernel and Ifix where the closed
form is meant.

6.1.1    Standing assumptions
These hold throughout unless stated otherwise.

(A1) Target cells T are held constant: no depletion, no saturation, no immune response.

6.2.3                          Bursting, and why no constant repairs it
In bursting, release and death are the same event: whatever has accumulated leaves at once and
the cell’s productive life ends in that instant. Take the cleanest consequence. Let N0 cells be
infected at the same moment, each running an independent copy of the single-cell process. The
number still productively infected at time t is N0 Ifix (t) exactly in expectation, and the expected
cumulative release is N0 V (t), so the release rate is N0 δK(t). It depends on the age of the cohort
and it is not constant. A cohort releases on a schedule, not at a rate (figure 6.1).
   The natural repair is to keep (6.1) and rebuild p from the intracellular theory — to set p
equal to the quasi-stationary mean load E[X]QS = a/(a − 1), on the reasoning that a long-lived
cell hovers at that level. The obstruction is dimensional. Here p is a number of particles per
unit time and E[X]QS is a pure count, so the substitution introduces a spurious factor of time;
when µ = 0 one has E[X]QS = V∞ exactly, and the proposal replaces a cell’s output rate by its
total lifetime
             R
               output. Averaging the burst size over the burst-time density fails the same way,
returning δ 0∞ K = V∞ , a count again. Weighting the load-dependent flux by the burst-time
density has the right dimensions but the wrong measure: that density says when a single cell
bursts, whereas a population release rate needs an average over the ages of the cells currently
infected, and in an asynchronous infection those are not the same.
   What survives is two facts rather than a substitute constant. The expected release flux of a
cell of age α is δK(α); and at any host time the infected population is a mixture of ages, one
cohort per past moment of infection. The population model must therefore track cell age.

                                                                            151
6.3     The intracellular process and its two kernels
6.3.1    The process
Inside a cell, replicative units follow a linear birth–death process with an added catastrophe.
Each unit gives birth at rate λ, dies at rate µ, and at rate δ per unit triggers rupture, at which
the cell releases its entire load and enters an absorbing state. Writing Xα for the load at cell age
α, the process is absorbed either by rupture — a burst — or by internal extinction, in which
the last unit dies and the cell never bursts at all. The chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe
process solves it. The roots
                               q                           q                        λ+µ+δ
                   a=η+        η 2 − µλ ,       b=η−           η 2 − µλ ,      η=         ,              (6.5)
                                                                                      2λ
satisfy b < 1 < a and carry everything: b is the probability that a cell clears internally without
ever bursting, and 1/a is the ratio of the geometric burst-size law. Section 6.A collects the closed
forms used below; the two that matter most are the lifetime yield and the mean productive
lifetime,
                                  a(1 − b)                  1       a
                           V∞ =            ,    E[Tprod ] = log         .                       (6.6)
                                    a−1                     λ     a−1

6.3.2    The two kernels
Age-structured population dynamics needs exactly two single-cell ingredients. The survival
kernel is the probability that a cell infected α time units ago is still productively infected,

                                                           (a − b)2 eθα
                               S(α) := Ifix (α) =                             ,                          (6.7)

and the release kernel is the expected release flux at cell age α,

                                            g(α) := δK(α) = V ′ (α).                                     (6.8)

The reasoning behind (6.8) is the one the failed proposal of section 6.2.3 stated and then did not
use: a cell bursts at rate δXα and

released in [α, α + dα] is δE Xα2 dα = δK(α)dα. With K from (6.43) both kernels are explicit
functions of age (figure 6.2). This is what it will mean for the population model to be forced by
the intracellular process: the kernels are computed, not fitted.

6.4                           Burst-aware renewal dynamics
6.4.1                         The renewal system
Hold target cells T fixed, let infection occur at rate γ per virion, clear virions at rate c, and
define the incidence i(t) = γT V(t). Each infection event at time t − α starts a cell of age α, still
productive with probability S(α) and releasing at expected rate g(α). Summing over infection
times and adding the I0 cells already infected at t = 0 gives the system, whose bookkeeping is
drawn in figure 6.3.

6.4.2      The equations are exact in the mean
Equations (6.9) and (6.10) are not an approximation to the underlying stochastic process. They
are its first moments exactly, and the reason is that every rate in sight is linear.

Proposition 6.2 (First-moment exactness). Consider the stochastic process in which each infec-
tion event starts an independent birth–death–catastrophe cell, each released particle independently
infects at rate γT or clears at rate c, and the I0 cells present at t = 0 have age zero. Write I(t)
and V(t) for the expected numbers of productive cells and free particles. Then I and V satisfy
(6.9) and (6.10) exactly.

Proof. Let N be the point process of infection events. Since each free particle infects at rate γT and
particles are independent, N has compensator γT V ω (t) dt, where V ω is the realised particle count; taking
expectations, its mean intensity is i(t) = γT V(t). A cell founded at time t − α evolves independently
of N and of every other cell, so it is still productive at t with probability Ifix (α) and its expected
release flux at t is g(α), both by definition of the kernels. Conditioning on N and applying Campbell’s


                                                                        154
formula — legitimate because Ifix and g are bounded and non-negative, so Fubini applies — gives
I(t) = I0 Ifix (t) + (i ∗ Ifix )(t) and an expected release flux of I0 g(t) + (i ∗ g)(t). Clearance removes particles
at rate c per particle, contributing −cV(t) to the mean. Collecting terms gives (6.10).                            □
The step that would fail for a nonlinear incidence is the identification of the mean intensity
with γT V: for f (V, T ) nonlinear, E[f (V ω )] ̸= f (E[V ω ]) and the moment hierarchy does not close.
Assumption (A1) is what keeps the incidence linear. This is one reason to prefer mass action
where the data permit it, and the review of [65] reports that they generally do, the saturating
alternatives — Holling type 2, Beddington–DeAngelis, Crowley–Martin — being well explored
theoretically but not validated against data.

6.4.3     One skeleton, many mechanisms
Nothing in (6.9) and (6.10) used the birth–death–catastrophe process beyond the two kernels it
supplied. With incidence i(t) = γT V(t), or a nonlinear f (V, T ), any pair (S, g) gives
                                                         dV
                  I(t) = I0 S(t) + (i ∗ S)(t),              = I0 g(t) + (i ∗ g)(t) − c V(t),                  (6.11)
                                                         dt
                                                                                                    R
with effective parameters peff (r) = ge(r)/S(r)
                                           e     and, for linear incidence, R0 = (γT /c) 0∞ g. This is
the chapter’s most general statement, and it is what makes release mechanisms commensurable:
they differ only in (S, g) (table 6.4). The first row is a structural check on the whole construction:
exponential kernels reduce (6.11) exactly to standard BMVR (section 6.4.5).

6.4.4     Relation to existing multiscale models
The renewal framework itself is standard: it is the McKendrick–von Foerster machinery of
mathematical demography [60, 61, 74]. So are age-structured within-host models. Nelson et al.
[75] write down a within-host system with age-dependent production and death rates that is
structurally (6.11), and take the age dependence as given. The multiscale hepatitis C models
[70, 71] go further and derive it, coupling infected cells of age a to an intracellular viral RNA
level R(t, a) obeying
               R
                      a transport equation with production, degradation and export; free virus is
then fed by ρ 0∞ R(t, a)I(t, a) da, which is (6.11) with g(α) = ρR(α)e−δα . That model is the
third row of table 6.4 and a direct ancestor of what is done here.
   The difference is what the intracellular layer is. In [70, 71] it is a deterministic pool, so it
supplies a mean release schedule and nothing else. Here it is a stochastic process solved in

closed form, so it supplies the schedule and the distributions built on it: the burst-size law, the
probability b that a cell clears internally and releases nothing, the offspring generating function,
and hence extinction probabilities. Everything in section 6.5 could in principle be asked of
a deterministic intracellular model; nothing in section 6.6 could, because establishment is a
question about the offspring distribution rather than its mean. That is the sense in which the
stochastic mechanism earns its place.

6.4.5                           Numerical verification
The renewal system was checked end to end at four levels: kernel integrity, the reduction at
γT = 0, the structural limits available in closed form, and stochastic ground truth. All 62 checks
pass. Exponential kernels reproduce standard BMVR to relative error below 5 × 10−5 ; the late
growth rate equals the characteristic root of section 6.5 to about 10−7 ; the R0 threshold is sharp;
and an independent Gillespie ensemble reproduces the renewal means for I and V to within
1–2% (figure 6.4). The suite runs over the three regimes of table 6.2 together with (1, 0.2, 0.05)
and (1, 0, 0.5), chosen to spread the kernel shapes; it is the script verify_result_20_1.py
distributed with the chapter source, and tests M and N of it are the two numerical sweeps cited
in sections 6.5.3 and 6.6.4.




                                                                  156
6.5      The standard model is a projection
The renewal system does not merely disagree with standard BMVR in transients. It contains
standard BMVR as an exact special case, and the relationship is precise enough to be turned
into predictions.

of a treated patient. The genuine left-hand endpoint mirrors the young-cell limit.

missing inverse time, and the load-proportional burst hazard weights by X a second time.

6.5.3    Monotonicity, and the size of the effect
Proposition 6.5 (Monotonicity of the effective release rate). Write ρ(α) = g(α)/S(α)
                                                                                    2
                                                                                            for the
expected release flux per still-productive cell, so that ρ(0) = δ and ρ(∞) = δE X QS . If ρ is
non-decreasing then peff is non-increasing on (−θ, ∞), strictly so if ρ is strictly increasing.

Proof. For r > −θ let πf,r be the probability density α 7→ e−rα f (α)/fe(r). Differentiating under the
integral, d log fe(r)/dr = −E[α]πf,r , so

                                   d
                                      log peff (r) = E[α]πS,r − E[α]πg,r .                         (6.18)
                                   dr
The two densities have ratio πg,r (α)/πS,r (α) ∝ ρ(α), independently of r. If ρ is non-decreasing, πg,r
dominates πS,r in the monotone likelihood ratio order, hence in the usual stochastic order, hence in mean;
so (6.18) is non-positive.                                                                              □
The hypothesis is a statement about one closed-form function of one variable: substituting

which must be shown decreasing in v ∈ (0, 1]. That reduces the question to a finite algebraic
one. It is not settled here, but ρ was found increasing at every one of 4000 triples sampled over
(µ, δ) ∈ [10−3 , 20]2 at λ = 1, and peff decreasing at every one (test M of the verification suite).
    The size of the effect is what matters. At (λ, µ, δ) = (1, 0, 0.1), peff (0) ≈ 4.59 against
peff (∞) = 0.1: the same cell, two orders of magnitude apart, with nothing separating the two
figures but how fast the epidemic around it is growing. Over the whole map the span is larger,
and exact — by (6.16) and (6.17) the ratio of the endpoints is

A caution attaches about assumption (A2): (6.22) is the continuum reproduction number, in
which one virion contributes mean γT /c new infections, whereas a discrete branching process
absorbing virions on infection carries the mean m = qV∞ with q = γT /(γT + c). The two agree
when γT ≪ c, and it is m that governs section 6.6.

6.5.5                     What the projection loses
To see what age structure does to an epidemic, integrate both systems side by side. In figures 6.6
and 6.7 the solid curve is the renewal system and the dashed one standard BMVR with the
fairest constant match, the r = 0 end of (6.13), so that total lifetime output and mean productive
lifetime agree.
    The reading is consistent across panels. The matched model misses three things: the early lag,
because young cells dominate and release is nearer the young-cell limit δ than peff (0); the timing
of the peak; and the late growth rate, since generation-time structure moves the operative release
rate away from its r = 0 value as the epidemic grows. The relative free-particle discrepancy is
often order one or larger by the end of the plotted window in supercritical regimes, and worst
when intracellular death is high, where V∞ is small while the age structure of release stays

Figure 6.7: Why no single constant pair will do. One renewal trajectory (solid) against two
standard parameter choices: the r = 0 match (dashed) and the young-cell limit p = δ, dI = µ + δ
(dash-dot). (a) The first six time units, where the r = 0 match overshoots by more than an order
of magnitude while the young-cell parameters are the closer of the two. (b) The full window,
where the ordering has reversed and the young-cell model has collapsed. Each choice is correct
at one growth rate and the trajectory passes through many, exactly as (6.13) predicts. Both
panels logarithmic in V. Parameters: (λ, µ, δ) = (1, 0, 0.1), γT = 0.025, c = 0.2, R0 = 1.38.


strongly non-exponential.
   Note that the early lag is an eclipse phase that nobody put in. Within-host models routinely
insert one by hand, as a fitted delay distribution or as explicit eclipse compartments [65, 66];
here it emerges from the intracellular dynamics with no extra parameter, its scale set by E[Tprod ].
The caution that goes with the observation is Herz et al.’s [66]: the shoulder in post-treatment
viral load is a compound of pharmacological delay, intracellular delay, infected-cell decay and
viral clearance, so the honest claim is not that this lag is the eclipse but that a lag of this shape
arises without being fitted.

6.5.6                    Identifiability: a negative result
How much of the intracellular process can a population-level fit recover? At any fixed growth
rate r, the map
                               (λ, µ, δ) 7−→ (peff (r), dI,eff (r))
sends three rates to two numbers, and is therefore not invertible (figure 6.8). This is a structural
identifiability statement of the kind Miao et al. [78] treat for viral dynamics generally, and it is
negative for the same reason: the observable is a projection, and the projection has a kernel.
   It is worth seeing that this result is corroborated from the fitting side. Mittler et al. [67]
compared a range of intracellular delay distributions against HIV RNA data after protease
inhibitor initiation and found the resulting parameter estimates largely insensitive to which
distribution was assumed — a finding influential enough that the review of [65] cites it to justify
preferring the simplest eclipse compartment. Read through theorem 6.3, that insensitivity is not
a rival claim but the same one: on a single exponential phase a constant pair reproduces the
kernel exactly, so the shape of the kernel is invisible in exactly the regime where those fits were
performed. Consistently, when Kakizoe et al. [69] fitted in vitro SHIV data resolving the early
transient, an Erlang delay did improve the fit.
   A third observable is therefore required; in order of experimental accessibility:
