Figure 6.8: Population observables leave level sets rather than identifying the intracellular rates.
With c = 1 and γT = 1, the interior flooding point (λ, µ, δ) = (1, 0.5, 1/3) has R0 = 2 and
r⋆ = 0.294. (a) One population observable leaves a level curve: in the slice λ = 1 the contours
R0 = 2 and r = r⋆ are distinct; alternatives A and C share R0 with the reference but not its
growth rate. (b) With λ free, a continuous family of triples shares both. Joint knowledge of
threshold and exponential growth therefore does not identify (λ, µ, δ).


 1. the burst-size dispersion — the geometric ratio 1/a fixes one combination of the rates
    directly;

 2. the fraction of cells that clear without bursting, which is b;

 3. the shape — not merely the mean — of the release-time density δJ.

Any one of these, together with (p, dI ), over-determines (λ, µ, δ) and converts the fit into a
consistency test of the intracellular model. The limitation is not one of fitting procedure but of
the information content of standard BMVR itself, and the remedy is the one [65] calls for in
general terms: data at a finer scale of resolution, chosen to match the model’s structure.


6.6                     Bursting versus budding: the flooding parameter L
Does bursting help a pathogen get established? The question belongs to the stochastic early
phase, where counts are small and extinction is a live possibility [27], and it is the question
the stochastic intracellular model was built to answer: both versions of the early infection are
branching processes whose offspring distributions are in closed form.
   The mechanism at issue is worth naming before it is analysed. A bursting cell delivers its
whole output in one instant, so a founder that survives to rupture presents the target population
with many simultaneous infection attempts rather than a trickle spread over its lifetime. Whether
that concentration helps or hurts is not obvious: many attempts at once buy insurance against
the loss of any one particle, but they are all forfeited if the intracellular population dies out
before the burst, which happens with probability b. We call the concentration flooding, and the
question is when it pays.




                                                                           162
6.6.1   The offspring of one infected cell
In the branching approximation each released particle independently either infects a target cell,
at rate γT , or is cleared, at rate c, and so infects with probability
                                                    γT
                                            q=            .                                (6.23)
                                                   γT + c
The offspring of one infected cell is the burst size K thinned by q. Summing (6.46), the
unconditional burst-size probability generating function is GK (z) = b + (δ/λ)z/(a − z) —
including the mass b of cells that never burst — and thinning replaces z by y := 1 − q + qz:
                                           δ y
                          Goff (z) = b +         ,            y = 1 − q + qz.              (6.24)
                                           λ a−y
One checks Goff (1) = b + (δ/λ)/(a − 1) = b + B = 1. The mean and variance of the offspring
number are
                                                          2q 2 V∞
                   m = G′off (1) = q V∞ ,      Varburst =         + qV∞ − q 2 V∞
                                                                               2
                                                                                 .        (6.25)
                                                           a−1
When γT ≪ c, q ≈ γT /c and m ≈ R0 .
   The comparator is the budding cell of section 6.2.2, whose output is geometric with mean
p/dI . Match it to the bursting cell’s total output, p/dI = V∞ . That matching is what makes the
comparison fair, and by theorem 6.6 it costs nothing: at equal total output the two mechanisms
have exactly the same R0 , so everything that follows compares two pathogens at one threshold,
and any difference between them is a difference in how the output is scheduled rather than
in how much of it there is. Thinning a geometric law leaves a geometric law, so the budding
offspring number is geometric with the same mean m = qV∞ .

6.6.2   Extinction, and the flooding advantage
The extinction probability of a branching process is the smallest root of Goff (z) = z in [0, 1].
With y = 1 − q + qz the fixed-point equation is quadratic, z = 1 is always a root, and the other
root gives, for m > 1,

Proof. The identity (6.28) is algebra: substitute (6.26), m = qV∞ = qL/(a − 1) and simplify. Since m > 1
makes the second factor negative, the sign of the difference is the sign of 1 − L. For the criterion, L > 1
is a(1 − b) > 1, i.e. a > 1 + µ/λ; inserting the definition of a from (6.5) and squaring — legitimate since
both sides are positive, or the right-hand side is not — yields (λ + µ + δ)2 − 4λµ > 4λ2 (1 + µ/λ − η)2 ,
which reduces to δ(λ + µ) > λµ.                                                                          □
   The criterion (6.29) is the chapter’s central biological statement, and it reads cleanly as
a comparison of timescales.
                         
                                The burst clock 1/δ must be shorter than the harmonic-type
combination 1/λ + 1/µ of the birth and death timescales. A cell that takes too long to rupture
is a cell whose internal population has time to die out first, and the all-or-nothing hazard b then
outweighs the insurance that a simultaneous batch buys. When µ = 0 that hazard vanishes and
bursting wins at every δ. Figure 6.9 shows where the boundary lies in the rate plane; table 6.5
confirms the identity in each regime, and figure 6.10 traces both extinction probabilities across
the whole range of q.

6.6.3                              The boundary L = 1: a coincidence of laws
The boundary case is more than a tie of extinction probabilities.
Proposition 6.9. The following are equivalent:
                                                                              µ                     1  1 1

So L does more than fix a sign. At the boundary, bursting and budding are the same branching
process in different clothes; away from it, (6.31) says precisely how the two offspring laws differ,
and the extinction ordering of theorem 6.8 is the ordering of their variances at fixed mean. That
is the mechanism behind the criterion: concentrating output into one batch reduces offspring
variance — which helps at establishment — but only if the batch is delivered often enough to
offset the mass b at zero.

6.6.4     The price: invasion speed
Establishment is half the story; the other half is how fast an established infection grows. Match

— and choose γT , or c, so that both share R0 = 2. Solving for the growth rates, rbud from

Proof. Each model’s growth rate is the unique root of ϕ(r) := γT ge(r) − (r + c); ϕ is strictly decreasing, and
ϕ(0) = γT V∞ − c = c(R0 − 1) > 0, so the root is positive and unique. If (6.33) holds then ϕburst ≤ ϕbud
pointwise, so the root of the former is no larger. Conversely, suppose geburst (r0 ) > gebud (r0 ) for some r0 > 0.
Choose any c > 0 and set γT = (r0 + c)/e    gburst (r0 ), so that rburst = r0 ; then ϕbud (r0 ) < 0 and rbud < r0 .
Taking c small makes R0 = γT V∞ /c exceed one, so the ordering fails for an admissible coupling.                □
Both sides of (6.33) agree at r = 0, by the matching, so its content is entirely in how the
two release schedules are spread over age. The inequality was checked at 1500 triples over
(µ, δ) ∈ [10−3 , 10] × [10−3 , 16] at λ = 1, at 60 values of r across four decades, without a violation
(test N of the verification suite). The usual stochastic order does not hold — the burst release-age
survival function dips below the matched exponential — so (6.33) is not a weaker way of saying
something stronger. It is the order the problem actually has.
   The mechanism is generation time in the exact sense of section 6.5.4. The budding cell starts
releasing at once while the bursting cell must first accumulate load, so budding’s generation-
time density A/R0 sits earlier, and by A(r)     e    = 1 an earlier density gives a larger root. At
(λ, µ, δ) = (1, 0, 0.1) with c = 1 the mean generation time is 2.398 + 1 = 3.398 for budding against
3.089 + 1 = 4.089 for bursting, which is what puts rbud = 0.250 above rburst = 0.180 (figure 6.11).
This is the same delay that appeared as the eclipse-like lag of section 6.5.5, and it is the phage
lysis-timing problem in another notation: holding on longer makes more particles per cell but
takes longer to make them [63, 77]. That an infectious-period distribution shifts the growth rate
at fixed threshold is a general fact about renewal epidemics rather than anything peculiar to
bursting [79, 80]; what is particular here is that the distribution is derived rather than assumed.

Figure 6.12: Tuning δ traces a strict extinction–growth trade-off, drawn parametrically as δ

productive lifetime. There is no setting of δ that improves both coordinates: faster growth is
bought with a higher extinction probability.


6.6.5                               The trade-off
Put together, the two comparisons say what bursting buys and what it costs. At fixed total
output per cell, bursting leaves R0 untouched, lowers the probability of early extinction when
L > 1, and slows deterministic invasion. A pathogen tuning δ therefore faces a genuine trade-off
(figure 6.12): zext and r move in opposite directions, and no setting of δ improves both. For
µ > 0, budding beats bursting on extinction until δ crosses δ∗ = λµ/(λ + µ), beyond which
bursting takes over; for µ = 0 bursting wins at every δ, at the continuing cost of a slower r.
    Two remarks place the result. First, its scope: both comparisons hold the offspring mean
fixed and are branching-process statements, so they say nothing about nonlinear effects, where
other advantages of flooding may live (section 6.8.2). Second, its company. Lord and Bonsall

                                                                                                                  167
[81] reach a qualitatively parallel conclusion for within-host virus evolution by a different route,
using fixed-delay models to compare virion release strategies and finding that lysis-like strategies
are favoured when the terminal event comes quickly and budding when it is slow. The criterion
(6.29) is a sharp version of “quickly”, written in intracellular rates, and it comes with the exact
size of the advantage from (6.28). The evolutionary framing belongs to the chapter on bursting
and budding pathogenesis, which inherits from this comparison the awkward fact that one
mechanism can be favoured on establishment and disfavoured on invasion speed at once — the
nested-model problem in its usual form [62].


6.7     The reach of the construction
Section 6.4.3 showed that any pair of kernels induces the same population equations. Bursting
and constant budding are the ends of a spectrum; between them lie mechanisms that release
many times per cell or release part of the load, and beyond it lie models that are not birth–death–
catastrophe processes at all. All supply a pair (S, g), and none changes (6.11). This section
works through the two intermediates that matter and then says where the generality stops.

6.7.1       Recurrent release: the reset process
The absorbing process releases at most once per cell. The intermediate worth isolating lets a
cell release many times: while the cell is alive the load undergoes birth λn, death µn, optional
immigration ν, and a dump that resets — at rate δn the whole pool is released and the load
returns to zero, after which it may re-accumulate — with the cell dying at a separate, load-
independent rate dI . Reading the load as a local assembled pool rather than the whole-cell
contents makes this a tractable multi-pulse caricature between full dumping and continuous
export.
   Because dumps do not absorb, the kernels change but the skeleton does not:
                                                                         h   i
                            S(α) = e−dI α ,      g(α) = δ e−dI α E Xα2 ,                     (6.34)
            
with E Xα2 computed for the non-absorbing reset process. Linear rates    
                                                                             keep its moments
tractable, so g is explicit once they are solved, and structurally g ∝ E X 2 exactly as for the
absorbing process. The effective parameters become

                             peff (r) = (r + dI ) ge(r),      dI,eff ≡ dI ,                  (6.35)
                   R
and R0 = (γT /c) 0∞ g, finite only for dI > 0 since otherwise recurrent dumps could continue
forever. If the reset process admits a stationary law π and dI is small, the mature-cell limit is

                                                g(α)
                                  p∞ = lim              = δ Eπ [X 2 ],                       (6.36)
                                          α→∞ S(α)


the same form as the old-cell limit (6.17) of the absorbing process. A long-lived infected cell
therefore behaves as constant-p BMVR with that p, while transients — first dumps, young cells
— still need the full kernel. This is what makes the spectrum claim concrete rather than gestural:
an intermediate with kernels, an effective release rate, a mature limit, and a definite answer as
to which standard model it converges on.

6.7.2       Partial release, and antiviral mode of action
The remaining axis is how much leaves the cell when a release event fires. The natural
intermediate removes a random part of the load; the default is boolean thinning, each unit


                                                  168
released independently with probability φ. At the mean-field level with event clock δX, writing
Q for the total intracellular stock across productive cells, the population equations are
        dI                       dQ           Q2                  dV      Q2
           = γT V − dI I,           = νI − δφ    − dI Q,             = δφ    − c V.           (6.37)
        dt                       dt           I                   dt      I
With a load-independent clock λ(X) = δ instead, the mean export is δφQ and the equations
collapse to stock-and-export dynamics with εeff = δφ. So partial release is invisible in the
mean-field equations whenever the event clock is load-independent, and visible when it is
load-dependent; in single-cell release-count distributions it is always visible.
    This axis is where the construction meets the second of the two payoffs that [65] identifies for
intracellular modelling: the ability to locate a drug’s mode of action at a named step of the viral
life cycle. In the multiscale hepatitis C models [70, 71] a direct-acting antiviral is represented by
three separate factors — blocking intracellular RNA production by (1 − ϵa ), blocking packaging
and secretion by (1 − ϵs ), and enhancing intracellular degradation by a factor at least one. Each
has a counterpart here. Blocking production lowers λ; enhancing degradation raises µ; blocking
entry lowers γT ; and blocking secretion is exactly a reduction in φ, since φ = 1 − ϵs . Every
one of these is a transformation of (S, g), so a drug in this framework does not add parameters
to the population layer but moves the kernels, and its population signature is whatever (6.13)
makes of the new pair.
    Two consequences follow, both in principle testable. The first is that a drug moves the
pathogen relative to the flooding boundary, and the direction is read off (6.29) without further
calculation. Enhancing intracellular degradation raises µ and so lowers 1/λ + 1/µ, moving the
pathogen toward the L < 1 side on which its burst schedule is a liability rather than an asset at
establishment; blocking intracellular production raises 1/λ and moves it the other way. Neither
move need cross the boundary — (6.29) is an inequality, not a monotone score — but the sign
of the shift is fixed, and (6.28) gives the size of what is gained or lost at each end. The second
is that because peff depends on r, an efficacy inferred from a decay slope inherits the same
phase dependence that section 6.5.3 finds in p itself, which is one reading of why the hepatitis C
clearance estimates of section 6.8.1 disagreed across therapies. Whether the flooding advantage
survives at intermediate φ, and whether the closed forms persist there at all, is open.

6.7.3    Beyond birth–death–catastrophe: HIV
The results above are written for pathogens whose intracellular life ends in load-proportional
catastrophe. HIV is not one: it buds from the plasma membrane, and an infected cell need
not rupture in a single event. After integration the template count is one or a few proviruses
and structural products do not self-replicate, so the mean load follows an immigration-like
x̄′ ≈ ν − µx̄ rather than the multiplicative growth that produces δK. Yet limiting-dilution
data show something surge-like — long quiet periods, then a concentrated bout of release —
which is the phenomenon the catastrophe process exists to explain. Hataye et al. [14] report
first detection around day 3 with bulk production over days 3–5, an Erlang eclipse with n ≈ 5
stages, many silent lineages, and a sigmoid rather than linear dependence of establishment on
the number of virus-releasing cells, with a threshold near 2.3 cells.
    The position taken here is neither to force HIV into the catastrophe process nor to retreat
to constant-p BMVR. The skeleton (6.11) carries HIV on a different pair of kernels: SHIV the
probability of still being in a productive lineage and gHIV the density of productive sojourn
times, with a multi-stage eclipse supplying the delay — near zero for small α, then rising and
falling. The effective parameters carry over verbatim under (Ifix , g) 7→ (SHIV , gHIV ). What does
not carry over is independent branching near the observed threshold: the data favour a sigmoid
incidence such as
                                                        V
                                   f (V, T ) = γT V ·        ,                               (6.38)
                                                      KA + V

                                                169
inefficient at very low V and saturating to mass action for V ≫ KA . It is worth noting that this
is the opposite curvature at low V from the saturating alternatives catalogued in [65] — Holling
type 2, Beddington–DeAngelis, Crowley–Martin — which depress infection at high viral load;
the departure from mass action wanted here is an Allee effect at establishment, not saturation at
saturation. Under (6.38), R0 remains the large-population threshold while early establishment
is governed in addition by the scale KA , and proposition 6.2 no longer holds exactly, since a
nonlinear incidence breaks the moment closure.

6.7.4    Where the reach stops
What carries over to any single-cell mechanism is (6.11), the map peff = ge/S,   e the reading of
constant p as an exponential-phase projection, and the generation-kernel formulation of R0 and
r. What does not is everything resting on the closed forms of the birth–death–catastrophe
process: the flooding criterion 1/δ < 1/λ + 1/µ, the geometric burst law and its identification
with the quasi-stationary distribution, and one-shot cumulative release as the only single-cell
trajectory. For HIV in particular, independent branching is not the full establishment story near
the observed threshold; the flooding comparison survives as a linear baseline, at matched mean
yield with offspring variances compared, but synergy can dominate variance effects there and
the two mechanisms must be separated before either is invoked.


6.8     Discussion
This chapter took a completed stochastic single-cell theory and embedded it in a population.
What came out is a renewal model of within-host infection forced by the intracellular process
and exact in its kernels; the demonstration that the standard model is a projection of it, valid
one
  
    exponential phase at a time; an effective-parameter map whose two ends differ by the factor
E X 2 QS ; and a closed-form answer to whether bursting pays, in which the flooding parameter
L = a(1 − b) decides the sign and (L − 1)(1/m − 1) gives the size.

6.8.1    Consequences for fitting practice
Three results bear on how within-host models are fitted, and each says what to do rather than
only what is true.

A fitted p is a property of the phase. Acute-phase and set-point estimates should therefore
differ systematically, in a direction and by an amount (6.13) specifies. Two published estimates
of p for the same organism at different phases arenotin conflict, and the ratio between them is
itself an observable: by (6.20) it cannot exceed E X 2 QS , so a reported spread larger than that
is evidence against the intracellular model rather than against either fit. There is precedent for
the general shape of this argument. Hepatitis C clearance was estimated at c = 6.2/day from
interferon-α trials [82] and at c = 23.3/day from direct-acting antiviral data [70] — close to a
fourfold spread in a rate that cannot depend on the therapy used to measure it. The resolution
was not to adjudicate between the fits but to add intracellular structure, after which the two
biphasic declines turn out to be reporting different pairs of underlying rates: under interferon-α
the slopes are the intracellular loss rate and the infected-cell death rate, and neither is c, whereas
under direct-acting antivirals the fast slope is c after all [71]. The earlier figure was not a bad
measurement of clearance; it was a good measurement of something else. That is the same error
section 6.5 identifies for p, and it is the general lesson: a slope read off a transient is not a rate
of the organism until a model says which rate it is.




                                                 170
The eclipse may be being fitted twice. The lag that within-host models insert by hand
emerges here from the intracellular dynamics with no extra parameter, its scale set by E[Tprod ].
A model already carrying a fitted eclipse and a bursting pathogen is therefore fitting the same
delay twice, and the two estimates should be checked against each other before either is trusted.

A population fit needs a single-cell companion. No fit of (p, dI ) recovers the three
intracellular rates (section 6.5.6), so a population fit is worth doing only alongside one of the
three single-cell observables named there. With any one of them the system is over-determined
and the fit becomes a test of the intracellular model rather than a description of it. This is a
concrete instance of the general call in [65] for data collected at a finer scale of resolution to
validate complex models: the chapter names which three measurements would do it, and in what
order of experimental accessibility.

6.8.2   Limitations
The assumptions of section 6.1.1 are load-bearing. Holding target cells constant (A1) makes
the model linear, so supercritical solutions grow without bound and peak times exist only in
subcritical cases; depletion would cap them, but the linear analysis remains the right object for
establishment and early growth, where the chapter’s claims live. The continuum convention (A2)
is why R0 = γT V∞ /c sits alongside the branching mean m = qV∞ : they agree when γT ≪ c
and are otherwise different quantities, and confusing them would misread theorem 6.8. The
synchronous initial condition (A3) is not innocuous, since a non-trivial initial age distribution
adds a term to (6.9).
    Single infection (A4) is the most substantive restriction. Multiply infected cells release
                                         (k)
superlinearly more — (6.49) gives V∞ — and multiplicity is not rare: as many as a tenth
of HIV-infected cells are thought to be multiply infected, and coinfection shapes estimates of
infected-cell death and production, drug-resistance evolution and immune escape [65]. The
standard remedy adds compartments for doubly and triply infected cells, which the same review
notes is difficult to validate against plasma titres because of the parameter count. The MOI-k
row of table 6.4 suggests a cheaper route: multiplicity enters through the kernels I k − Dk , δKk
while the skeleton stays one-dimensional, adding no compartments and no fitted parameters.
Carrying those kernels through the population layer has not been done here, and it is the
most obvious extension. It would also connect to the alternative low-dimensional treatment of
coinfection due to Koelle et al. [83], whose macroparasite-style bookkeeping tracks the total
internalised virus across all cells — the quantity written Q in (6.37) — and which likewise
abandons the standard assumption that burst size is independent of the extent of coinfection.
Absence of spatial structure (A5) is the most consequential omission for a pathogen that spreads
cell to cell.
    Finally, the chapter is written in dimensionless rates with λ = 1 and does not say where a
real pathogen sits on figure 6.9. For Y. pestis in the macrophage the intracellular interval is
short and the biology resists a clean measurement: the bacterium enters a permissive vacuole
and replicates there while the temperature shift from the flea to the host induces the capsule,
the pH 6 antigen and the type III secretion system, and then leaves by a route the primary
literature still treats as unresolved, with apoptosis, pyroptosis and necroptosis all reported.
Placing that organism relative to L = 1 needs λ, µ and δ for the intracellular phase — and the
exit mechanism is exactly what fixes δ.




                                               171
6.8.3   Open problems
 1. Two-type killed second moments. The release kernel of a two-type renewal model needs
                     h              i                              h             i
                    E Xα2 1{τc > α} ,    E[Xα Yα 1{τc > α}] ,     E Yα2 1{τc > α} ,

    together with the two-type analogue of D. The moment hierarchy does not close upward,
    exactly as in one type; the one-type escape was to differentiate the Riccati equation, and
    the two-type route should be to differentiate the backward system of the two-type chapter.
    This is the main technical obstacle between this chapter and an eclipse-aware renewal model,
    and it should not be assumed easy.

 2. The flooding boundary against real parameters. Locate Y. pestis in the macrophage
    relative to δ(λ + µ) > λµ and compute the size of the advantage from (6.28). The estimates
    discussed in the chapter on its distribution theory — the near-certain bursting of F. tularensis
    [16], the clearance-dominated anthrax case — suggest real pathogens sit on both sides of
    the boundary, which is what makes the question worth asking.

 3. Population-level variance. Propagate Var(W ) through the branching structure to a
    whole-host prediction. For single-cell assays the first moments suffice; for whole-host data
    the variance is the main stochastic signal that standard BMVR cannot reproduce, and the
    integer-valued nature of the offspring count enters through the same quantity [55].

 4. Multiplicity through the population layer, and partial release: where in φ the
    flooding advantage survives.

   Two smaller questions are single definite statements rather than open directions. The
hypothesis of proposition 6.5 — that ρ = g/S increases with cell age — is a claim about the
one closed-form function (6.19) of one variable, so proving it is finite algebra; and the ordering
rbud > rburst is by proposition 6.10 equivalent to the single Laplace inequality (6.33), with the
coupling constants eliminated. Both are supported by wide numerical sweeps and neither is
proved.

6.8.4   Connections forward
The two-type process of the two-type chapter supplies the most immediate continuation, and
it is where the principal technical obstacle lives. In the regime δ1 = 0 < δ2 — no burst before
conversion — the no-catastrophe probability has zero initial hazard and then rises: precisely
the eclipse-phase signature that within-host models insert by hand, emerging mechanistically,
with the eclipse duration set by the conversion rate. Nothing in (6.11) need change to accept
those kernels when they exist. The bursting–budding comparison finds its evolutionary home
in the chapter on bursting and budding pathogenesis, where the intermediate mechanisms of
section 6.7 supply the phenotypes a selection argument needs. The nonlinear effects that the
later modelling chapters treat — immune saturation, local depletion, the all-run-out-at-once
intuition — are exactly what the branching comparison deliberately excludes, and should be
named wherever the flooding advantage is invoked.


6.A     Single-cell results quoted from earlier chapters
Write w = eθt . The roots of λf 2 − (λ + µ + δ)f + µ are a, b of (6.5), with b < 1 < a, and the
derived constants are

                                           175
Chapter 7

Type-specific catastrophe rates in a
two-type branching process:
an exact hypergeometric formula

      Abstract.
      For a continuous-time two-type birth–death process, we derive the exact finite-time probability
      that catastrophe has not yet occurred. Type 1 changes irreversibly into type 2, and an
      individual of either type can trigger a global absorbing catastrophe. Because the catastrophe
      rate depends on both populations along the path, the probability is an occupation-time
      transform. Its backward equations are triangular: the type-2 equation is autonomous, and
      substituting its solution into the type-1 equation leaves a Riccati equation. After linearisation,
      this becomes the Gauss hypergeometric equation. A Wronskian-normalised form keeps the
      coefficient enforcing the initial condition regular. The calculation also gives the probability of
      avoiding catastrophe forever and covers the zero-birth and zero-catastrophe-rate boundaries.
      With the appropriate initial data, the zero-catastrophe case recovers the two-type branching
      equations of Antal and Krapivsky. As one reading, the types may describe early and adapted
      intracellular phenotypes of Yersinia pestis, with catastrophe representing irreversible loss of
      macrophage containment. The formula is exact for any type-specific rate ordering, without
      an asymptotic or small-rate approximation.




7.1     Introduction
A population can disappear by ordinary deaths. It can also end for a different reason: one
individual may trigger an event that stops the whole process. That event might be detection,
intervention, or some other absorbing mechanism; the physical name depends on the application.
The mathematical question is simpler: if individuals of two types contribute different amounts
to the instantaneous catastrophe rate, what is the probability that the event has not happened
by a finite time? The answer depends on how long each type is present, not only on the terminal
counts, so that a lineage which grew early and then died can contribute more catastrophe
exposure than a small lineage which remains alive. The natural object is therefore an average of
exponential weights for total type-specific exposure: an occupation-time transform, with one
weight for each type. Because any individual can trigger the same absorbing event, the model
keeps a branching mechanism while the observable is global.
   A concrete setting is the early intracellular phase of Yersinia pestis within macrophages.
Under that reading, type 1 is an early intracellular state, conversion is progressive physiological
adaptation rather than a genetic mutation, and type 2 is a later adapted state; catastrophe stands
for irreversible loss of intracellular containment (macrophage burst, release of the bacterial load,

                                                    176
or another terminal failure of the compartment), as distinct from ordinary bacterial death. The
adapted phase could be more strongly associated with that endpoint, and unequal type-specific
rates then let us compare that idea with alternatives; the biological background and the regimes
are in section 7.5. The exact avoidance probability simply asks how long containment can persist
under any such mechanism.
   The underlying population process is the familiar two-type birth–death model with one-way
conversion. A type-1 individual can give birth, die, or change into type 2; a type-2 individual
can give birth or die. Multitype branching processes provide the general setting [86]. Antal and
Krapivsky obtained an exact finite-time generating function for this triangular two-type process
and used the hypergeometric structure created by its backward equations [87]. Their calculation
follows terminal population counts; they obtain a pair of probability generatng functions. Here
we keep the same triangular structure and add type-specific routes to an absorbing catastrophe.
The shared skeleton and the precise equation-level map to their system are set out in section 7.D;
the short point is that their object and ours differ, so setting δ1 = δ2 = 0 in the present
initial-value problem does not recover their generating functions.
   The extension gives the following two coupled nonlinear equations, derived in (7.3a) and
(7.3b):

                          dS
                             = λ1 S 2 − (λ1 + µ1 + ν + δ1 )S + µ1 + νG,
                          dt
                          dG
                             = λ2 G2 − (λ2 + µ2 + δ2 )G + µ2 ,
                          dt
with S(0) = G(0) = 1. The second equation is autonomous, which is why an exact calculation
remains possible.
   The derivation proceeds as follows.
 • Solve the type-2 equation as a rational function of an exponentially decaying variable.
 • Substitute that solution into the type-1 equation, leaving a non-autonomous Riccati equation.
 • Shift by the long-time type-1 root, then linearise the Riccati equation by a logarithmic
   derivative.
 • Change independent variable to the type-2 coordinate z2 , so that the linear equation becomes
   the Gauss hypergeometric equation.
The special function is forced by the reduction: it appears because the type-2 probability has
one moving rational pole.
   The principal result is a closed finite-time expression for the probability of avoiding catastrophe
from one initial type-1 individual, with unequal type-specific catastrophe rates allowed. We also
obtain the corresponding type-2 probability, identify both infinite-time limits, and treat the
boundaries where either birth rate vanishes or type 2 cannot cause catastrophe. The calculation
is exact within the model; what catastrophe means depends on the application. One reading,
developed in section 7.5, takes early and adapted intracellular phenotypes of Y. pestis, with
containment failure as the absorbing event. The formulae themselves do not depend on that
reading.
   The paper is organised around the derivation. Section 7.2 defines the process, gives its
occupation-time representation, and derives the backward equations. Section 7.3 solves the
autonomous equation. The hypergeometric reduction and the main formula appear in section 7.4,
which closes with a short mathematical interpretation. Section 7.5 then develops an intracellular
application and compares catastrophe-rate regimes. Boundary cases and zero-birth formulae are
collected in section 7.C; the map to Antal–Krapivsky is section 7.D. A short conclusion and four
technical appendices complete the paper.


                                                 177
7.2     Model, assumptions, and backward equations
7.2.1    The two-type process
We begin with the process before catastrophe. Let Xt and Yt denote the numbers of type-1
and type-2 individuals. A type-1 individual gives birth at rate λ1 , dies at rate µ1 , and changes
irreversibly into type 2 at rate ν. A type-2 individual gives birth at rate λ2 and dies at rate µ2 .
All these rates are non-negative.
   Catastrophe is a single global absorbing event. When the population is (x, y), its instantaneous
rate is
                                             δ1 x + δ2 y,                                      (7.1)
where δ1 , δ2 ≥ 0. Thus one individual contributes a catastrophe rate determined by its type, but
the first trigger sends the complete process to an absorbing state, denoted by (R, R).

Definition 7.1 (Birth–death–catastrophe process). Before absorption, the non-zero transition
rates from (x, y) ∈ Z2≥0 are

                 (x, y) −→ (x + 1, y)          at rate     λ1 x,          (birth)
                 (x, y) −→ (x − 1, y)          at rate     µ1 x,          (death)
                 (x, y) −→ (x − 1, y + 1)      at rate     νx,            (conversion)
                 (x, y) −→ (x, y + 1)          at rate     λ2 y,          (birth)
                 (x, y) −→ (x, y − 1)          at rate     µ2 y,          (death)
                 (x, y) −→ (R, R)              at rate     δ1 x + δ2 y.   (catastrophe)

The state (R, R) is absorbing.

  Write τc for the catastrophe time. We need two probabilities because the initial type matters:

                                        S(t) = P(1,0) {τc > t},
                                        G(t) = P(0,1) {τc > t}.

They satisfy S(0) = G(0) = 1. These are no-catastrophe probabilities rather than population-
survival probabilities: an ordinary death may remove the final individual without causing
catastrophe, and such a path continues to count as catastrophe-free.

7.2.2    Occupation-time representation
The same probabilities admit a second description that keeps ordinary extinction separate from
catastrophe. Imagine first generating only the birth–death–conversion history (Xs , Ys )0≤s≤t ,
as if the absorbing catastrophe jump were switched off. Each such history is one population
path: a full record of how many type-1 and type-2 individuals are present at every time. Along
that record, catastrophe is restored as a Poisson clock of rate λ(s) = δ1 Xs + δ2 Ys . Over a short
interval of length ∆t the chance of no trigger is about 1 − λ(s)∆t. The chance of no trigger on
the whole of [0, t] is therefore a product of such factors, one per bin of a fine partition. As the
mesh tends to zero that product becomes an exponential of a sum, and the sum becomes an
integral (see section 7.A):
                           n   Z t        o        n     Z t                   o
                                                                           
                       exp −         λ(s) ds = exp −           δ1 Xs + δ2 Ys ds .
                                 0                        0

Thus, given the path, this is the probability that the clock has not fired by time t. The
expectation then averages that pathwise probability over random branching histories, recovering
P{τc > t}.

                                                 Xt type 1 · Yt type 2

Figure 7.1: Two-type birth–death–conversion with a single global absorbing catastrophe. Type 1
(blue) gives birth at rate λ1 , dies at µ1 , and converts irreversibly to type 2 (vermillion) at rate ν;
type 2 gives birth at λ2 and dies at µ2 . In state (x, y) catastrophe fires at rate δ1 x + δ2 y and
absorbs the process in (R, R). S(t) and G(t) are the probabilities that this has not occurred by
time t, from one type-1 or type-2 individual respectively.


Proposition 7.2 (Occupation-time transform). For the corresponding catastrophe-free branching
process,
                                                          Z t                     
                                  S(t) = E(1,0) exp −             (δ1 Xs + δ2 Ys ) ds     ,                   (7.2a)
                                                             0
                                                          Z t                     
                                  G(t) = E(0,1) exp −             (δ1 Xs + δ2 Ys ) ds     .                   (7.2b)
                                                             0

Consequently S and G lie in [0, 1] and are non-increasing in time.

Proof. Conditional on a path, (7.1) is the intensity of an inhomogeneous Poisson clock, whose survival
probability is the displayed exponential. Taking the expectation over the branching path gives (7.2). The
bounds and monotonicity hold pathwise, hence also after averaging.
   A lineage may die out by ordinary deaths and still contribute a positive weight to S(t) or
G(t) if the catastrophe clock never rang. The calculation below uses backward equations, but
this is the object those equations compute.

7.2.3    First-event calculation
The backward equations follow from the first event in a short interval ∆t. Start with one type-1
individual. With high probability nothing happens and the no-catastrophe probability from
that state remains S(t). A birth produces two independent type-1 lineages, so the contribution
is S(t)2 . An ordinary death leaves no one, and an empty population contributes probability
one of avoiding future catastrophe. Conversion replaces the type-1 lineage by a type-2 lineage
and contributes G(t). Catastrophe in the interval means the event being measured has already

                         dS

with S(0) = G(0) = 1. Immediately,
                                  S ′ (0) = −δ1 ,             G′ (0) = −δ2 .                                               (7.4)
The equations therefore reproduce the initial catastrophe rates before any birth, death, or
conversion has had time to occur.

7.2.4    Dimensionless form
It is convenient to measure time in units of the type-1 birth timescale. We therefore assume
from this point that
                                           λ1 > 0,                                       (7.5)
and set t̂ = λ1 t. Define
                                    b t̂) = S(t̂/λ1 ),
                                    S(                              b t̂) = G(t̂/λ1 ),
                                                                    G(
together with
                                    µi                ν                     δi                   λ2
                            µ̂i =      ,       ν̂ =      ,          δ̂i =      ,         λ̂2 =      .                (7.6)
                                    λ1                λ1                    λ1                   λ1
Every rate is now a dimensionless ratio to λ1 , and the type-1 birth coefficient in the scaled
equations is one. With primes denoting differentiation in t̂, the system becomes

               Sb′ = Sb2 − κ̂1 Sb + µ̂1 + ν̂ G,
                                             b                                         κ̂1 = 1 + µ̂1 + ν̂ + δ̂1 ,   (7.7a)
                         b 2 − (λ̂2 + µ̂2 + δ̂2 )G
               b ′ = λ̂2 G
               G                                 b + µ̂2 .                                                          (7.7b)

   For the main formula we take λ̂2 > 0, δ̂2 > 0, and ν̂ > 0. The cases λ1 = 0, λ̂2 = 0, δ̂2 = 0,
and ν̂ = 0 are separated in section 7.C. Those boundaries matter: the scaling or the coordinate
used in the hypergeometric reduction degenerates there, even though the original unscaled
initial-value problem remains regular.


7.3     The autonomous type-2 probability
The second equation in (7.7) does not depend on b S: it is autonomous. This is the small structural
fact on which the full calculation rests. We solve it first: factor the right-hand side, introduce a
decaying coordinate z2 , and write Gb as a rational function of z2 . That rational time dependence
will then drive the type-1 equation.
   Constant solutions of (7.7b) satisfy a quadratic equation. Setting

7.4                                           Hypergeometric reduction and main result
With the type-2 solution in hand, we substitute (7.13) into the equation for S.
                                                                             b The result is a
forced Riccati equation whose time dependence occurs only through z2 /(1 − z2 ). We shift by
a long-time root, linearise with the standard Riccati trick, change independent variable to z2 ,
match the Gauss hypergeometric equation, and fix the free mix of solutions by S(0)
                                                                                b    = 1. The
method is inspired by that of Antal and Krapivsky; the subsections below present each of the
steps.

7.4.1                                           Selecting the stable constant part
As t̂ → ∞, we have G  b → r2,− . With type 2 settled, the equation for Sb becomes autonomous,
so any long-time limit of Sb must be a constant solution of the algebraic equation obtained by
setting Sb′ = 0 with G
                     b = r2,− . That constant is a long-time root of the limiting type-1 equation.

depends on Z only through the logarithmic derivative Z ′ /Z, any overall multiplicative constant
in Z cancels from the probability.

7.4.2.1    Change of independent variable

All remaining time dependence in (7.18) sits inside z2 (t̂): the coefficient is z2 /(1 − z2 ), and z2
itself is a known exponential function of t̂. Measuring “time” by z2 rather than by t̂ turns a
linear ODE with exponential-looking coefficients into the Gauss hypergeometric equation, whose
solutions are known.
   Since z2′ = q̂2 z2 and z2 is monotone, write

                                             Z(t̂) = Φ(z2 (t̂)).

If primes on Φ mean differentiation with respect to z2 , the chain rule gives

                                                Z ′ = q̂2 z2 Φ′ ,                               (7.19)

and then                                                                
                                        Z ′′ = q̂22 z22 Φ′′ + z2 Φ′ .                           (7.20)
Substitute (7.19) and (7.20) into (7.18). For every finite t̂, z2 ̸= 0, so we may divide by q̂22 z2 and
obtain the intermediate form

7.4.2.2    Matching to the Gauss equation

Equation (7.21) is an instance of the canonical Gauss hypergeometric equation

                        z(1 − z)Φ′′ + [C − (A + B + 1)z]Φ′ − ABΦ = 0.

When C ∈
       / Z, its standard fundamental solutions are

                       Φ1 (z) = 2 F1 (A, B; C; z),                                         (7.23a)
                       Φ2 (z) = z 1−C 2 F1 (A − C + 1, B − C + 1; 2 − C; z).               (7.23b)

Every solution is then a linear combination Φ = c1 Φ1 + c2 Φ2 . The parameters A, B, and C are
fixed by matching coefficients with (7.21). Comparing the coefficient of Φ′ already gave C in
(7.22). The remaining matches are

                                                  κ̂1 − 2h                      ν̂α2
                          A+B =C −1=                       ,           AB =           .     (7.24)
                                                      q̂2                        q̂22

Thus A and B are the two roots of one quadratic:
                                                     s
                                        C −1             (C − 1)2 ν̂α2
                                 A, B =      ±                   − 2 .                      (7.25)
                                          2                 4      q̂2

They need not be individually real. If the discriminant is negative, they form a complex-conjugate
pair; the differential equation and its physical boundary data remain real. At this point the
differential equation for Φ is completely specified. What remains is to rebuild the probability
and fix the free mix of the two solutions.

7.4.3     The closed formula
7.4.3.1    Rebuilding Sb from Φ

We first reverse the substitutions, before choosing a basis for Φ. From

                                                          Z′
                              Sb = h + X,       X=−          ,         Z = Φ(z2 ),
                                                          Z
the chain rule (7.19) gives
                                                               Φ′ (z2 (t̂))
                                     b t̂) = h − q̂2 z2 (t̂)
                                     S(                                     .               (7.26)
                                                               Φ(z2 (t̂))
The probability depends on Φ only through the logarithmic derivative Φ′ /Φ. Any overall
multiplicative constant in Φ cancels, as already noted for Z.

7.4.3.2    Ansatz for Φ: the two-dimensional solution space

Equation (7.21) is a second-order linear ODE, so its solution space is two-dimensional. We do
not guess Φ as a special function of the probability; we take the general solution of the equation
we have derived. For C ∈ / Z that general solution is any mix of the standard pair (7.23).
   Our path has z2 (t̂) < 0 for all finite t̂, so we evaluate everything on the negative real axis.
The textbook second solution uses the factor z 1−C , which need not stay real for z < 0 and real
non-integer C. On the same path, −z > 0, so we replace that factor by (−z)1−C and work with
the real-axis pair

                     U (z) = 2 F1 (A, B; C; z),                                            (7.27a)


                                                    187
                      V (z) = (−z)1−C 2 F1 (A − C + 1, B − C + 1; 2 − C; z).                      (7.27b)

Here U is exactly Φ1 , while V plays the role of Φ2 in a form that stays real for real C whenever
−z > 0. Thus U and V are two independent real solutions of the hypergeometric ODE on the
negative axis where z2 lives. Any solution Φ is a mix of them,

                                       Φ(z) = c1 U (z) + c2 V (z),

with two coefficients c1 , c2 . Integer C does not change that strategy; it only changes how one
writes the second basis function (a logarithm may appear), as discussed in section 7.B.
   Physics fixes only the mix of the two solutions, not the overall scale of Φ. From (7.26), Sb
depends on Φ only through Φ′ /Φ. Scaling both coefficients by the same nonzero factor,

                                         (c1 , c2 ) 7−→ (λc1 , λc2 ),

leaves Φ′ /Φ unchanged, and therefore leaves Sb unchanged. The overall scale of Φ is free: we
never need absolute values of both coefficients. What we do need is the right mix, equivalently
the ratio c2 /c1 .

7.4.3.3    Initial condition and the constant L

That mix is fixed by the single initial condition S(0)
                                                    b      = 1. At t̂ = 0 we have z2 = z2,0 . Inserting
S(0) = 1 into (7.26) gives
b
                                                       Φ′ (z2,0 )
                                      1 = h − q̂2 z2,0            ,
                                                       Φ(z2,0 )
and therefore
                                    Φ′ (z2,0 )                   h−1
                                               = L,        L=             .                        (7.28)
                                    Φ(z2,0 )                     q̂2 z2,0
There is only one physical condition on Φ: its logarithmic derivative at the starting point z2,0
must equal the number L. There is not a second condition that pins down c1 and c2 separately.

7.4.3.4    Choosing the mix: coefficients KU and KV

It remains only to pick one concrete mix that satisfies (7.28). We write the same linear
combination with names KU , KV instead of c1 , c2 :

                                     Φ(z) = KU U (z) + KV V (z).                                   (7.29)

These are simply a particular choice of (c1 , c2 ), not new unknowns. Write

                  U0 = U (z2,0 ),   V0 = V (z2,0 ),     U0′ = U ′ (z2,0 ),    V0′ = V ′ (z2,0 )

for the basis values at the initial coordinate. We take a convenient Wronskian normalisation,

                                            KU = V0′ − LV0 ,                                      (7.30a)
                                            KV = LU0 − U0′ .                                      (7.30b)

This is a standard trick for a linear second-order equation. Once a fundamental pair is fixed,
the two coefficients above are chosen so that Φ′ /Φ = L at z2,0 ; multiplying both by the same
nonzero constant does not change Φ′ /Φ, so the overall scale of (KU , KV ) is free. Only the origin
of L is model-specific. At that point one has

                     Φ(z2,0 ) = U0 V0′ − U0′ V0 ,      Φ′ (z2,0 ) = L(U0 V0′ − U0′ V0 ).


                                                    188
The common factor is the Wronskian of (U, V ) at z2,0 , which is nonzero for C ∈       / Z. Hence
Φ(z2,0 ) ̸= 0 and Φ (z2,0 )/Φ(z2,0 ) = L holds at once. Any other multiple (λKU , λKV ) would work
                   ′

equally well for S;
                  b the formulae above just pick one scale for us, without hand-solving for a ratio.
  The same mix may equally be written Φ = U + DV with a single ratio

                                                 KV  LU0 − U0′
                                        D=          = ′        ,
                                                 KU  V0 − LV0

whenever KU ̸= 0. In the present probability regime that is automatic: a pure-V solution would
tend, as z → 0− , to a value strictly larger than one, contradicting Sb ≤ 1 from proposition 7.2.

7.4.3.5   Main formula

  Theorem 7.4 (Exact finite-time no-catastrophe probability). Assume λ1 > 0, λ̂2 > 0,
  δ̂2 > 0, ν̂ > 0, and C ∈
                         / Z. With the quantities of this section defined as above, then

  solves (7.7a) with S(0)
                      b    = 1. Together with (7.13), it gives both finite-time no-catastrophe
  probabilities. In physical time, after every hatted rate is replaced by the ratio in (7.6),

                                          KU U ′ (z2 (λ1 t)) + KV V ′ (z2 (λ1 t))
                      S(t) = h − q̂2 z2 (λ1 t)                                    ,

  A compact nested evaluation map for every constant appears later in this section (remark 7.6).


    As t̂ → ∞, one has z2 → 0 and therefore G
                                            b → r2,− and Sb → h. These limits are the probabil-
ities of avoiding catastrophe forever from one initial type-2 or type-1 individual, respectively.
Checks of the initial slopes and degenerate parameter boundaries are collected in section 7.C.
    The same result can be written as a logarithmic derivative,

7.4.4    Mathematical interpretation
The formula in (7.31) is exact within the model: it is the finite-time no-catastrophe
                                                                        R         R 
                                                                                       probability
and, by proposition 7.2, the occupation-time average of exp −δ1 X − δ2 Y . The two
catastrophe rates do not enter symmetrically. The type-2 rate δ̂2 reshapes the roots r2,± , the
coordinate z2 , and the whole function G, b which then drives the type-1 equation; the type-1 rate
δ̂1 enters only through κ̂1 , so it changes h and the hypergeometric parameters A, B, C without
feeding back into bG. That asymmetry is forced by one-way conversion.
    We next focus on one potential application of the model: early and adapted intracellular
phenotypes of Y. pestis, with catastrophe as irreversible loss of host-cell containment.


7.5     Intracellular application: comparing catastrophe-rate regimes
The formula of section 7.4 holds for any non-negative pair of type specific catastrophe rates
(δ1 , δ2 ), and the two rates enter it asymmetrically: δ2 reshapes the autonomous type-2 coordinate
that drives the whole system, while δ1 does not feed back into G. That asymmetry is not only
algebraic. In physical applications, there may be cases where two types lead to a global absorbing
event at differing rates. In the case of Yersinia pestis, it is scientifically unsettled whether an
early or a later bacterial state is more associated with lysis of their host cells. We will explore
that application in this section by fixing a reading of the model’s components, and use the exact
expression to compare four orderings of (δ1 , δ2 ). The aim is not to determine which case is likely
to represent physical reality, but to show what an exact finite-time probability makes visible
within the context of this modelling regime.

7.5.1    Intracellular mapping and scope
Throughout this section the unit of analysis is a single host cell with a resident bacterial population.
Catastrophe means irreversible loss of intracellular containment (burst, lysis, terminal death with
release, or another failure of the compartment). It is distinct from ordinary bacterial death of
individual cells, which is already encoded by the per-capita rates µi . Lung-level spread, immune
recruitment, transmission, and the extracellular phase after τc lie outside the process.


                                                             191
  Table 7.1: Reading of mathematical components in an early intracellular infection setting.
   Model component                    Biological meaning under this reading
   Xt (type 1)                        Early or incompletely adapted intracellular phenotype
   Yt (type 2)                        Later mammalian-adapted intracellular phenotype
   Births (λi )                       Intracellular replication of that phenotype
   Deaths (µi )                       Ordinary bacterial killing or non-replicative loss
   Conversion (ν)                     Progressive physiological adaptation (one-way)
   Catastrophe rates (δi )            Per-capita association with containment failure
   Absorbing state                    Irreversible loss of intracellular containment
   Termination at τc                  End of the intracellular process for this cell

   Table 7.1 is the map used below. The coefficients δi are effective per-capita associations with
containment failure under a linear additive intensity δ1 Xt + δ2 Yt ; they are not molecular claims
that every adapted bacterium has completed effector injection.
   A short biological rationale is enough to make the map usable. In mammalian hosts Y.
pestis typically begins with an intracellular phase in which a subset of the initial inoculum is
taken up by, and survives within, macrophages. Extracellular proliferation follows only after
a period of intracellular adaptation. During a time within the relatively protected niche, the
bacterium remodels its surface proteins under host temperature and phagosomal stress [89, 7, 5].
Newly internalised bacteria occupy a specialised vacuole, survive and replicate, and reprogramme
toward the temperature-induced virulence package of F1 capsule, pH 6 antigen (Psa), and the
Ysc–Yop type III secretion system that defines a later anti-phagocytic bacterial phase. Once that
programme is in place, adapted bacteria leave the intracellular niche and grow freely outside
the cell, resisting re-uptake. The model coarse-grains this continuous intracellular maturation
into two states linked by one-way conversion: type 1 represents the early, incompletely adapted
bacterial phase; type 2 the later conditioned bacterial phase; conversion ν the arming step
between them; and catastrophe the irreversible loss of host-cell containment, the release event
that ends the single-cell process and opens extracellular outgrowth.




   How the intracellular phase ends remains incompletely resolved: host-cell death programmes
and timing depend on strain, host background, and infection conditions [10, 9]. Pathogenic
yersiniae can kill macrophages; the best-characterised genetically supported link is apoptosis
driven by the type III secretion system (T3SS) effector YopJ [8], and components of the
temperature-induced virulence package associated with a later anti-phagocytic phenotype may
raise the per-capita association with containment failure after intracellular conditioning [9, 6].
That supplies a working hypothesis for a maturation-associated ordering of (δ1 , δ2 ), not a theorem

                                                192
of active type-2-driven rupture. Survival and replication within macrophages can, moreover, be
established without treating the T3SS as the main intracellular survival engine [89, 6]. Holding
(λi , µi , ν) fixed while comparing (δ1 , δ2 ) is therefore a controlled mathematical comparison that
is compatible with that literature, rather than a claim of full biological independence between
growth and catastrophe.

7.5.2    Four catastrophe-rate regimes
Unequal (δ1 , δ2 ) encode different ideas about whether adaptation advances, delays, or leaves
unchanged the end of the intracellular phase. We label four regimes for reference. When they
are compared later they share the same growth and conversion rates.

 • EQ. δ1 = δ2 = δ > 0: phenotype-blind per-capita rate (null: failure tracks total load rather
   than adapted-state machinery).

 • MAT. 0 < δ1 < δ2 : higher adapted-type rate (maturation-associated). Working hypothesis
   under the reading above: adaptation raises failure association while residual early risk
   remains nonzero.

 • GATE. δ1 = 0 < δ2 : idealised type-2-only extreme, so failure is possible only after conversion
   and S ′ (0) = 0. Pure GATE may overstate exclusivity of type-2 contribution, because efficient
   Yop injection is often studied for extracellular bacteria contacting the plasma membrane,
   whereas bacteria sealed in an intact vacuole may not have the same access.

 • EARLY. δ1 > δ2 ≥ 0 (illustrations take δ2 > 0): higher early-type rate, so adaptation may
   prolong containment (reverse ordering).

   Which ordering holds is an empirical question. Under the illustrative normalisation δ1 + δ2 = c
used in figure 7.10, GATE sits at (0, c) while MAT uses (δ1 , c − δ1 ) with δ1 > 0; both coefficients
differ, so GATE is the type-2-only boundary of that family with S ′ (0) = 0, not simply “MAT
with δ1 set to zero”.

7.5.3    Exact comparison and limits of inference
From one early founder, S(t) = P(1,0) {τc > t} is the probability that containment still holds at
time t. Its time derivative supplies the probability density of the catastrophe
                                                                            R∞
                                                                                time: f (t) = −S ′ (t)
is the unconditional rate at which still-open infections fail at age t, and 0 f = 1 − h whenever
a positive mass h = lim S never fails. Among infections that remain contained at t, the relevant
object is not f itself but the conditional intensity

                                                  f (t)    S ′ (t)
                                       qS (t) =         =−         .
                                                  S(t)     S(t)

Conditioning on {τc > t} discards paths that have already failed, so qS (t) ∆t is the short-time
probability of loss of containment in [t, t + ∆t] given that the host cell is still intact. The identity
S ′ (0) = −δ1 (equivalently qS (0) = δ1 ) is general mathematics: at the instant of founding there
is no selection among survivors yet, and the initial hazard is simply the type-1 catastrophe rate.
     Figure 7.10 compares EQ, MAT, GATE, and EARLY under shared growth rates and the
coefficient normalisation δ1 + δ2 = c. The parameters are illustrative, not fitted. Realised
process intensity remains δ1 Xt + δ2 Yt , so equalising the coefficient sum does not equalise realised
exposure. Under this normalisation the survival curves S(t) separate only modestly, whereas
the early hazard separates sharply because qS (0) = δ1 , and GATE’s zero intercept is forced by
S ′ (0) = 0. Later features of both panels are specific to the chosen rates. By one-way conversion,
G depends on δ2 alone, so an adapted-founder experiment isolates the type-2 channel in a way

                                                    193
 that S cannot. Changing ν can advance or shorten engagement of the higher-rate class depending
 on the ordering, so faster adaptation is not universally more hazardous; elevated risk may also
 reflect greater type-2 abundance through net growth rather than a larger intrinsic δ2 .

Figure 7.10: Competing catastrophe-rate regimes under an intracellular Y. pestis reading.
(A) Survival S(t) from one early founder; (B) conditional intensity qS = −S ′ /S. Shared growth
rates with δ1 + δ2 = c = 0.30 (coefficient normalisation, not matched realised exposure): EQ
(0.15, 0.15), MAT (0.05, 0.25), GATE (0, 0.30), EARLY (0.25, 0.05) (λ1 = 1, µ1 = 0.45, ν = 0.35,

solution path.

   The measurements that help most are joint and time-resolved intracellular burden, a phenotype
or adaptation marker, and host-cell fate, including the fraction of cells that clear without
containment failure. Failure times alone are likely under-identifying: there are trade-offs
among (λi , µi , ν, δi ), and survival curves need not separate intrinsic δ2 from type-2 abundance.
Even under MAT or GATE, agreement with data would support an association between a
latent adapted state and failure risk under the stated assumptions, not a proof that adapted
bacteria actively rupture the host cell. Alternatives include infection-age markers correlated
with burden, host-driven deterioration concurrent with maturation, or mechanisms outside a
two-type description. Beyond containment failure itself, the release distributions of section 7.E.4
and figure 7.13 record the pre-catastrophe load and offer a natural next link from these exact
laws toward host-level outcomes.


 7.6                      Conclusion
We set out the finite-time no-catastrophe probability for a two-type population in which either
type may trigger one global absorbing event. Because the intensity depends on the whole path,
the natural object is an occupation-time transform of the two type abundances rather than a
terminal-count generating function alone (section 7.2).
   The calculation is exact because the backward system is triangular. Solving for G, shifting
S by its long-time root, linearising the Riccati equation, and changing variable to z2 produces
the Gauss hypergeometric equation and the boxed formula (7.31). The type-2 catastrophe
rate reshapes the autonomous coordinate seen by the whole system; the type-1 rate does not
feed back into G. The limits h and r2,− are forever-avoidance probabilities. Equal rates and
zero-birth boundaries are recorded in section 7.C; the equation-level map to Antal and Krapivsky
is section 7.D.
   One natural reading is the early intracellular phase of Yersinia pestis: early and adapted

                                                                                              194
phenotypes, with catastrophe as irreversible loss of host-cell containment. The regimes of
section 7.5 then compare orderings of (δ1 , δ2 ).
   Beyond the work of this paper, one might consider the release distributions (section 7.E.4
and figure 7.13) more closely and their implications for a broad infection-success probability
within the host. Analysis along these lines may be capable of contributing a quantitative
justification for a rate hypothesis favouring MAT/GATE in which the second phase of Y. pestis
actively triggers burst of the containing cell.


7.A        From product of survival factors to the pathwise exponential
Along a fixed path the catastrophe clock has instantaneous rate λ(s). Over a short bin of length
∆t the chance of no trigger is about 1 − λ(s)∆t. Partitioning [0, t] into n equal bins of width t/n,
the chance of no trigger on the whole interval is a product of n such factors. In the constant-rate
case the elementary limit              
                                      λt n
                                 1−         −→ e−λt      (n → ∞)
                                      n
turns that product into an exponential. With a slowly varying rate the same mesh argument
yields                      Z                    Z
                                 n          t        o        n        t                  o
                             exp −              λ(s) ds = exp −            δ1 Xs + δ2 Ys ds ,
                                        0                          0
which is the pathwise weight averaged in proposition 7.2.


7.B        Hypergeometric derivatives and resonant parameters
The derivative of the first basis function follows from the standard identity

   For the second basis function, set

                     A2 = A − C + 1,                     B2 = B − C + 1,           C2 = 2 − C.

The product rule gives
                         "                                                                       #
                   1−C       1−C                           A2 B 2

Equations (7.33) and (7.34) show that the main result uses only shifted Gauss hypergeometric
functions and elementary factors.
   When C ∈ Z, the pair in (7.27) is not generally a fundamental pair because the two Frobenius
exponents differ by an integer. A logarithmic second solution can be constructed in the usual
way, or the physical solution can be obtained as a continuous limit from nearby noninteger C.
The probability itself is fixed by the regular initial-value problem (7.7); only this convenient
basis changes. In computation, direct ODE integration near resonance is a useful independent
check.




                                                            195
7.C      Checks, limits, and the earlier branching model
This appendix records checks and degenerate cases omitted from the main text. We return to
the probability first, then to several parameter limits, because a special-function expression is of
limited use if its boundaries are unclear. Some limits are smooth inside (7.31); others are better
solved before the hypergeometric coordinate is introduced.

7.C.1    Initial and infinite-time behaviour
At t̂ = 0, the Wronskian construction in (7.29) and (7.30) forces the logarithmic derivative in
(7.31) to equal L. Consequently bS(0) = 1. Differentiation of the backward equations then gives
the scaled initial slopes
                                Sb′ (0) = −δ̂1 ,  Gb ′ (0) = −δ̂2 ,

in agreement with (7.4).
   As t̂ grows, z2 (t̂) tends to zero and G
                                          b tends to r2,− . The occupation-time representation
shows that both probabilities are monotone and hence have limits. Passing to the limit in the
type-1 equation with Sb′ → 0 forces Sb to a root of (7.14). The upper root exceeds one, while the
physical solution is bounded by one, so the long-time value must be the lower root h. Hence we
obtain a useful corollary.
Corollary 7.7 (Avoiding catastrophe forever). Under the assumptions of theorem 7.4,

                               lim G(t) = r2,− ,           lim S(t) = h.
                               t→∞                         t→∞

These are the probabilities that catastrophe never occurs from one initial individual of the
corresponding type.

   A birth process can avoid catastrophe forever with positive probability for an ordinary reason:
a lineage may die out before accumulating enough exposure to trigger the absorbing event.

7.C.2    Equal catastrophe rates
If δ1 = δ2 = δ, then δ̂1 = δ̂2 . The derivation is unchanged. The common rate enters the type-2
roots through δ̂2 and the type-1 stable root through κ̂1 . Thus the unequal-rate result restricts
algebraically to the shared-rate model on the diagonal

                                               δ1 = δ2 .                                      (7.35)

We do not know of a separate published closed form for the equal-rate case against which a
term-by-term expansion could be checked. What can be verified is consistency: under (7.35) the
governing equations and every derived parameter reduce as expected.

7.C.3    Type 2 cannot trigger catastrophe
Now take δ2 = 0. Starting from one type-2 individual, neither birth nor ordinary death creates
catastrophe, and type 2 has no route back to type 1. Therefore

                                              G(t) ≡ 1.

This is why the root coordinate of section 7.3 degenerates: the autonomous probability has
stopped moving.
   In scaled time, the first equation becomes

                                     Sb′ = Sb2 − κ̂1 Sb + µ̂1 + ν̂.                           (7.36)

7.C.4    No type-1 births
The scaling in (7.5) excludes λ1 = 0, although the original process does not. At this boundary
the type-1 equation is no longer Riccati. It is linear:

                                 S ′ = −(µ1 + ν + δ1 )S + µ1 + νG.

An integrating factor therefore gives the exact unscaled solution
                                                     Z t                                      
                             −(µ1 +ν+δ1 )t                    (µ1 +ν+δ1 )s               

Here G is the elementary solution of (7.3b); it has the root form of proposition 7.3 with unscaled
type-2 rates when λ2 , δ2 > 0, and the corresponding linear or constant boundary form otherwise.
Thus the absence of type-1 births is an exactly solvable degeneration, not a singularity of the
probability model.
