7.C.6     No conversion
When ν = 0, the two types do not communicate. Each no-catastrophe probability solves its
own one-type Riccati equation. The type-2 solution is already (7.13). For type 1, the same root
construction applies to
                               Sb′ = Sb2 − (1 + µ̂1 + δ̂1 )Sb + µ̂1 .
The hypergeometric equation is unnecessary in this case. The boundary can be obtained by
continuity from the central formula along non-degenerate parameter paths, but the direct scalar
solution is clearer and numerically safer.


7.D       Equation-level recovery of Antal–Krapivsky
This appendix records the precise relation to Antal and Krapivsky [87]. Their observable is
the generating function of the terminal population counts; ours is the probability that a global
catastrophe has not occurred. The branching skeleton (two types, one-way conversion, triangular
backward structure) is shared (figure 7.11).

                                                    198
                       Antal–Krapivsky                                                            This paper
                                                                                                    δ1 x
                  λ1                              λ2                             λ1                     λ2

                             conversion ν
                                                                                                                          Catastrophe
               Type 1                           Type 2                         Type 1                Type 2
                  Xt                              Yt                              x          ν          y                     (R, R)
                                                                                                                 δ2 y
                                                                                                                          rate δ1 x + δ2 y
              readout: generating function of final counts

                                                                                readout: probability the absorbing event has not fired
                                                                                       hazard clock integrated along the path




Figure 7.11: Antal–Krapivsky terminal-population generating functions (left) versus the no-
catastrophe probabilities studied here (right). Both use the same two-type one-way branching
skeleton. Left: A(x, y, t) = E[xXt y Yt ] with initial data A(x, y, 0) = x, B(x, y, 0) = y, and no
global absorbing catastrophe. Right: S(t) = P(1,0) (τc > t) and G(t) = P(0,1) (τc > t) with
S(0) = G(0) = 1 and catastrophe rate δ1 x + δ2 y.


   To make the equation-level map exact, set δ1 = δ2 = 0 and let A(x, y, t) and B(x, y, t) denote
the generating functions starting from one type-1 and one type-2 individual. Their initial data
are
                               A(x, y, 0) = x,    B(x, y, 0) = y,
rather than the survival values S(0) = G(0) = 1. The backward system is then

                                      ∂t A = λ1 A2 − (λ1 + µ1 + ν)A + µ1 + νB,
                                       ∂t B = λ2 B 2 − (λ2 + µ2 )B + µ2 .

Under the rate map
                                        αiAK = λi ,          βiAK = µi ,              ν AK = ν,
these are exactly the triangular equations used in that paper, before its normalisation α1AK = 1.
   If instead the catastrophe rates vanish while our survival data are kept, (7.38) follows and
both probabilities are identically one. Recovering the earlier governing system therefore requires
the generating-function initial data x, y; the no-catastrophe formula alone does not yield a
nontrivial terminal population distribution.


7.E      A catastrophe-aware population generating function
We can retain population-count information without removing catastrophe from the model. For
this purpose, use a killed, or defective, generating function. Its coefficients record the counts on
paths that have not yet reached catastrophe. For i = 1, 2, define
                                                                  h                           i
                                             F1 (x, y, t) = E(1,0) xXt y Yt 1{τc >t} ,                                                       (7.43a)
                                                                  h                           i
                                             F2 (x, y, t) = E(0,1) xXt y Yt 1{τc >t} .                                                       (7.43b)

The variable x plays no role in F2 , but we keep both arguments so that the definitions remain
parallel. We may also take the expectation over the catastrophe-free branching skeleton and
write                                           Z                      t
                           Fi (x, y, t) = Ei xXt y Yt exp −                    (δ1 Xs + δ2 Ys ) ds               .
                                                                       0
Hence,
                                  [xm y n ]Fi (x, y, t) = Pi {Xt = m, Yt = n, τc > t},


                                                                199
and the total masses give the no-catastrophe probabilities:

                              F1 (1, 1, t) = S(t),           F2 (1, 1, t) = G(t).

Thus these functions are generally subnormalised at (x, y) = (1, 1). Their mass deficit is the
probability that catastrophe has occurred by time t.

Proposition 7.10 (Backward system for the killed generating functions). The functions in
(7.43) satisfy

                         ∂t F1 = λ1 F12 − (λ1 + µ1 + ν + δ1 )F1 + µ1 + νF2 ,                      (7.44a)
                         ∂t F2 = λ2 F22 − (λ2 + µ2 + δ2 )F2 + µ2 ,                                (7.44b)

with
                                 F1 (x, y, 0) = x,           F2 (x, y, 0) = y.                     (7.45)

Proof. Condition on the first event, as in section 7.2. A birth produces two independent descendant
lineages, which gives Fi2 . Ordinary death leaves the empty population and contributes one, while
conversion replaces F1 by F2 . A catastrophe contributes zero. This last point explains why δi appears in
the negative linear coefficient but not in the constant term: the catastrophe rate cannot be absorbed into
the ordinary death rate.
   If δ1 = δ2 = 0, (7.44) and (7.45) becomes the Antal–Krapivsky generating-function system
recovered in section 7.D. With positive catastrophe rates it is instead a Feynman–Kac deformation
of that system, and its total mass need not equal one.

7.E.1     The absorbing state and conditional populations
To represent absorbed paths explicitly, let ρ mark the absorbing state R and set
                                                                                   
                            Hi (x, y, ρ, t) = Fi (x, y, t) + ρ 1 − Fi (1, 1, t) .                  (7.46)

The coefficient of ρ is the probability of catastrophe by time t. Hence,

                                             Hi (1, 1, 1, t) = 1.

Because the process stops at its first catastrophe, (7.46) is linear in ρ. After absorption there is
no finite pair of population counts.
  On paths that avoid catastrophe up to time t, the population PGF is

                                                         Fi (x, y, t)
                                        Qi (x, y, t) =                .                            (7.47)
                                                         Fi (1, 1, t)

For example, ∂x Qi (1, 1, t) and ∂y Qi (1, 1, t) are the conditional mean type-1 and type-2 counts.
To record the population just before catastrophe, use the flux generating function

                          Ji (x, y, t) = δ1 x ∂x Fi (x, y, t) + δ2 y ∂y Fi (x, y, t).

This follows coefficientwise:

                        [xm y n ]Ji = (δ1 m + δ2 n)Pi {Xt = m, Yt = n, τc > t},

the right-hand side is the time density of catastrophe from the pre-absorption state (m, n).
Summing over all counts gives

                            J1 (1, 1, t) = −S ′ (t),         J2 (1, 1, t) = −G′ (t).

   The exceptional value z2,0 (y) = 0 follows by continuity. It can also be obtained directly
because the type-2 solution is then constant. Extracting every coefficient with a bivariate Cauchy
integral requires complex x and y, so the second hypergeometric solution must use a consistent
complex branch. One can avoid this branch choice by integrating (7.44) directly on the contour.
Derivatives at (x, y) = (1, 1), including the conditional moments, stay on the original real branch.

Remark 7.11 (Generic regime versus boundaries). In the generic regime δ1 > 0, δ2 > 0, the
only way to have τc = ∞ is ordinary extinction to (0, 0) before the catastrophe clock fires. The
origin atom of (Xrel , Yrel ) is then exactly the forever-avoidance probability, and coincides with
clearance without release. On certain catastrophe-rate boundaries (for instance δ1 = 0), paths
with τc = ∞ need not be extinct; (7.51) still assigns (0, 0) on those paths, now meaning “no
release event” rather than “empty burst”. The formulae below remain valid on those boundaries;
only the biological reading of the origin atom changes.

                                                  204
Chapter 8

Bursting and budding pathogenesis

8.1     Introduction
Under linear clearance the schedule by which an infected cell releases its load is invisible to
the basic reproduction number: replacing constant budding by a single burst leaves R0 exactly
unchanged, a fact proved in the chapter on population dynamics (theorem 6.6). The schedule is
not invisible to establishment, but that comparison is again a comparison of branching structures
at fixed mean. This chapter asks whether a depletable defence changes either conclusion —
whether bursting can be an advantage, or a disadvantage, once the host’s capacity to kill is
finite.
   Two sites are treated. Inside the host cell, some leucocytes carry a finite store of oxidative
granules, spent as they act. Outside it, killer cells in the extracellular matrix remove free
agents on contact and are themselves used up. In both cases the per-agent hazard depends
on how many agents arrive at once. Constant budding, as used in the basic model of viral
replication [90, 12, 13], is an approximation that ignores that dependence; single-cell observations
of HIV release are already intermittent rather than constant [91, 28, 92]. Eclipse phases and
load-dependent production [93, 94, 95] address a different approximation, and are not pursued
here.
   Comparing bursting with budding is not new [27, 15, 96, 97, 98]. What is added is a
quantification of flooding — that a burst-style release is more likely to exhaust the local killing
capacity. The word is used below in two senses that must be kept apart — a comparison of two
offspring laws at one delivery, and a comparison of the same yield split across more deliveries —
and the two do not always agree.

8.1.1    Three candidate advantages
Is there a fundamental advantage to bursting dynamics in pathogenesis? For F. tularensis and
Y. pestis, host cell lysis may be no more than the simplest available means of release: bacteria
are large in comparison with the protein complexes that constitute a cell membrane, so it would
be difficult to employ a strategy in which some but not all of the pathogen were released. Viruses
are different. They are small enough to allow partial release of an intracellular load, and for
viruses such as HIV there is an explicit advantage to not killing the cell, since its machinery
continues to produce virions. That an all-at-once strategy nonetheless persists is what needs
explaining, and the explanations on offer fall into three categories.

Subductive proliferation. Replication rate declines over time within a host cell, perhaps
  following recognition, or as the resources available there are used up.
Limited killing. The means of removing agents are finite on the relevant timescales, so the


                                                205
   effective death rate of invading pathogen falls as the incident cohort is killed. This may
   happen inside the cell, where some host cells carry oxidative granules of finite number, or
   outside it.
Detection avoidance. Continual budding may be detected more readily, by immune cells that
  induce apoptosis, than release that is sporadic and clustered, as HIV’s is observed to be.

    All three amount to catching some immune mechanism off guard. This chapter pursues the
second, because it is the one that can be settled by calculation; the other two are named here and
left. Subductive proliferation would need no new machinery, its dynamics being the standard
time-inhomogeneous theory already available in section 2.2.3, but that theory does not by itself
distinguish bursting from budding. Detection avoidance would need a model of detection, which
this chapter does not have. Section 8.6.1 returns to both.
    Three results carry what follows. If a fixed count ϑ of released agents is removed on each
delivery, the expected surviving release of one cell is R(ϑ) = V∞ a−ϑ , where V∞ is the expected
lifetime yield of an infected cell and a is the larger root of the characteristic quadratic taken
from the chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe process. Every unit of removal costs the burst
one factor of a. In the replicator–suppressor model of section 8.3, survival is certain once the
incident cohort exceeds the granule store; on the matched diagonal the infection fails with a
probability that decays only polynomially in cohort size, at exponent λ/ς. And the flooding
criterion of the chapter on population dynamics reverses under a removal threshold: bursting
wins the matched-mean comparison exactly when L < 1, the complement of theorem 6.8.
    The derivation proceeds as follows.
 • A chained birth–death–catastrophe process with complete transfer cannot prefer one release
   schedule, so a nonlinearity is forced into the model.
 • The intracellular chain is solved by first-step analysis; the certainty threshold and the diagonal
   law are closed.
 • The extracellular toll is reduced to an integer budget, and memorylessness of the burst law
   gives R(ϑ).
 • The same functional is compared across bursting and budding, and the criterion reverses.
   The intracellular process, its roots a and b, its lifetime yield V∞ and its burst-size law are
taken from the chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe process (lemma 4.4 and (4.23) and
(5.46)); the single-cell comparison of the two release strategies is established in the chapter on
its distribution theory (section 5.11); and the population comparison, the flooding theorem
and the catalogue of intermediate release mechanisms are proved in the chapter on population
dynamics (theorem 6.8 and section 6.7). New here are the two depletion models, the closed
form R(ϑ) = V∞ a−ϑ , and the reversal. Section 8.2 forces the nonlinearity; sections 8.3 and 8.4
supply it, one site each; section 8.5 places the result against the flooding theorem and closes on
Y. pestis. Section 8.A records the numerical checks.




                                                206
               Symbol          Meaning
               Inherited, and used here without redefinition
               λ, µ, δ         birth, death and catastrophe rates of one replicator
               a, b            roots of the characteristic quadratic, b < 1 < a
               L = a(1 − b) the flooding parameter of theorem 6.8
               Wt              agents released by one infected cell up to time t
               V∞              expected lifetime release of one infected cell, limt E(Wt )
               r               burst size: the load released at rupture
               q               probability that a released agent infects a new cell
               Particular to this chapter
               ϑ               fixed removal budget, an integer count of killed agents
               ϑ∗              the budget at which R = 1, log V∞ / log a

               Xt , Qt         intracellular replicators, and remaining granules
               ς               suppression rate per replicator–granule pair

               N               granule store of a fresh host cell

Table 8.1: Notation. The upper block is taken from the chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe
process and the chapter on population dynamics; the lower block carries meanings specific to
this chapter, and those letters were chosen to avoid collisions with the ones those meanings
would otherwise have taken.


8.2     Why the release schedule alone cannot matter
In the pursuit of modelling multi-stage intracellular infection, an obvious first move is an
iterated birth–death–catastrophe process with the assumption of complete transfer — chained
ruptures. In trying to determine whether there is a fundamental reason for bursting dynamics in
pathogenesis, this model is of limited efficacy. With agents replicating identically whenever they
are within a host cell, it matters not how they are moved, just that they are replicating. With
chained ruptures, the burst event is merely a recorded instance without tangible implication
to agent spread. And if instead the bacteria modelled were to make their way one by one to a
new host, or even to multiple hosts, no change would be made to the expected growth rate of
the overall population. Even if some delay were introduced for unit transfer between hosts, this
would merely have the effect of adding intervals of inactivity to the time axis; broad propagation
dynamics would remain identical.
   It seems, then, that if we wish to understand why pathogens follow a bursting regime of
infection — or whether there is a reason at all — we must consider additional factors. Namely, we
must take into account that the death and replication rates of individual agents are not entirely
independent of the surrounding cohort. Various of the body’s immune factors are depletable.
Leucocytes including macrophages and neutrophils contain on the order of ten so-called granules,
small packets containing oxidising compounds with a toxic effect on invading pathogens. Given
that these can be used up, the effective within-host death rate of infectious agent will reduce as
more and more of the incident cohort are killed. There are likely extracellular immune factors
which exhibit a similar pattern of depletion.

8.2.1   Successive birth–death–catastrophe
The concept of chained ruptures is simple. A series of birth–death–catastrophe processes follow
on from one another, the result of the last going on to found an initial condition for the next
[99, 100, 58]. Starting with a single replicative unit, the first process undergoes the standard
dynamics of birth and death. Then, at the time of the catastrophe event, the quantity of units


                                                  207
present immediately transfers into another target cell, founding the second process with its initial
condition as given. This is the main assumption: that released replicative units somehow find
their way, all at once, to the same host cell. Of course this is quite reduced from the biological
context. The chapter on its distribution theory solves the chain in  closed form at µ = 0 for every
                                                             n+k−2 k n−1
chain position, giving the rupture-size law P(rk = n) = k−1 s ρ             of (5.79) together with
its moments and rupture times, and records the case µ > 0 as open (section 5.10.4).
   A less restricted view would be that following rupture, all of the r1 released bacteria undergo
Brownian motion and drift gradually from the burst location. The cohort, as it spreads out,
will encounter multiple new target cells and get taken up one by one. Albeit less so, this is also
greatly reduced from biological reality, which in its full complexity will be beyond the scope of
concise mathematical description. For example, the target cells we have focused on mostly are
macrophages. Macrophages do not merely float around in Brownian drift; they are motile, and
essentially walk around their environment seeking out debris and alien material to engulf and
make away with. The agents themselves are little more straightforward. Y. pestis can “decide”
whether it will be absorbed or not by such phagocytes [8, 6]. In addition to this, the bacteria can
inject immune cells with a host of special proteins with various effects including neutralisation
[5].
   Generally, the playing field of these actors is just that, an environment of play, in which the
responsive agents interact with one another; causal relationships ensue which go far beyond
mere drift and chance.
                                             time −→




            (a)                      (b)                     (c)                     (d)

Figure 8.1: Four frames of a particle simulation, showing what the chained model leaves out.
(a) Intracellular replication before bursting: the load accumulates out of reach. (b) The cell
bursts and the contents span the extracellular space before finding new hosts — the interval the
chained model collapses to a point. (c) Macrophages are motile; they wander the extracellular
matrix phagocytosing debris and extracellular pathogens alike, so which cell a released agent
reaches is not settled by diffusion alone. (d) A second round of bursting, here with a larger load.
Under identical intracellular rates the growth rate is the same however the transfers between (a)
and (d) are arranged, which is why the argument of this section forces a nonlinearity into the
model.

   Figure 8.1 shows the four stages of that picture as a simulation run. What matters for the
argument is the contrast between panels (a) and (d): both show a burst, and under identical
intracellular rates the two carry the same expected yield whatever route the released agents take
between them.
   The conclusion is negative, and it sets the chapter’s agenda. A release schedule can only
matter if something in the environment responds to how many agents arrive at once — if the
per-agent hazard depends on the size of the cohort it arrives in. Two such mechanisms are treated
in what follows, one inside the host cell and one outside it: section 8.3 makes the intracellular
killing capacity finite and depletable, and section 8.4 does the same for the extracellular one.
Both are nonlinear, and both are therefore outside the scope of the branching comparison in the

                                                208
chapter on population dynamics, which compares offspring distributions at fixed mean and says
so explicitly (??).


8.3     Intracellular depletion: replicator and suppressor
A number of replicative units found an infection within a host cell, and their dynamics accord
with a continuous-time birth process; what is the probability that at time t at least one unit is
still alive, so that the infection has not died out? For a linear process this depends only on the
criticality — whether a given unit is expected to leave behind more or less than a replacement of
itself — and sections 2.2.1.2 and 2.2.2.4 settle it. Section 2.A asks the question this section asks,
in its linear form: how much difference does the size of the founding group make? What is sought
here is that same non-extinction probability through time when the dynamics are nonlinear, and
with it the effect of the nonlinearity on survivability at varied initial counts X0 = n.

8.3.1    The granule store
Most of the handful of leucocyte types known to science carry packets of toxic material called
granules; granulocytes not least. Their contents are generally oxidative, and the immune cell
employs them to attack invading pathogens [101, 102]. The leucocytes of interest in this research,
macrophages and neutrophils, both carry granules, but they carry different numbers of them and
use them differently. Neutrophils are of the category granulocyte and, as the name suggests, hold
more granules than macrophages; they lyse themselves to release those inflammatory granules into
the extracellular medium. Macrophages instead employ their granules intracellularly, merging
them with the phagosomal membranes that harbour pathogen after engulfing has taken place —
a compartment some pathogens escape rather than survive [103].
   It is this dynamic which forms the basis of the model presented here. Picture a leucocyte
which has just engulfed some number of pathogen and which holds, besides, some number of
toxin-containing granules, assumed to be depleted in the process of being used to kill. Because
the granules are finite in number and depletable, a larger incident cohort of replicators may have
a greater chance of eventual survival. This is the intracellular form of the dose-dependence that
stochastic models of F. tularensis and of Salmonella have found at the level of the whole host
[16, 104, 105, 53].
   Figure 8.2 shows the two forces in a simulation. The point to take from it is that the granules
are consumed as they act: the red population in panel (b) is smaller than in panel (a), and it is
that reduction, not any change in the pathogen, which lets the green population run away.

8.3.2    The replicator–suppressor process
We explore a simple model representing the interplay between the two opposing forces: replicative
pathogenic particles fighting for survival against their host cell’s inbuilt defence mechanism. The
model constitutes two unit types, and from a defined initial condition the dynamic between
them results in either pathogen extinction or the overthrowing of immunity, which here appears
as unbounded growth in the replicator count.




                                                209
                                    (a)                                             (b)

Figure 8.2: The two opposing forces, simulated. Intracellular granules are in red, pathogenic
particles in green; when one of each makes contact, both are annihilated, and that mutual
annihilation is the depletion the model turns on. (a) Early, with the store still nearly full. (b)
Later in the same run, with most of the store spent: the pathogen count is little changed, but
the suppression rate ςij has fallen with j, so what remains of the defence can no longer keep
pace with the birth rate.


Definition 8.1 (Replicator–suppressor process). Let Xt count the infectious agents inside one
host cell and Qt the remaining number of within-cell granules. The process X = {(Xt , Qt ) : t ≥ 0}
is the continuous-time Markov chain on
                                              
                                       S = (i, j) : i ∈ N ∪ {0}, j ∈ {0, . . . , N }                      (8.1)

with, for (i, j) ∈ S,

                           P(∆Xt = 1, ∆Qt = 0 | Xt = i, Qt = j) = λi ∆t + o(∆t),
             P(∆Xt = −1, ∆Qt = −1 | Xt = i, Qt = j) = ςij ∆t + o(∆t),                                     (8.2)
                           P(∆Xt = 0, ∆Qt = 0 | Xt = i, Qt = j) = 1 − (λi + ςij) ∆t + o(∆t).

                           2

                               0   1      2        3     4        5         6   7

                                                  Xt replicators

Figure 8.3: The state space S of definition 8.1. Births move right and spend no granule;
suppressions move down and left, spending one of each. The column Xt = 0 is extinction; the
row Qt = 0 is a spent store, from which only births remain, so survival is certain. That is why
the diagonal i = j matters.


                                                                      210
    Units of Xt divide and increase according to the standard mechanism of a birth process.
Suppressive interactions occur at a rate proportional to the product of the two counts and result
in a single elimination of each type; it is that product which makes the process nonlinear, and it
is the only nonlinearity in the model. Because each suppression costs one of each, a cohort that
outnumbers the store cannot be cleared.
    In reality growth will not be truly without limit — intracellular capacity will eventually
impose a threshold on further expansion without cell bursting — but the model is not run
beyond the point at which the granules are exhausted, so the idealisation costs nothing that is
used below. Figure 8.3 is the chain.
    Our interest is in the relationship between incident pathogen count and infection survivability,
that is in
                             P(infection survives) = 1 − lim P(Xt = 0) ,                      (8.3)
                                                                         t→∞

which is the probability of late-time non-extinction. Survival is assured on any path that exhausts
the antagonistic granules: once Qt = 0 the process is a pure birth process, and extinction is
impossible thereafter.
Remark 8.2 (No replenishment). The granule store is fixed at Q0 and never supplemented. One
may devise a model in which granules are produced at some rate by the cell, and for a long
infection that would be the right model; the version analysed here takes the initial immune
capacity as given, which is the regime in which a single engulfment episode is decided. Every
result below is a statement about that regime, and the certainty threshold of proposition 8.3 in
particular depends on it.
   The asymmetry of the two arrow families is the whole model: a birth spends nothing, whereas
a suppression spends a granule, so the store can only fall and each unit of it can be used once.

                                6                                        ···
                                5                                        ···   certain, i > j



                  Qt granules
                                4                                        ···   contested, 1 ≤ i ≤ j
                                                                               extinct, i = 0
                                3                                        ···
                                2                                        ···
                                1                                        ···


                                    0   1   2   3 4 5 6           7    8 ···
                                                Xt replicators

Figure 8.4: The three fates, read off figure 8.3. In i > j survival is certain: extinction would
need i suppressions and only j are available (proposition 8.3). In 1 ≤ i ≤ j both outcomes have
positive probability, and πi,j is what the rest of this section computes. The boundary is the
diagonal, and it is sharp.

  Figure 8.4 is the section’s headline. A cohort that outnumbers the store cannot be cleared,
however unfavourable the rate constants; a cohort that does not outnumber it may still win,
and with what probability is the question.

8.3.3    First-step analysis
The process is defined by its one-step transition probabilities,

                 p(i,j)→(k,l) (∆t) = P((Xt+∆t , Qt+∆t ) = (k, l) | (Xt , Qt ) = (i, j)) ,



                                                                 211
and enumerating the steps available from an arbitrary state (i, j) gives
                                              
                                              λi ∆t + o(∆t),             (k, l) = (i + 1, j),
                                              
                                              
             p(i,j)→(k,l) (∆t) =                ςij ∆t + o(∆t),           (k, l) = (i − 1, j − 1),               (8.4)
                                              
                                              
                                              1 − (λi + ςij) ∆t + o(∆t), (k, l) = (i, j),

as ∆t → 0. Write πi,j for the probability of fixation in the set {Xt = 0} from the state (i, j).
Conditioning on the first jump, and using the first-step principle of proposition 2.6 in the
continuous-time form (2.52),
                                                                              λ                         ςj
                πi,j = α̂j πi+1,j + β̂j πi−1,j−1 ,                  α̂j =          ,       β̂j =             ,   (8.5)
                                                                            λ + ςj                    λ + ςj
with π0,j = 1 for every j and πi,0 = 0 for every i ≥ 1. The jump probabilities depend on j alone:
the competing rates are λi and ςij, and the common factor i cancels. The replicator count
therefore affects the outcome only through how many suppressions are needed, never through
how likely the next event is to be one.
   Collecting (8.5) over all transient states gives the linear system

                                                       p(0) = A p(0) + c,                                        (8.6)

in which p(0) collects the unknowns πi,j for 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ N , the matrix A carries the coefficients
α̂j and β̂j , and the vector c carries the single inhomogeneous term β̂j contributed by π0,j−1 = 1
at i = 1. The system is block lower-triangular when the states are ordered by the level j − i,
because the two states appearing on the right of (8.5) sit one at level j − i − 1 and one at level
j − i; those levels are drawn in figure 8.5. Nothing beyond that structure is needed: it makes
the system solvable level by level, and the solver of section 8.B exploits nothing else.

                                                                                        level j − i

                                          0    1   2      3   4     5   6     7
                                                       Xt replicators

Figure 8.5: The level structure of the first-step system (8.5). States are grouped by the level
j − i. A suppression stays on its diagonal; a birth moves one level down. No transition raises
the level, so A in (8.6) is block lower-triangular in this ordering and the levels may be solved in
turn, starting from level 0.

  Level 0 is the diagonal i = j, and it closes on itself. That is enough for two results.

Proposition 8.3 (Certainty threshold). πi,j = 0 whenever i > j: the infection survives with
probability one as soon as the incident cohort exceeds the granule store. For 1 ≤ i ≤ j we have
πi,j ∈ (0, 1).

Proof. Each suppression removes one pathogen and one granule; a birth removes no granule. Reaching
X = 0 from (i, j) therefore requires at least i suppressions, so j ≥ i as claimed.

Table 8.2: The diagonal law (8.7) at λ = 1. Reading across a row shows the decay in cohort size
at fixed rates; reading down a column shows how much slower that decay becomes as suppression
strengthens relative to birth. The bottom row is the borderline case λ = ς, where the failure
probability is exactly 1/(j + 1) — a cohort of twenty-five matched to twenty-five granules still
fails one time in twenty-six.


   Now take 1 ≤ i ≤ j. A run of i suppressions reaches (0, j − i) and has positive probability, since β̂j > 0
at every j ≥ 1; thus πi,j > 0. A run of j − i + 1 births reaches (j + 1, j), where π = 0 by the first claim,
and has positive probability since α̂j > 0; thus πi,j < 1.
    The boundary is the diagonal drawn in figure 8.4, and nothing about the rate constants moves
it. Consequently the only remaining question on the contested side is how πi,j varies with dose.
Monotonicity in i and in j was checked numerically over ς ∈ {0.1, 0.2, 0.3} at N = 20, and both
hold everywhere (section 8.A).

Proposition 8.4 (The diagonal law). Write ν = λ/ς. On the matched diagonal,
                                                          !−1
                             j! Γ(ν + 1)            j+ν
              j
              Y      ςm
        πj,j =            =              =                      ∼ Γ(ν + 1) j −ν         (j → ∞),       (8.7)
               m=1
                   λ + ςm   Γ(j + ν + 1)             j

so a cohort matched to its own granule store fails with a probability falling only polynomially in
cohort size, at exponent λ/ς. When λ = ς this is exactly 1/(j + 1).

Proof. From (j, j) a birth reaches (j + 1, j), where π = 0 by proposition 8.3. Hence πj,j = β̂j πj−1,j−1
with β̂j = ςj/(λ + ςj), and π0,0 = 1. Iterating gives the product, and the ratio of Gamma functions
             Qj                     Qj
follows from m=1 ςm = ς j j! and m=1 (λ + ςm) = ς j Γ(j + ν + 1)/Γ(ν + 1). The asymptotic is the
standard ratio limit Γ(j + 1)/Γ(j + ν + 1) ∼ j −ν .
   Table 8.2 shows why (8.7) is worth stating separately. Doubling the cohort on the diagonal
does not halve the failure probability: it divides it by 2ν , and ν = λ/ς is a property of the cell
rather than of the dose. Safety in numbers is therefore real but slow, and qualitatively weaker
than the certainty that arrives the moment the cohort crosses the store.

8.3.4     The survival maps
Solving (8.6) over the whole grid gives the survival probability 1 − πi,j for every initial condition.
   Figures 8.6 and 8.7 show the maps at N = 20 and N = 10. Two features carry the section.
The first is the flat region of certain survival below the diagonal, which is proposition 8.3 drawn:
it is not that survival becomes likely there, but that it becomes certain. The second is the
marginal bar chart above each map, the case j = N of a full store, in which the rise from
near-zero to near-one is spread over several units of dose rather than concentrated at one — the
polynomial decay of (8.7), seen from the side.
   For a pathogen, the reading is direct. If a host cell can be entered by several agents at once
rather than one at a time, and the store is finite, then arriving together is worth more than
arriving in succession by exactly the margin these maps display. That is the intracellular version
of the flooding advantage, and section 8.4 builds its extracellular counterpart.

8.4                                              Extracellular depletion: fixed removal
The second nonlinearity is extracellular, and it enters as an extension to the chained-rupture
model of section 8.2.1. The extension is one assumption, arising from the same argument of
immune depletion: that following a burst release event, some defined fixed count of the agents is
removed. From that assumption alone, the question of whether bursting strategies might be of
fundamental advantage can be put to a calculation.




                                                                                                                    214
8.4.1    The playing field
Consider again the concept of spatial drift, which for all its imperfection improves on the
simpler dynamic of chained immediate transfer. Picture the playing field: a three-dimensional
extracellular matrix populated by three types of agent — replicative units, that is pathogen;
host cells, here macrophages; and another type of immune-active cell, which we shall call simply
killer cells. This third type is able to eliminate units of pathogen on contact, being used up in
the process: one-to-one encounters in which a killer cell removes a replicative agent and itself
from play.
    To begin with, the extracellular matrix is populated evenly with target cells and killer cells,
each group occupying the space at a uniform spatial density. Killer cells are smaller and more
numerous than target cells, a greater number per unit volume. Dynamics within target cells are
as understood in the birth–death–catastrophe process: the units of pathogen replicate and die
within the cell until eventually rupture is caused and the load is released.
    In this model, movement is taken to be Brownian. An extracellular colony will drift from
its burst location, spreading out and coming into contact with surrounding cells. Some will
encounter target cells, others killer cells. The intention here is not to construct a full mathematical
description of this game, which would be cumbersome and intractable; it can be simulated
computationally, and that simulation is what supports the assumption the model is used to
justify. Suppose that no new killer cells are introduced into the environment, so that pathogen
movement and absorption occur on a shorter timescale than killer generation. Then, with greater
burst sizes, each individual bacterium will have a smaller probability of being removed. Those
at the leading edge, as it were, clearing the way for their comrades in the rear. Figure 8.8 puts a
large and a small release of that simulation side by side.
    If free agents do tend to find a new host before long — an assumption taken to its limit in the
case of chained immediate transfer — then this observation offers the basis of an approximate
mathematical rule. Burst size and the number of pathogen killed in the process of transfer stand
in some relation to one another, and across larger and larger bursts the casualty count should
rise ever more slowly. Qualitatively this might be a logarithmic progression,
                                          ϑ(r) ∼ log(r + 1),                                     (8.8)
where r is a given burst size and ϑ the number of replicative agents removed by killer cells; the
+1 simply makes the curve pass through the origin.
   Figure 8.9a draws that curve, and its flatness is what the rest of the section idealises. It is
reasonable to impose a fixed removal following release, in which the number removed by killer
cells is a specified constant regardless of burst size, except that if the burst size is smaller than
that constant, all of the units are killed. Taking ϑ now to be that constant,
                                      (
                                          r, r ≤ ϑ,
                          removal =                     that is, min(r, ϑ).                      (8.9)
                                          ϑ, r > ϑ,
    Figure 8.9b shows the idealisation against which the rest of the section works. Its efficacy
rests on the expectation that the same qualitative dynamics are shared with logarithmic removal,
or with whatever the actual relation turns out to be, provided it is of this sort. Two further
assumptions are needed before the calculation can proceed, and both are restrictions rather than
conveniences.
Remark 8.5 (Killer prevalence resets). At the time of each rupture event the killer cell prevalence
is assumed to have returned to its steady state and to be once again of uniform spatial distribution.
This is what makes ϑ the same at every delivery. It requires the interval between ruptures to be
long relative to the recovery of the effector population, whose turnover in an established infection
is itself a modelled quantity [106]; where it is not, the budget paid at successive deliveries would
be correlated, and the independence used below would fail.

                                                 215
                                                time −→




   large load

                         (a)                       (b)                            (c)




    small load

                         (d)                       (e)                            (f)

Figure 8.8: Two runs of the same simulation differing in released number alone; every rate is the
same. Depletable extracellular immune factors are consumed by the agents they remove, so a
large load creates a safety in numbers — a “flooding effect” — and the killers that act early are
not available later. The comparison to draw is between (c) and (f). In (c) the large load has
cleared a patch of the killer field and survivors remain inside it, poised on the surrounding host
cells; in (f) the small load is exhausted before the field is, and nothing persists to the next round
of absorption and replication.


Remark 8.6 (One target cell, or many). The fixed-removal dynamic may be framed in the context
of chained transfers, where all agents released have the same target cell to go to; or in a case
where each successful pathogenic unit goes on to found an infection in a new target cell. Under
the first framing the replication ratio would be the expected number of surviving released units
per incident agent. The second framing is taken here, so that the ratio counts newly infected
target cells, which is what makes it comparable with the reproduction numbers of the chapter
on population dynamics.

8.4.2            The replication ratio
Take, then, the second option: each released agent goes on to found a new infection in a new
target cell. The quantity of interest is the effective replication ratio between target cell infections
— the expected number of newly infected target cells created by a host founded from one initial
bacterium — and because each surviving released agent infects a new host, that ratio is simply
the expected number of survivors.
   Define r as the outcome of a birth–death–catastrophe process with a single incident unit. We
must choose whether to include outcomes that are zero through internal death, and it makes

intuition in one line. In (b) that flatness is pushed to its limit: below the budget every agent
dies, and above it the plateau at ϑ means a burst pays the same toll however large it is, so all the
marginal yield survives. The reason for adopting the caricature is that it makes the replication
ratio computable in closed form.

8.4.3       Analysis of proliferability
The question is whether R(ϑ) is greater or less than one, and if greater, by how much; and how
that changes with δ. Do larger burst sizes make an infection more viable? The geometric tail of
(8.17) already answers: a budget spent against a geometric law removes a fixed proportion of it
rather than a fixed amount.

Proposition 8.7 (Fixed-removal replication ratio). For an integer removal budget ϑ ≥ 0,

                                     R(ϑ) = E(max(0, r − ϑ)) = V∞ a−ϑ .                               (8.18)

Each unit of removal costs the burst a factor 1/a, independently of ϑ, of µ, and of everything
about the burst-size law except a.

Proof. The burst law (8.13) is geometric on {1, 2, . . . } with ratio 1/a, hence memoryless above any integer
threshold:
                        X          δ        δ      X
                            (i − ϑ) a−i = a−ϑ             k a−k = a−ϑ E(r) = V∞ a−ϑ .
                                   λ       λ
                            i>ϑ                        k≥1

   Two consequences follow in one line each, and one restriction.


                                                         218
Corollary 8.8 (Threshold). R(ϑ) > 1 if and only if ϑ < ϑ∗ := log V∞ / log a. At (λ, µ, δ) =
(1, 0.2, 0.05) this gives ϑ∗ = 44.13; at (1, 0.5, 1/3) it gives ϑ∗ = 1.71.

Corollary 8.9 (Why removal creates a threshold at all). V∞ > 1 for every admissible (λ, µ, δ),
so R(0) > 1 always. The subcritical regime exists only because of the removal.

Remark 8.11 (Scope: ϑ is an integer). The derivation sums to ϑ − 1 and so requires ϑ ∈ Z≥0 .
That is the honest reading in any case, since ϑ counts killed particles, and it is adopted as part
of the model definition.
                          For a real budget the corresponding quantity is E(max(0, r − ϑ)) =
a−⌈ϑ⌉ V∞ + (⌈ϑ⌉ − ϑ)L with L = a(1 − b), which reduces to (8.18) exactly when ϑ is an integer;
section 8.A records its accuracy.
   The behaviour of (8.18) in δ is drawn in figure 8.10. The reading that matters for what follows
is the geometry of the crossing: because R(ϑ) = V∞ a−ϑ is a product of a term that grows as
δ falls and a penalty that is exponential in the budget, an increase in the toll can always be
answered by a large enough burst, and the exchange rate between the two is exactly one factor
of a per unit of ϑ.

Figure 8.10: The replication ratio (8.18) against δ at λ = 0.8, µ = 0, for budgets ϑ = 0, 1
and 2, with V∞ dashed for reference throughout. In panel (a) the two curves coincide, since

is corollary 8.9 drawn. Elsewhere the marked point is the crossing R = 1 of corollary 8.8, at
δ = 0.800 and δ = 0.494: to the left of it the infection proliferates, to the right the removal
extinguishes it. Each further unit of budget moves the crossing left, so a pathogen facing a
heavier toll needs a lower catastrophe rate — a larger burst — to stay supercritical.




                                                                          219
8.5      Bursting and budding: where the advantage lives
8.5.1     What is settled, and what was left open
The chapter on population dynamics settles the comparison under linear clearance, and it does so
twice. First, at fixed total per-cell output V∞ , replacing constant budding by bursting leaves the
basic reproduction number exactly unchanged (theorem 6.6): under linear clearance the release
schedule is invisible to R0 . Second, the schedule is nevertheless not invisible to establishment.
Theorem 6.8 shows that bursting strictly lowers the extinction probability of a small inoculum,
relative to matched-mean budding, if and only if the flooding parameter
                                                                      µ
                                             L := a(1 − b) = a −                                               (8.20)
                                                                      λ
exceeds one, equivalently 1/δ < 1/λ + 1/µ ((6.29)); at L = 1 the two offspring laws coincide
exactly and every functional of them ties (proposition 6.9).
   The letter R0 in the chapter on population dynamics denotes the expected number of secondary
infections produced by one infected cell in a wholly susceptible population under linear clearance,
in the sense of [76]; a theorem there proves that quantity invariant. The quantity of (8.18)
is not that: it is the expected number of surviving released agents per infected cell when the
clearance is a threshold rather than a rate. Under linear clearance at infection probability q
the two agree up to the thinning factor, since R(0) = V∞ and the mean offspring number in
the chapter on population dynamics is qV∞ . Under threshold removal they part company, and
hence the different letter. Reading R(ϑ) as R0 would put this chapter in contradiction with
theorem 6.6, and there is none.
   ?? records that both results in the chapter on population dynamics compare branching
structures at fixed offspring mean, and therefore say nothing about nonlinear effects. Fixed
removal is exactly such an effect — a threshold is not a rate — so the comparison can be run
again through it. It does not come out the same way.

8.5.2     The reversal
Two arguments will appear, and they are not the same argument. The distributional one holds
the number of deliveries fixed at one and compares two laws for that delivery, matched at mean
V∞ ; its verdict depends on L. The batch-count one holds the law fixed and asks how the same
total fares when split across more deliveries, each paying a toll ϑ; its verdict is unconditional
concentration. The reversal below is the first. The spectrum figure is the second.

Proposition 8.12 (The flooding criterion reverses under threshold removal). Let the budding
comparator of section 6.6 release a total that is geometric on {0, 1, 2, . . . } with the same mean
V∞ , and write σ = V∞ /(1 + V∞ ). Then under an integer removal budget ϑ,

                                Rburst (ϑ) = V∞ a−ϑ ,          Rbud (ϑ) = V∞ σ ϑ ,                             (8.21)

so bursting exceeds matched-mean budding if and only if 1/a > σ, that is if and only if V∞ (a−1) <
1, that is if and only if L < 1 — the exact complement of theorem 6.8. At L = 1 the two laws
coincide by proposition 6.9 and every functional ties.

Proof. Both expressions in (8.21) follow from the memorylessness argument of proposition 8.7, applied to
a geometric law on {1, 2, . . . } with ratio 1/a in the first case and to one on {0, 1, 2, . . . } with ratio σ in the
second. For the criterion, take proposition 8.7 at ϑ = 1 against the tail (8.17): V∞ /a = E(r) − P(r ≥ 1) =
V∞ − (1 − b), whence V∞ = a(1 − b)/(a − 1) = L/(a − 1). Therefore

                    1     V∞
                      >        ⇐⇒ 1 + V∞ > aV∞ ⇐⇒ V∞ (a − 1) < 1 ⇐⇒ L < 1.
                    a   1 + V∞

                                                                                                     winner under
           (λ, µ, δ)                     L       ϑ                  Rburst     Rbud      linear clearance    threshold removal
           (1, 0, 0.1)                  1.100     5                 6.830      7.120     bursting            budding
           (1, 0.5, 1/3)                1.000     5                 0.263      0.263     tie                 tie
           (1, 0.9, 0.1)                0.416     5                 0.333      0.078     budding             bursting
           (1, 0.2, 0.05)               0.862    10                 7.692      7.011     budding             bursting
           (1, 0.9, 0.02)               0.207    5                  1.164      0.241     budding             bursting
           (1, 0.95, 0.5)               1.017    3                  0.138      0.142     bursting            budding

Table 8.3: The reversal in six regimes. The last two columns name which strategy wins under
linear clearance and which under threshold removal, and they disagree in every row except
the tie. The middle row is the boundary L = 1, where the bursting offspring law is exactly
geometric (proposition 6.9) and the two ratios agree to the digits shown. The fourth row is
strongly supercritical on both sides, so the reversal is not an artefact of one of them being near
extinction.


    The result is a reversal rather than a contradiction, and the reason is a single fact about
variance. At matched mean, extinction probability rewards low offspring variance, whereas
E(max(0, r − ϑ)) is a convex functional of the offspring count and so rewards high variance.
(6.31) gives the variance ordering in terms of the same parameter, Var( · )bud − Var( · )burst =
2q 2 V∞ (L − 1)/(a − 1). The sign of L − 1 therefore drives both criteria, and drives them opposite
ways: where bursting is the safer bet for establishment it is the worse bet against a threshold,
and conversely. Both criteria turn on the offspring count being integer-valued. Only an integer
count lets a threshold bite, and the same feature governs establishment [55].

Figure 8.11: The sign change, drawn. The gap Rburst − Rbud against δ at λ = 1, µ = 0.5, for
three removal budgets. All three curves cross zero at the same place, the flooding boundary
δ∗ = λµ/(λ + µ) = 1/3 of (6.29), and they cross it in the direction opposite to theorem 6.8:
bursting wins to the left, where L < 1. Raising the budget deepens the advantage on the left
without moving the boundary, because the boundary is a property of the two laws and not of
the toll.

   Table 8.3 and figure 8.11 exhibit the reversal. The boundary is unmoved by ϑ. Carry that
forward: the removal budget sets how much the winner wins by, and the rate constants alone set
who wins.
   If the toll is paid per delivery, a cell that delivers a total yield V∞ in n instalments retains
max(0, V∞ − nϑ), against a bursting cell’s max(0, V∞ − ϑ). Since n ≥ 1, concentration wins for

                                                                                   221
every n, every ϑ and every L: there is no threshold and no reversal in this version. That is the
batch-count argument of section 8.4.1, not proposition 8.12.
Remark 8.13 (Scope of the comparison). The two arguments answer different questions and
neither subsumes the other. The distributional one holds the number of deliveries fixed at one
and asks which law for that delivery does better, and its answer depends on L. The batch-count
one holds the law fixed and asks how the same total fares when split across more deliveries, and
its answer does not depend on L at all. Conflating them produces the impression that flooding is
unconditionally advantageous under depletion — true of the second argument, false of the first.

8.5.3    Placement on the spectrum
Section 6.7 lays the release mechanisms out as a spectrum (??). The catalogue is built there
and is not rebuilt here. What a per-delivery toll does at each station follows from the batch
count without a new derivation: the surviving output is set by how many times ϑ is paid.

                constant p      stock-and-export   reset / partial φ        full clear      absorbing BDC


         budding                                                                                     bursting
             pays nϑ, n large   pays per export      pays ∼ ϑ/φ        pays once per dump     pays once


Figure 8.12: Per-delivery toll along the budding–bursting spectrum of ??. Moving right, fewer
deliveries are made and a smaller total is paid. The ordering is the batch count, not the
distributional reversal.

   Under a per-delivery toll, selection on release phenotypes is therefore monotone toward fewer,
larger deliveries (figure 8.12 and ??). That is a different statement from proposition 8.12, which
compares two laws at one delivery each and can go either way. It is also what makes depletion
a candidate selective pressure at all, in the sense in which nested within-host models pose the
question [62]: it supplies a gradient rather than merely a difference.
   That gradient does not run unopposed. It runs against the one the chapter on population
dynamics identifies, since concentrating release lengthens the generation time and so lowers the
deterministic growth rate [77], and the phage lysis-timing literature poses the same trade-off
in its own terms [63]; how a lytic strategy can be evolutionarily competitive at all under that
tension is asked directly [15]. The contribution here is to name the third quantity in the trade-off
and to give it a closed form.
   Self-excitation (??) is the one station where the toll itself moves. Remark 8.5 assumes the
killer field has recovered between deliveries; a self-exciting mechanism makes deliveries cluster in
time, so successive dumps meet a field that has not recovered and pay less than ϑ each. Whether
that is enough to overcome the batch-count penalty of making several deliveries instead of one
is not settled here — it depends on the recovery timescale, which the model does not carry —
and it is recorded as an open problem in section 8.6.2. It is worth naming because it is the
only mechanism in the catalogue for which the two arguments of remark 8.13 pull in opposite
directions.
   This is a statement about which release phenotypes a depletable defence favours, not about
which pathogens possess which phenotypes, and it holds at the level of one cell’s output. It
inherits the restriction that theorem 6.8 and [27] carry: what is being described is the early
phase [14].

8.5.4    Y. pestis
One reading takes the model of section 8.4 in the context of Y. pestis and neutrophils: the
target cells are macrophages, the killer cells are neutrophils. Y. pestis does replicate within

                                                        222
macrophages [89, 5], subverting the phagosome rather than being destroyed by it [7, 6], and it
encounters those cells early in the pulmonary route [107]. The injected effectors of section 8.2.1
act on the same cells, driving or withholding apoptosis [8, 10, 9]. Both actors of the model are
present. The formulae themselves do not depend on that reading.

                     Model            Reading
                     replicators Xt   intracellular pathogen in one macrophage
                     granules Qt      remaining oxidative store
                     ς                suppression per replicator–granule pair
                     burst size r     load at rupture
                     budget ϑ         killer cells removed per delivery
                     R(ϑ)             expected surviving release

Table 8.4: A reading of already-named objects under the Y. pestis mapping. The formulae
themselves do not depend on it.

    Read that way, the argument is two-phase. While killer-cell density is roughly constant,
the bursting advantage of section 8.5.3 applies: a macrophage releasing its whole load at once
pays one toll where a budding cell would pay many. If a later rise in killer cells pushes the toll
past ϑ∗ = log V∞ / log a, no intracellular strategy remains supercritical (corollary 8.8), and the
advantage lies with becoming extracellular. Why not replicate extracellularly from the beginning
is then a separate question: being within macrophages may buy transport to lymph nodes, and
perhaps food itself.
    What the mathematics supplies is a single number. At (λ, µ, δ) = (1, 0.5, 1/3) one has
ϑ∗ = 1.71, so a killer field able to remove two agents per delivery already forces the switch;
at (1, 0.2, 0.05) it is 44.13. If effector density at the site crosses ϑ∗ during the infection, the
intracellular phase should end at a level set by the intracellular rate constants and by nothing
else. An analogous reading for F. tularensis [52, 16] would use the same threshold with different
constants.


8.6     Discussion
8.6.1    Limitations
Of the three candidate advantages set out in section 8.1.1, this chapter settles one. Limited
killing is what sections 8.3 and 8.4 model, and the results are the certainty threshold, the
diagonal law and R(ϑ) = V∞ a−ϑ . The other two were named there and left, and this is where
the reasons are given.
   Subductive proliferation — a replication rate that declines during residence in a host cell —
has its machinery already: it is a time-inhomogeneous birth process, a special case of (2.29)
with λ(t) = βe−αt + γ, and the first-jump probability, the mean and the deterministic trajectory
are all elementary. That material is not carried in this chapter, because it does not by itself
distinguish the two release strategies. Indeed it is not obviously an argument for bursting at
all: a rate that decays with residence time penalises precisely the strategy that waits before
releasing, so the sign of the effect is unclear before the calculation is done, and the calculation
is not done here. The apparatus is retained as a candidate worked example for section 2.2.3,
where the general result it specialises already lives.
   Detection avoidance is named and not pursued because this chapter has no model of detection.
It would need a hazard that responds to the rate of release rather than to its size, which is the
opposite dependence from the one modelled here, and it is the mechanism most often invoked for
HIV, where release is observed to be clustered and where the responding immune compartment


                                                223
has models of its own [108].
   Four further restrictions are worth stating.
   The replicator–suppressor process of definition 8.1 lets the pathogen grow without bound
once the store is spent. Real intracellular capacity is finite, and a full model would terminate
the run at rupture rather than let Xt diverge. Nothing in proposition 8.3 or proposition 8.4
depends on the late-time behaviour — both are statements about which absorbing set is reached
— but the survival maps of section 8.3.4 should be read as probabilities of avoiding clearance, not
as predictions of intracellular load.
   The granule store is never replenished (remark 8.2). This is the assumption that makes
depletion permanent, and it is what turns a larger cohort into a lasting advantage rather than a
temporary one. With replenishment at any positive rate the certainty threshold would soften
into a race between refill and consumption.
   The removal budget is fixed, and figures 8.9a and 8.9b show what is being idealised: a slowly
saturating casualty count is replaced by a step. The replacement is what makes (8.18) available
in closed form, and the honest claim is the one made in section 8.4.1, that the qualitative
dynamics are expected to be shared with the logarithmic case rather than that the two agree;
which of a model’s predictions survive such a substitution is not a question that answers itself
[109]. It also fixes ϑ as an integer (remark 8.11), which is right for a count of killed particles
and wrong for an average over deliveries.
   Finally, proposition 8.12 compares single batches. It matches two laws at one delivery each,
exactly as theorem 6.8 does, and remark 8.13 sets out what that does and does not license. In
particular it is not a statement about a whole infection, and the population-level consequences
of a threshold clearance are not derived anywhere in this thesis.

8.6.2    Open problems
 1. The chained process at µ > 0. The joint laws of the rupture times and sizes (tk , rk ) are
    known in closed form only for µ = 0. Section 5.10.4 states why the decomposition fails once
    a lineage can vanish internally, and states the problem first; this chapter inherits it, because
    section 8.2.1 is the model it concerns.
 2. Off the diagonal. Proposition 8.4 gives πj,j in closed form because level 0 of the first-step
    system closes on itself. No such product form is available for πi,j with i < j: the generating
    function in i couples level j to level j − 1 with π1,j entering as an unknown fixed by a degree
    condition. Off-diagonal values are computed and tabulated (figures 8.6 and 8.7) and left at
    that.
 3. The reversal at intermediate φ. Section 6.8.3 asks where on the partial-release continuum
    of section 6.7.2 the flooding advantage of theorem 6.8 survives. Proposition 8.12 answers the
    same question from the other side, at the two endpoints, and finds the criterion reversed.
    What happens at intermediate φ under a threshold is not known, and the two sub-questions
    are plainly the same problem viewed from opposite ends.
 4. Clustered deliveries and a recovering field. If the killer field recovers on a timescale
    comparable with the interval between deliveries, then remark 8.5 fails and the toll paid
    at successive deliveries is correlated. Section 8.5.3 notes that self-excitation is the one
    mechanism in section 6.7 for which this matters, and the question of whether reduced tolls
    can compensate for extra deliveries needs a model that carries the recovery timescale, which
    this one does not.




                                                224
8.6.3    Connections forward
The selection gradient identified in section 8.5.3 is a statement about variation between release
phenotypes, and the chapter on variability in evolution is where variability itself becomes the
object of study rather than a property of a fixed mechanism. The Y. pestis account of section 8.5.4
is the piece this chapter contributes to the concluding chapter, whose subject is the evolved
strategy of that organism: what is offered here is one testable number, the threshold budget ϑ∗ ,
and the claim that the switch between the intracellular and extracellular phases should occur
where the effector density crosses it.


8.A      Numerical verification
Every quantitative claim in this chapter was checked against independent numerics before being
stated. The checks were run in numpy with no other numerical library; the method in each case
was to compute the quantity twice, once from the closed form and once from a construction that
does not use it, and to compare.
    Three of the rows in table 8.5 deserve a word, because what they establish is used rather than
merely tallied.
    The reversal row is the strongest evidence in the chapter. The sign of Rburst − Rbud was
predicted from L alone, by proposition 8.12, and compared against brute-force summation of
both laws over ten rate regimes and five removal budgets. There were no exceptions among
the fifty rows. Swept in δ across the boundary at λ = 1, µ = 0.5, the sign changes exactly at
δ∗ = 1/3 to within 10−16 , which is the crossing drawn in figure 8.11.
    The first-step row is the only stochastic check. The linear solve of (8.6) was compared against
direct simulation of the embedded chain rather than against another algebraic route, because
there is no second algebraic route off the diagonal. Monotonicity of πi,j in i and in j is a
numerical check, not a claim of proposition 8.3: it was verified over the same grids at N = 20
and holds everywhere tested.
    The intermediate-form row records a scope limit rather than an error. The expression (8.19)
is exact at integer ϑ and is not the quantity E(max(0, r − ϑ)) at non-integer ϑ, where its relative
error reaches −14% at (λ, δ, ϑ) = (0.8, 2, 0.8). That is the reason remark 8.11 declares ϑ integral
and the reason the figures of section 8.4.3 are drawn at integer budgets.
    The scripts that regenerate the figures of this chapter sit with the chapter source, one directory
per figure, each self-contained and runnable on its own. The value µ = 0 in the lifetime-yield
and replication-ratio figures was recovered by fitting the original published curve; the verification
computations above were run separately and are not reproduced here.

limited by anything other than floating-point arithmetic: its error is set by the truncation tail,
not by the formula. Every other row agrees to within a few units in the last place, which is what
one expects of two algebraically equivalent evaluations rather than of a formula that happens to
fit.


8.B       Computational notes
8.B.1     The first-step solver
The system (8.6) is assembled directly from the jump probabilities of (8.5) and solved once. The
unknowns are πi,j for 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ N ; the two boundary conditions π0,j = 1 and πi,j = 0 for
i > j enter as the inhomogeneous term and as an omission from the index set respectively. The
assembly is direct enough to describe in words, and the working implementation is a file rather
than a printed page.
   Index the transient states (i, j) with 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ N in any fixed order. For each such state,
place α̂j in the column of πi+1,j whenever i + 1 ≤ j, and β̂j in the column of πi−1,j−1 whenever
i − 1 ≥ 1; when i = 1 the second coefficient has no column to go in, because π0,j−1 = 1 is known,
and it becomes that row’s entry of c. Nothing else is non-zero. One call to a dense linear solver
on (I − A)p(0) = c then returns every πi,j at once. At N = 20 the system has 210 unknowns and
solves in milliseconds, so the block-triangular structure of figure 8.5 is a fact about the matrix
rather than a computational necessity; it becomes one only for much larger stores.
   Two features of that recipe are worth noting, and both come from section 8.3.3. The coefficients
depend on j alone, so each takes one argument where the state has two; and the omission of the
states i > j is not an approximation but proposition 8.3, which is what keeps the system finite
despite Xt being unbounded. Get either wrong and the solve still returns numbers, which is
why the implementation checks its diagonal against the closed form of proposition 8.4 on every
run, and agrees to 1.9 × 10−16 .
   The working implementation sits with the chapter’s figure-generation scripts. It is self-
contained, depends only on numpy and matplotlib, carries the assembly above, the solve, that
check, and the plotting that produces figures 8.6 and 8.7.

                                                  226
8.B.2    Quadtree sampling
Collision tests for the particle runs of figures 8.1, 8.2 and 8.8 were quadtree-indexed, so that
only agents local to a macrophage are tested. The index is rebuilt each frame; the saving is that
collision testing stops being quadratic in the particle count, which is what the large-load runs of
figure 8.8 require.




                          (a)                                           (b)

Figure 8.13: The quadtree index during a simulation run, with the cells local to the macrophage
shaded pink; only the particles inside those cells are tested for collision. (a) A sparse field, where
the index buys little. (b) A dense one, where it buys a great deal: the shaded region is a small
fraction of the frame, so the number of collision tests grows far more slowly than the number of
particles. This is why the large-load runs of figure 8.8 are feasible at all.

  Figure 8.13 shows the index in a sparse field and a dense one.




                                                 227
Chapter 9

Models with non-constant rates

9.1     Introduction
Every model carried through to a result in the preceding chapters holds its rates fixed. A
replicative unit is born at rate λ, dies at rate µ and triggers catastrophe at rate δ, and those
three numbers are the same at the end of the process as at the beginning. That assumption
is what makes the generating functions close, and it buys a great deal: the roots a and b, the
burst-size law, the flooding criterion and the removal budget. It is also the first thing an ecologist
will question, because in almost every setting one might want to describe, a rate is not a constant
— resources run out, competitors adapt, and success carries its own cost.
   This chapter asks what happens when a rate is allowed to move. The models collected here
were built at different times and stand at different stages of completion, but they sort by one
structural feature: what moves the rate. Four mechanisms recur, and where a rate is driven by
the process’s own state, the behaviour turns on whether the feedback exhausts a quantity or
replenishes it.

 • Room that is spent and never returned. Speciation under logistic growth consumes
   remaining capacity K − N (t), the integrated rate converges, and the species count has a
   proper limit law (section 9.2).

 • A critical point dragged after the leader. Resistance in the Pimentel model acts not
   on a level but on the state of no preferred direction, and so keeps restoring criticality rather
   than approaching an equilibrium (section 9.3).

 • A potential restocked from outside. An accumulating potential, consumed per capita
   and replenished at a constant inflow, yields a cycle in four movements (section 9.4).

 • A potential the population makes for itself. Take that same hazard and let the inflow
   be produced by the population’s own area, with the cost charged per type rather than per
   head. The cycle becomes a marginal state; the catastrophe divides rather than destroys; and
   because its products obey the same law, the cycle branches instead of repeating (section 9.5).

   The last two are a matched pair, and the chapter is arranged so that they can be read as
one movement. They also mark where the mathematics stops. The first two sections carry
closed-form results; the second two carry simulations of models that outrun the machinery of
the preceding chapters, and say as exactly as they can what would have to be proved.
   Three results carry the finished mathematics, and each is stated here so that the derivations
may be read knowing where they go.

 • For a pure birth process from one founder with per-capita rateR β(t), the species count St
   is geometric on {1, 2, . . . } with parameter e−B(t) , where B(t) = 0t β(u) du (proposition 9.2).

                                                 228
   The rate law enters the distribution only through its integral.

• Under the logistic rate that integral converges, so the process has a proper limit law and
  not merely a finite late-time mean: S∞ is geometric with mean (K/N0 )σK/ϱ (corollary 9.3),
  depending on the parameters through exactly those two dimensionless groups.

• In the Pimentel competition model the state at which the chain is equally likely to step up
  as down is available in closed form ((9.21)), and it is unstable. Two trajectories started from
  the same state diverge exactly when they are near it.

   To these the automaton of section 9.5 adds an observation rather than a theorem, and it
is the one most worth taking further: a species is driven onto the locus Xi = φNi /c at which
production and dissipation cancel, and on that locus its catastrophe hazard is proportional to its
area — which is the chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe process’s own δXt . The chapter’s
least tractable model may therefore reduce to the chapter’s own machinery, and section 9.5.6
says what would have to be shown.
   The Yule process, its geometric marginal and the method of characteristics are the mathe-
matical introduction’s; the logistic speciation model itself, with its mean, is already a worked
example there. The birth–death–catastrophe process, the roots a and b and the burst-size law
are the chapter on the birth–death–catastrophe process’s and the chapter on its distribution
theory’s. None of that is re-derived here. What this chapter adds is the distribution under a
moving speciation rate, the moving critical point of the Pimentel chain, the automaton and its
balance point, and the remaining specifications, stated precisely enough to be taken up where
they stop.
